H^{1}(X;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})=\operatorname{Hom}_{cont}(G,\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
(with the compact-open topology; this is a locally compact Hausdorff abelian group);

3.

for
i\geq 2
, the condensed abelian group
H^{i}(X;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
identifies with
H^{i+1}(X;\mathbb{Z})
and is discrete.

Proof.

Claims 1 and 2 are immediate. For Claim 3, by Proposition
3.6
part 2 applied to
V=\mathbb{R}
with trivial
G
-action, we get in particular that
H^{i}(X;\mathbb{R})=0
for all
i\geq 2
. Thus 3 follows from the long exact sequence associated to
\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

We can also describe the cohomology of
X
with coefficients in any locally compact Hausdorff group
A
(with trivial action) in terms of the above answer for
A=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
. This is rather formal.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
X
be a condensed anima, let
A
be a locally compact Hausdorff abelian group, and let
A^{\vee}
denote its Pontryagin dual. The natural map

R\Gamma(X;A)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\underline{R\operatorname{Hom}}(A^{\vee},R\Gamma(X;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}))

in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{CondAb})
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

For formal reasons of adjunction we have

\underline{R\operatorname{Hom}}(A^{\vee},R\Gamma(X;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}))\simeq R\Gamma(X;\underline{R\operatorname{Hom}}(A^{\vee};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})).

The natural map in question arises from
A\to\underline{R\operatorname{Hom}}(A^{\vee};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
, and it is an isomorphism as a special case of derived Pontryagin duality,
2.9
.
∎

We will also want to study a generalization of this where the locally compact Hausdorff group
A
has nontrivial
G
-action. In this setting the usual trick of passing to the universal cover yields the following:

R\Gamma(X;A)\simeq R\operatorname{Hom}_{G}(A^{\vee};R\Gamma(\widetilde{X};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})),

where
\widetilde{X}=X\times_{BG}\ast
.Thus it suffices to understand the
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
-cohomology of
\widetilde{X}
as a
G
-object.

Our main case of interest is that where
A
is compact. Then there is a slight simplification, because the
G
-action on
A
necessarily factors through the quotient of
G
by its identity component
G^{\circ}
. This leads to the following.

Definition 3.9
.

For a condensed anima
X
equipped with a map
X\to BG
for some locally compact Hausdorff group
G
, define

X^{\circ}=X\times_{BG}BG^{\circ},

where
G^{\circ}
denotes the connected component of the identity in
G
.

We recall that
G^{\circ}
is a closed normal subgroup of
G
, and
G/G^{\circ}
is a totally disconnected locally compact Hausdorff group. Note that there is a natural action of
G/G^{\circ}
on
BG^{\circ}
(as an unpointed condensed anima), and

(BG^{\circ})/(G/G^{\circ})=BG.

This follows by descent, since
BG^{\circ}=\operatorname{fib}(BG\to B(G/G^{\circ}))
. For the same reason, with notation as above we have a natural
G/G^{\circ}
action on
X^{\circ}
and

X^{\circ}/(G/G^{\circ})=X.

In this way, the data of the map of condensed anima
X\to BG
is equivalent to the data of the map of
G/G^{\circ}
-equivariant condensed anima
X^{\circ}\to BG^{\circ}
.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
X
be a condensed anima equipped with a map
f:X\to BG
for some locally compact Hausdorff group
G
. Suppose given a compact abelian group
A
with
G
-action, which we can also view as an abelian group object over
BG
. Then:

1.

The
G
-action on
A
factors through to a
G/G^{\circ}
-action, thereby inducing a
G/G^{\circ}
-action on the (discrete) Pontryagin dual
A^{\vee}
as well;

2.

We have
R\Gamma(X;f^{\ast}A)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\operatorname{RHom}_{G/G^{\circ}}(A^{\vee},R\Gamma(X^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})).

Proof.

Part 1 follows from the fact that the automorphism group of a compact Hausdorff abelian group (with the compact-open topology) is totally disconnected. Part 2 then follows by descent from Lemma
3.8
.
∎

Using this, we have the following interpretation of the usual procedure of building anima step-by-step via their Postnikov tower.

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
G
be a locally compact Hausdorff group. For
n\geq 1
, let
\mathcal{X}_{n}(BG)
denote the
\infty
-groupoid of
n
-truncated condensed anima
X
equipped with an isomorphism
\tau_{\leq 1}X\simeq BG
such that
\pi_{i}X
is compact Hausdorff for all
i\geq 2
. Note that
\tau_{\leq n}
induces a natural map

\mathcal{X}_{n+1}(BG)\to\mathcal{X}_{n}(BG).

For
x\in\mathcal{X}_{n}(BG)
corresponding to
X\to BG
, the
\infty
-groupoid of lifts of
x
to
\mathcal{X}_{n+1}(BG)
identifies with the
\infty
-groupoid of pairs
(A,\kappa)
where
A
is a compact Hausdorff abelian group with continuous
G/G^{\circ}
-action and

\kappa:X^{\circ}\to K(A,n+2)

is a
G/G^{\circ}
-equivariant map of condensed anima, recalling that
X^{\circ}:=X\times_{BG}BG^{\circ}
. More precisely, this identification is induced by sending a pair
(A,\kappa)
as above to the condensed anima
Y=\operatorname{fib}(\kappa)/(G/G^{\circ})
, equipped with its tautological map to
X^{\circ}/(G/G^{\circ})=X
, which induces the required
\tau_{\leq n}Y\simeq X
.

Proof.

We build the map backwards using the Postnikov tower of
(Y\to BG)\in\mathcal{X}_{n+1}(BG)
in condensed anima over
BG
(see
[
40
]
6.5 and 7.2), or equivalently by descent, the Postnikov tower of
Y^{\circ}\to BG^{\circ}
in
G/G^{\circ}
-equivariant condensed anima over
BG^{\circ}
.

The
G/G^{\circ}
-equivariant map
Y^{\circ}\to\tau_{\leq n}Y^{\circ}=X^{\circ}
is an
n+1
-gerbe. Thus there is a unique
G/G^{\circ}
-equivariant abelian group object
\mathcal{A}
in (relatively
0
-truncated) condensed anima over
X^{\circ}
such that
Y^{\circ}\to X^{\circ}
promotes to an
n+1
-gerbe banded by
\mathcal{A}
. A relatively
0
-truncated anima over
X^{\circ}
uniquely descends to
\tau_{\leq 1}X^{\circ}=BG^{\circ}
, so we can equivalently treat
\mathcal{A}
as a
G/G^{\circ}
-equivariant abelian group object over
BG^{\circ}
. Next we claim it is uniquely pulled back from a
G/G^{\circ}
-equivariant abelian group object over
\ast
, i.e. a condensed abelian group
A
with
G/G^{\circ}
-action, and that this condensed abelian group
A
is compact Hausdorff.

For this, note that on pullback along any point
x
of
X
(unique up to homotopy, as
\pi_{0}X=\ast
and
\ast
is projective in condensed anima),
\mathcal{A}
gives
\pi_{n+1}(X,x)
, which is compact Hausdorff by assumption. A
G/G^{\circ}
-equivariant abelian group object over
\tau_{\leq 1}X=BG^{\circ}
is the same as an abelian group object over
(BG^{\circ})/(G/G^{\circ})\simeq BG
. Thus
\mathcal{A}
corresponds to a compact Hausdorff group
A
with continuous
G
-action. Since the automorphism group of a compact abelian Hausdorff group is totally disconnected, the action must be trivial on
G^{\circ}
. Therefore, the
G
-action descends (uniquely) to a
G/G^{\circ}
-action, and this proves the claim (that
\mathcal{A}
is uniquely pulled back from a
G/G^{\circ}
-equivariant compact Hausdorff abelian group
A
).

We deduce that
Y^{\circ}\to X^{\circ}
has the structure of an
n+1
-gerbe banded by this
A
. Thus by
[
40
]
Theorem 7.2.2.6 it is uniquely classified by a map

X^{\circ}\to K(A,n+2).

The universal
n+1
-gerbe banded by
A
corresponds to the canonical base-point of
K(A,n+2)
, so this translates to the desired statement.
∎

To finish this section, we discuss phenomena related to filtered inverse limits of these “almost compact” condensed anima, again starting with the compact case. For motivation, we recall the following well-known fact (for which we provide a proof since we couldn’t find one in the literature):

Lemma 3.12
.

The functor from the pro-category of compact Lie groups to the category of compact Hausdorff groups, given by sending a pro-system to its inverse limit, is an equivalence.

Proof.

Let
G
be a compact Hausdorff group. By the Peter-Weyl theorem,
L^{2}(G)
is the Hilbert space direct sum of finite-dimensional unitary representations of
G
. It follows that
G
maps by an injective continuous homomorphism to a product of unitary groups. Thus
G
is isomorphic to a closed subgroup of a product
\prod_{i\in I}H_{i}
of compact Lie groups. For a finite subset
I_{0}\subset I
, let
G_{I_{0}}
be the image of
G
under the projection
\prod_{i\in I}H_{i}\to\prod_{i\in I_{0}}H_{i}
. Then
G
is the filtered inverse limit of the
G_{I_{0}}
, and each
G_{I_{0}}
is a compact Lie group because a closed subgroup of a Lie group is a Lie group.

Thus the functor from the pro-category of compact Lie groups to compact Hausdorff groups is essentially surjective. To show fully faithfulness, we need to see that if
(G_{i})_{i\in I}
is a pro-system of compact Lie groups (indexed by a filtered poset
I
) with inverse limit
G
, then for any compact Lie group
H
we have

\varinjlim_{i\in I}\operatorname{Hom}_{cont}(G_{i},H)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\operatorname{Hom}_{cont}(G,H).

In fact, we will see that this holds for any Lie group
H
. Let
\mathcal{N}
denote the poset of closed normal subgroups
N
of
G
for which
G/N
is a Lie group. Note that
\mathcal{N}
is closed under finite intersection (as
G/(N\cap N^{\prime})\hookrightarrow G/N\times G/N^{\prime}
and a closed subgroup of a Lie group is a Lie group), so that
N\mapsto G/N
gives a pro-system. Then we claim that
(G_{i})_{i\in I}
is pro-isomorphic to
(G/N)_{N\in\mathcal{N}}
. Admitting this for now, to complete the proof it then suffices to show that

\varinjlim_{N\in\mathcal{N}}\operatorname{Hom}_{cont}(G/N,H)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\operatorname{Hom}_{cont}(G,H).

Now the map is clearly injective. To show surjectivity, let
f:G\to H
be a continuous homomorphism. Then
N=\operatorname{ker}(f)
is a closed normal subgroup such that
G/N\simeq\operatorname{im}(f)
is a closed subgroup of
H
, hence a Lie group, and moreover
f
factors through
G/N
, as required.

To prove the claim that
(G_{i})_{i\in I}
is pro-isomorphic to
(G/N)_{N\in\mathcal{N}}
, it suffices to combine the following two facts:

1.

The map
I\to\mathcal{N}
sending
i\in I
to
N_{i}:=\operatorname{ker}(G\to G_{i})
is cofinal (so that the pro-system
(G/N_{i})_{i\in I}
maps isomorphically to the pro-system
(G/N)_{N\in\mathcal{N}}
);

2.

The map of inverse systems
(G/N_{i})_{i\in I}\to(G_{i})_{i\in I}
induced by the evident termwise inclusions is a pro-isomorphism.

For 1, we need to see that for all
N\in\mathcal{N}
, there is an
i\in I
with
N_{i}\subset N
. More generally we claim this holds for any filtered system
(N_{i})_{i\in I}
of closed normal subgroups of a compact Hausdorff group
G
such that
\cap_{i}N_{i}=\{1\}
. First suppose
G
is a Lie group and
N=\{1\}
. We need to show
N_{i}=\{1\}
for some
i
. Note that
(\operatorname{Lie}(N_{i}))_{i\in I}
gives a filtered system of subspaces of the finite-dimensional
\operatorname{Lie}(G)
, hence it must stabilize, say at
i_{0}\in I
, so
\operatorname{Lie}(N_{i})=\operatorname{Lie}(N_{i_{0}})
for all
i\geq i_{0}
. As
N_{i}
is a closed subgroup of
N_{i_{0}}
and these are Lie groups, this implies that
(N_{i})^{\circ}=(N_{i_{0}})^{\circ}
for all
i\geq i_{0}
. Then again the
\pi_{0}(N_{i})
, being finite, must also stabilize after some point, and we deduce that the
N_{i}
themselves stabilize after some point. Since
\{1\}=\cap_{i}N_{i}
, we find that
N_{i}=\{1\}
after the stabilization point, as desired. Returning to the general case, let
N^{\prime}_{i}
be the image of
N_{i}
in
G/N
. A filtered inverse limit of surjective maps of compact Hausdorff spaces is surjective, so
\cap_{i}N_{i}\twoheadrightarrow\cap_{i}N^{\prime}_{i}
, hence
\cap_{i}N^{\prime}_{i}=\{1\}
and we conclude from the Lie case that
N^{\prime}_{i}=\{1\}
for some
i
, which says
N_{i}\subset N
for some
i
, as required for 1.

For 2, we need to show that for all
i_{0}\in I
, there is an
i\geq i_{0}
such that
G_{i}\to G_{i_{0}}
lands inside
G/N_{i_{0}}=\operatorname{im}(G\to G_{i_{0}})
. Let
H_{i}=\operatorname{im}(G_{i}\to G_{i_{0}})
. As in the proof of 1 above we see that the
H_{i}
stabilize and that
\operatorname{im}(G\to G_{i_{0}})=\cap_{i}H_{i}
. The claim follows.
∎

We would like to extend this to “compact anima” with trivial
\pi_{0}
.

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
(X_{i})_{i\in I}
be a filtered inverse system of condensed anima, each of which satisfies
\pi_{0}(X_{i})=\ast
and
\pi_{n}(X_{i})\in\operatorname{CHaus}
for all
n\geq 1
. Then:

1.

X=\varprojlim_{i}X_{i}
satisfies the same conditions (
\pi_{0}X=\ast
,
\pi_{n}X\in\operatorname{CHaus}
).

2.

We have
\pi_{n}X\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\varprojlim_{i}\pi_{n}(X_{i})
for all
n\geq 0
(all basepoints of
X
are homotopic by 1).

3.

Suppose
Y
is a condensed anima such that
\pi_{n}(Y,y)
is a Lie group for all
n\geq 1
and all
y:\ast\to Y
, and such that
Y
is
d
-truncated for some
d<\infty
. Then

\varinjlim_{i}\operatorname{Map}(X_{i},Y)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\operatorname{Map}(X,Y).

Proof.

Parts 1 and 2 follow from induction on the Postnikov tower using Lemmas
2.13
and
2.14
. For 3, by 1 we have that maps to a
0
-truncated
Y
are the same for
X
and any
X_{i}
, and are the same as maps from a point. Thus pulling back along any
y:\ast\to Y
we can assume
\pi_{0}Y=\ast
. Again by induction on the Postnikov tower of
Y
we reduce to the case
Y=BH
for a Lie group
H
plus the claim that cohomology with abelian Lie group coefficients on
X
is the colimit of cohomology on the
X_{i}
(for a coefficient system coming from some
X_{i_{0}}
). The case
Y=BH
follows from the classical remarks about inverse limits of compact Lie groups made above. For the claim about cohomology, suppose
N
is an abelian Lie group with
\pi_{1}X_{i_{0}}
-action for some
i_{0}\in I
; we want that

\varinjlim_{i>i_{0}}H^{n}(X_{i};N)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}H^{n}(X;N)

for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
We have the natural short exact sequence

0\to N^{\circ}\to N\to N/N^{\circ}\to 0

which means we can reduce to two separate cases: the discrete case and the connected case.

First suppose
N
is discrete. Arguing using bar resolutions and the Postnikov tower of the
X_{i}
as in the proof of Lemma
3.5
we reduce to showing that
N
-cohomology on compact Hausdorff spaces sends inverse limits to filtered colimits; but this follows by comparison with sheaf cohomology,
2.2
.

Now suppose
N
is connected. Then we have a natural short exact sequence

0\to\Lambda_{N}\to\operatorname{Lie}(N)\to N\to 0

with
\Lambda_{N}
discrete, so we reduce to the case where
N
is a finite dimensional
\mathbb{R}
-vector space. But in that case by Lemma
3.5
we reduce to the claim that

N^{\pi_{1}X}=\cup_{i}N^{\pi_{1}X_{i}},

which again follows from the facts recalled above about inverse limits of compact Hausdorff groups.
∎

Theorem 3.14
.

Let
\operatorname{CLieAn}_{<\infty}
denote the full subcategory of
\operatorname{CondAn}
spanned by the
Y
such that:

1.

\pi_{0}Y=\ast
;

2.

\pi_{n}Y
is a compact Lie group for all
n\geq 1
;

3.

Y
is
d
-truncated for some
d<\infty
.

Then the functor

\operatorname{Pro}(\operatorname{CLieAn}_{<\infty})\to\operatorname{CondAn}

sending a pro-object in
\operatorname{CLieAn}_{<\infty}
to its inverse limit in
\operatorname{CondAn}
is fully faithful, and the essential image consists of those condensed anima
X
such that
\pi_{0}X=\ast
and
\pi_{n}X
is compact Hausdorff for all
n\geq 1
.

Proof.

Fully faithfulness is immediate from Lemma
3.13
, as is the fact that every object
X
in the essential image satisfies the stated properties. Thus we need to show that any
X
satisfying the stated properties lies in the essential image. The essential image is closed under filtered inverse limits by the fully faithfulness, so we can reduce to the case of
d
-truncated
X
, which in turn we will handle by induction. When
d=1
this follows from the fact (recalled above) that every compact Hausdorff group is a filtered inverse limit of compact Lie groups. Assume now
X
is
d+1
-truncated and that
\tau_{\leq d}X=\varprojlim_{i}C_{i}
with
C_{i}\in\operatorname{CLieAn}_{<\infty}
. By Lemma
3.13
we can assume each
C_{i}
is
d
-truncated, and if
G
denotes the fundamental group of
X
and
G_{i}
for
i\in I
denotes the fundamental group of
X_{i}
, then

G=\varprojlim_{i}G_{i}.

Now, let

(\tau_{\leq d}X)^{\circ}\to K(A,n+2)

denote the k-invariant for
X
, from Lemma
3.11
, so this is a
G/G^{\circ}
-equivariant map with
A
a compact Hausdorff group with
G/G^{\circ}
-action. The Pontryagin dual
A^{\vee}
is a discrete abelian group with
G/G^{\circ}
-action, hence it can be written as a filtered union

A^{\vee}=\cup_{j}D_{j}

where each
D_{j}
is a finitely generated abelian group with continuous
G/G^{\circ}
-action that factors through to a
G_{i}/G_{i}^{\circ}
-action for some
i
(depending on
j
). Applying Pontryagin duality, we get that

A=\varprojlim_{j}A_{j}

where
A_{j}
is a compact abelian Lie group with
G_{i}/G_{i}^{\circ}
-action. For each
j
, the composition

(\tau_{\leq d}X)^{\circ}\to K(A,n+2)\to K(A_{j},n+2),

which is equivalently an
n+2
-cocycle on
X
for the twisted coefficient system determined by
A_{j}
, factors through a
G_{i^{\prime}}/G_{i^{\prime}}^{\circ}
-equivariant map
C_{i^{\prime}}^{\circ}\to K(A_{j},n+2)
for some
i^{\prime}>i
by Lemma
3.13
. Varying over
j
, we deduce that
X
is an inverse limit over extensions of
C_{i^{\prime}}
by
K(A_{j},n+1)
, finishing the proof.
∎

One advantage of reducing to this sort of “finite type” situation is that we get a clearer handle on discrete coefficient systems.

Lemma 3.15
.

Suppose
C\in\operatorname{CondAn}
is such that
\pi_{0}C=\ast
and
\pi_{n}C
is a Lie group for all
n\geq 1
. Then there is an initial anima
|C|
equipped with a map

C\to|C|.

It is uniquely characterized by the following properties:

1.

\pi_{0}|C|=\ast
;

2.

\pi_{1}C\to\pi_{1}|C|
identifies
\pi_{1}|C|
with the component group of the Lie group
\pi_{1}C
;

3.

for all discrete abelian groups
M
with
\pi_{1}|C|
-action, the map

H^{i}(|C|;M)\to H^{i}(C;M)

is an isomorphism for all
i\geq 0
.

We moreover have that discrete coefficient systems over
C
(that is, discrete anima with an action of the condensed
\Omega C
) are the same as over
|C|
.

Proof.

It is clear from a Postnikov tower argument that any
C\to|C|
satisfying 1-3 will be the initial anima with a map from
C
. Thus it suffices to show existence of
C\to|C|
satisfying 1,2,3 and the “moreover”.

Let us show that any such
C
lifts (uniquely!) to the
\infty
-topos of sheaves on real topological manifolds with respect to the open cover topology. This easily implies the desired claim using that manifolds are locally contractible, c.f.
[
14
]
Section 5. For the lifting claim, working inductively on the Postnikov tower, it suffices to show that cohomology with abelian Lie group coefficient systems is the same for a sheaf on manifolds as for its underlying condensed anima. But this is follows from Lemma
2.2
(and its more obvious analog for sheaves on manifolds), using the usual devissage to reduce to discrete coefficients and real vector space coefficients.∎

Remark 3.16
.

Combining, we deduce that if
X
is a condensed anima with
\pi_{0}X=\ast
and
\pi_{n}X
compact Hausdorff for
n\geq 1
, then we get a pro-system of anima
(A_{i})_{i\in I}
with a map

X\to\varprojlim_{i}A_{i}

which is pro-universal with respect to maps to discrete anima; moreover we can choose the
A_{i}
such that each
\pi_{1}A_{i}
is a quotient of
\pi_{1}X
by an open normal subgroup (in particular,
\pi_{1}A_{i}
is finite) and each higher
\pi_{n}A_{i}
is a finitely generated abelian group. The same conclusion applies if
\pi_{1}X
is only almost compact with quotient
\mathbb{R}
, as the extra
\mathbb{R}
does not affect maps to anima, c.f. the proof of Lemma
3.6
.

Namely, we can take
A_{i}=|C_{i}|
where
X=\varprojlim_{i}C_{i}
with
C_{i}\in\operatorname{CLieAn}_{<\infty}
as in Theorem
3.14
.

Remark 3.17
.

If
X,Y
both satisfy the hypotheses of Lemma
3.15
(
\pi_{0}=\ast
and
\pi_{n}
is Lie for
n\geq 1
), then so does
X\times Y
, and

|X\times Y|\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}|X|\times|Y|.

This follows easily from the proof of Lemma
3.15
, compare with the discussion in
[
14
]
Section 5.

It follows from this that if
G
is a group object satisfying the hypothesis in Lemma
3.15
acting on
X
, then

|X/G|=|X|/|G|.

Fixing basepoint of
X
and let
G=\pi_{1}X
, we deduce

|X|=|\tau_{\geq 2}X|/|G|,

so that applying
|\cdot|
to the first stage of the Postnikov tower of
X
still yields a fiber sequence

|\tau_{\geq 2}X|\to|X|\to|BG|.

This reduces the study of
|X|
to that of
|BG|
and that of
X
with
\pi_{1}X=\{1\}
.

This
|BG|
is just the classical classifying space of
G
from algebraic topology. As for
|X|
when
X
satisfies
\pi_{1}X=\{1\}
, it can be understood in terms of the Postnikov tower, based on the following: if
A
is an abelian Lie group, then there is a canonical exact sequence

0\to\Lambda_{A}\to\operatorname{Lie}(A)\to A\to A/A^{\circ}\to 0

with
\Lambda_{A}
a free
\mathbb{Z}
-module of finite rank,
A/A^{\circ}
a discrete abelian group, and
\operatorname{Lie}(A)
a finite-dimensional
\mathbb{R}
-vector space. Moreover

0\to\operatorname{Lie}(A)/\Lambda_{A}\to A\to A/A^{\circ}\to 0

is (noncanonically) split. Comparing cohomology using Serre spectral sequences, it follows that we have a canonical fiber sequence

K(\Lambda_{A},n+1)\to|K(A,n)|\to K(A/A^{\circ},n)

and that noncanonically
|K(A,n)|\simeq K(\Lambda_{A},n+1)\times K(A/A^{\circ},n)
. Returning to general
X
, using the Postnikov tower (which still yields fiber sequences on
|\cdot|
by a similar argument to the above) we deduce natural short exact sequences

0\to\pi_{n+1}X/(\pi_{n+1}X)^{\circ}\to\pi_{n+1}|X|\to\Lambda_{\pi_{n}X}\to 0

for
n\geq 1
which are noncanonically split.

Continuing the theme, we can furthermore pass to the profinite completion.

Proposition 3.18
.

Let
X\in\operatorname{CondAn}
with
\pi_{0}X=\ast
(for simplicity). Then
X
admits a profinite completion, namely a map

X\to\widehat{X}

in
\operatorname{CondAn}
such that:

1.

\pi_{0}\widehat{X}=\ast
, and
\pi_{n}\widehat{X}
is a profinite group for all
n\geq 1
;

2.

If
Y\in\operatorname{CondAn}
is such that
\pi_{0}Y=\ast
and
\pi_{n}Y
is a profinite group for all
n\geq 1
, then

\operatorname{Map}(\widehat{X},Y)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\operatorname{Map}(X,Y).

Proof.

The
\infty
-category of all connected
\pi
-finite anima
A
equipped with a map
X\to A
is essentially small and co-filtered (via pullbacks), and we define
\widehat{X}
to be the resulting filtered inverse limit

\widehat{X}:=\varprojlim_{X\to A}A.

By Lemma
3.13
, if
B
is any connected
\pi
-finite anima we have

\varinjlim_{X\to A}\operatorname{Map}(A,B)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\operatorname{Map}(\widehat{X},B).

On the other hand the composition

\varinjlim_{X\to A}\operatorname{Map}(A,B)\to\operatorname{Map}(X,B)

is also an isomorphism, as an instance of the following general categorical fact: if
\mathcal{C}
is an
\infty
-category and
F:\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{An}
is a functor, then for all
B\in\mathcal{C}
the natural map

\varinjlim_{A\in\mathcal{C},\xi\in F(A)}\operatorname{Map}(A,B)\to F(B)

is an isomorphism. (Writing
F
as a colimit of functors of the form
\operatorname{Map}(A_{0},-)
, we reduce to that case, when the indexing category for the colimit has terminal object given by
A=A_{0}
and
\xi=\operatorname{id}
.) Combining, we deduce that

\operatorname{Map}(\widehat{X},B)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\operatorname{Map}(X,B)

for any connected
\pi
-finite anima
B
. The same necessarily holds when
B
is any inverse limit of connected
\pi
-finite anima. But the proof of Theorem
3.14
shows that any
Y
as in 2 is an inverse limit of connected
\pi
-finite anima. Thus we deduce that 2 holds for
X\to\widehat{X}
. By Lemma
3.13
we also have that
\widehat{X}
satisfies 1, so we conclude the proof.
∎

In particular, if
X
satisfies
\pi_{0}X=\ast
and
\pi_{n}X\in\operatorname{CHaus}
for
n\geq 1
, then if we write

X=\varprojlim_{i}C_{i}

with
C_{i}\in\operatorname{CLieAn}_{<\infty}
(Theorem
3.14
), then

\widehat{X}=\varprojlim_{i}\widehat{|C_{i}|}

where
\widehat{|C_{i}|}
is the profinite completion of the usual anima
|C_{i}|
. Since
\pi_{1}|C_{i}|
is finite and
\pi_{n}|C_{i}|
is finitely generated for all
i\geq 2
, we have that
\pi_{1}\widehat{|C_{i}|}=\pi_{1}|C_{i}|
and

\pi_{n}\widehat{|C_{i}|}=(\pi_{n}|C_{i}|)\otimes\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}

for
n\geq 2
.

We can view an object
C\in\operatorname{CondAn}
with
\pi_{0}C=\ast
and
\pi_{n}C
a compact Lie group for all
n\geq 1
as some kind of differential refinement of its homotopy type
|C|
and ask, what kinds of more standard geometric structures on a connected topological space will give rise to such a refined homotopy type?

More precisely, we recall (c.f.
[
52
]
Lemma 11.9) that if
X
is, e.g. a topological space homotopy equivalent to a CW-complex, then its associated condensed set
\underline{X}
admits an initial map to an anima
|X|
, and this anima is the usual homotopy type of
X
. One can ask what structure on a connected
X
will naturally produce a
C
as above and a map

\underline{X}\to C

which induces an isomorphism on
|\cdot|
.

Example 3.19
.

Let us consider the simplest nontrivial example, that of an
n
-sphere
S^{n}
with
n
odd. The homotopy groups of
S^{n}
are finite except in degree
n
where
\pi_{n}S^{n}\simeq\mathbb{Z}
. Our desired
C
should therefore have the same homotopy groups as
S^{n}
except in degrees
n-1
and
n
, where we should have
\pi_{n}C=0
and
\pi_{n-1}C=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
.

I claim we can build a canonical such
C
plus a map
S^{n}\to C
as above given any choice of smooth structure and Riemannian metric on
S^{n}
(or really, just the associated volume form). Let
\operatorname{or}
denote the orientation local system on
S^{n}
(it is trivial, but never mind that). As is well-known, the Riemannian metric gives rise to a canonical volume form, which is a global section of the sheaf

\Omega^{n}\otimes\operatorname{or}

on
S^{n}
. This sheaf canonically maps to the shifted twisted de Rham complex
\Omega^{\bullet}\otimes\operatorname{or}[n]
, and even to

\operatorname{Fib}(\Omega^{\bullet}\otimes\operatorname{or}[n]\to\Omega^{0}\otimes\operatorname{or}[n]).

Up to quasi-isomorphism, the source is
\mathbb{R}^{\delta}\otimes\operatorname{or}[n]
; on the other hand the target maps to
\mathbb{R}\otimes\operatorname{or}[n]
because smooth functions yield continuous functions.

Thus we have a section of the family of Eilenberg-Maclane spaces
K(\mathbb{R}^{\delta},n)
over
S^{n}
twisted by the orientation sheaf, together with a nullhomotopy of the induced section of
K(\mathbb{R},n)
. This gives rise to the desired data. Indeed, if we forget the twists for simplicity, then we get

S^{n}\to K(\mathbb{R}^{\delta},n)\to K(\mathbb{R},n)

with null composition; the first map uniquely factors through
|S^{n}|
and we set

C=\operatorname{fib}(|S^{n}|\to K(\mathbb{R},n)).

If
G
is a compact Lie group acting freely on
S^{n}
by isometries (or, just preserving the volume form), then since the above was canonical we get an induced

S^{n}/G\to C/G

which provides a solution to our problem for
X=S^{n}/G
as well. For example, when
n=3
we can take

G=\operatorname{Pin}^{-}(2)=U(1)\cup j\cdot U(1)\subset\mathbb{H}^{\times}

(which is the norm-1 subgroup of
W_{\mathbb{R}}
) acting by left multiplication on the unit quaterions
S^{3}
with their canonical invariant metric and in this way we get a canonical “compact homotopy type” for
X=\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}
.

4
Moore anima

We set ourselves the following problem: for an almost compact group
G
(Definition
3.1
), we want to modify the condensed anima
BG
by adding higher homotopy in order to simplify the cohomology. More precisely, we make the following definition.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
G
be a locally compact Hausdorff group. A
Moore anima
for
G
is a condensed anima
X
equipped with a map
t:X\to BG
satisfying the following properties:

1.

The map
t
induces an isomorphism
\tau_{\leq 1}X\simeq BG
.

2.

For all
i\geq 2
, the homotopy (condensed) group
\pi_{i}X
is compact Hausdorff.

3.

We have

H^{i}(X^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})=0

as a condensed abelian group for all
i\geq 2
, where (see Definition
3.9
)
X^{\circ}=X\times_{BG}BG^{\circ}
.

Remark 4.2
.

Our main case of interest is when
G
is almost compact; then the condensed abelian group
H^{i}(X^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
is discrete for
i\geq 2
by Corollary
3.7
, so in 3 above, vanishing in the condensed sense and vanishing in the ordinary sense are equivalent. This discreteness is also what motivates the requirement that the
\pi_{i}X
be compact Hausdorff, as we will eventually see that the homotopy groups are determined as Pontryagin dual to such cohomology groups, e.g.

\pi_{2}X=H^{3}(BG^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})^{\vee}.

Example 4.3
.

Suppose
G
is totally disconnected. Then
BG^{\circ}=\ast
, and it follows that
BG
itself (or, more precisely, the identity map
BG\to BG
) is already a Moore anima for
G
in this sense. In fact the
\infty
-groupoid of Moore anima for
G
is contractible, as we will see from the general analysis. Thus the theory in this section is trivial in the totally disconnected case. Later we will formulate a stronger Moore-like property for which the theory is interesting even when
G
is totally disconnected.

The method for producing Moore anima (or more precisely, for analyzing the
\infty
-groupoid of Moore anima) will be via the familiar procedure of
obstruction theory
from homotopy theory, based on the Postnikov tower. In this we follow
[
62
]
on equivariant Moore spaces, but transported to the condensed context.

We start with a truncated variant of Definition
4.1
.

Definition 4.4
.

Let
G
be a locally compact Hausdorff group and
n\geq 1
. An
n
-stage Moore anima
for
G
is a condensed anima
X
equipped with a map
t:X\to BG
satisfying the following properties:

1.

X
is
n
-truncated:
X\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\tau_{\leq n}X
.

2.

The map
t
induces an isomorphism
\tau_{\leq 1}X\simeq BG
.

3.

For all
i\geq 2
, the homotopy (condensed) group
\pi_{i}X
is compact Hausdorff.

4.

Letting
X^{\circ}=X\times_{BG}BG^{\circ}
, we have
H^{i}(X^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})=0
when
n+1\geq i>1
.

Formally, we can allow
n=\infty
: an
\infty
-stage Moore anima for
G
is just a Moore anima for
G
in the sense of Definition
4.1
.

Remark 4.5
.

Naively, it would seem more natural to take the weaker version of 4 where you only ask cohomology vanishing up to degree
n
, not
n+1
. But the above form of the definition makes the obstruction theory much simpler.

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
G
be a locally compact Hausdorff group.

1.

Let
1\leq n<\infty
, and let
Y\to BG
be an
n+1
-stage Moore anima for
G
. Then
X:=\tau_{\leq n}Y\to BG
is an
n
-stage Moore anima for
G
.

2.

Let
t:X\to BG
be a map of condensed anima. Then
t
is a Moore anima for
G
if and only if for all
1\leq n<\infty
, the induced map
\tau_{\leq n}X\to BG
is an
n
-stage Moore anima for G.

Proof.

For 1, we clearly have that
X
is
n
-truncated, that
\tau_{\leq 1}X\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}BG
, and that
\pi_{i}X
is compact Hausdorff for all
i\geq 2
. Note that, since
BG
and
BG^{\circ}
are 1-truncated, we have

\tau_{\leq n}(Y^{\circ})=X^{\circ}.

Since
K(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z},i)
is
i
-truncated, it follows immediately from the corresponding fact for
Y^{\circ}
that
H^{i}(X^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})=0
for
n\geq i>1
. The only nontrivial point is to show that
H^{n+1}(X^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})=0
. For this, note by Lemma
3.11
that there is a fiber sequence

Y^{\circ}\to X^{\circ}\to K(A,n+2)

where
A
is a compact Hausdorff abelian group (isomorphic to
\pi_{n+1}Y^{\circ}
). Consider the associated Serre spectral sequence for cohomology with
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
-coefficients. The terms on the
E_{2}
-page which contribute to
H^{n+1}(X^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
are the

H^{i}(K(A,n+2);H^{j}(Y^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}))

for
i+j=n+1
. Since
\tau_{\leq n+1}K(A,n+2)=\ast
, the degree-
i
cohomology of
K(A,n+2)
with any coefficients vanishes for
0<i\leq n+1
, so the only possibly nonzero term is
H^{0}(K(A,n+2);H^{n+1}(Y^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}))
. But this is also
0
because
H^{n+1}(Y^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})=0
by the hypothesis on
Y
.

For 2, the implication that
X\to BG
is a Moore anima
\Rightarrow

\tau_{\leq n}X\to BG
is an
n
-stage Moore anima follows exactly as in the proof of 1 above, and the converse follows from the fact that
H^{i}(X^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})=H^{i}(\tau_{\leq n}X^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
for large enough
n
(namely
n\geq i
).
∎

We can reinterpret this in light of the following definition.

Definition 4.7
.

Let
G
be a locally compact Hausdorff group. Let
\mathcal{M}(BG)
denote the
\infty
-groupoid (anima) of Moore anima for
G
, and for
n\geq 1
, let
\mathcal{M}_{n}(BG)
denote the
\infty
-groupoid of
n
-stage Moore anima for
G
.

Corollary 4.8
.

Let
G
be a locally compact Hausdorff group. Then we have

\mathcal{M}(BG)=\varprojlim_{n}\mathcal{M}_{n}(BG),

where the implicit maps are induced by Postnikov truncation.

Proof.

The maps are well-defined by Proposition
4.6
part 1 and the forward direction of Proposition
4.6
part 2. That we get an isomorphism results from the Postnikov completeness of condensed anima and the backward direction of Proposition
4.6
part 2.
∎

This reveals the strategy for analyzing
\mathcal{M}(BG)
: first study
\mathcal{M}_{1}(BG)
, then study the homotopy fibers of each map
\mathcal{M}_{n+1}(BG)\to\mathcal{M}_{n}(BG)
.

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
G
be a locally compact Hausdorff group. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

1.

\mathcal{M}_{1}(BG)\neq\emptyset
;

2.

\mathcal{M}_{1}(BG)\simeq\ast
;

3.

\operatorname{id}:BG\to BG
is a 1-stage Moore anima for
G
;

4.

H^{2}(BG^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})=0
.

If
G
is almost compact, these conditions are furthermore equivalent to the following:
\pi_{1}(K^{\circ})_{tors}=0
, where
K^{\circ}\subset G^{\circ}
denotes the maximal compact subgroup of
G^{\circ}
, and see Lemma
4.10
for the meaning of
\pi_{1}(K^{\circ})_{tors}
.

Proof.

Axioms 1 and 2 in Definition
4.4
imply that if
t:X\to BG
is a 1-stage Moore anima for
G
, then
t
is an isomorphism. This uniquely determines
t
and proves that 1, 2 and 3 are equivalent. To see that 3 is equivalent to 4, note that the only nontrivial condition is axiom 4 in Definition
4.4
, which for
t=\operatorname{id}:BG\to BG
exactly says 4. Finally, suppose
G
is almost compact. Then so is
G^{\circ}
is almost compact (being a closed subgroup of
G
, Lemma
3.4
). But it is also connected, hence either
K^{\circ}=G^{\circ}
or
G^{\circ}=\mathbb{R}\times K^{\circ}
by Lemma
3.3
, and in either case we have

H^{2}(BG^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})=H^{3}(BG^{\circ};\mathbb{Z})=H^{3}(BK^{\circ};\mathbb{Z}),

and
K^{\circ}
is connected. Thus the conclusion follows from Lemma
4.10
.
∎

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
K
be a connected compact Hausdorff group. Define a profinite abelian group
\pi_{1}(K)_{tors}
as follows: Let
N
run over all closed normal subgroups of
K
such that
K/N
is a (connected compact) Lie group (by the Peter-Weyl theorem and the fact that a closed subgroup of a Lie group is Lie, this collection filters down to
\{1\}
), and set

\pi_{1}(K)_{tors}=\varprojlim_{N}\pi_{1}(K/N)_{tors},

where
\pi_{1}(K/N)
stands for the usual fundamental group of a compact connected Lie group (or equivalently the quotient of the co-weight lattice by the co-root lattice).

Then:

1.

We have a canonical functorial isomorphism

H^{3}(BK;\mathbb{Z})\simeq(\pi_{1}(K)_{tors})^{\vee},

where
(-)^{\vee}
means Pontryagin duality.

2.

H^{4}(BK;\mathbb{Z})
is torsionfree.

Proof.

Recall that every compact Hausdorff group
K
is an filtered inverse limit
\varprojlim_{i}K_{i}
of compact Lie groups, unique up to pro-isomorphism. If
K
is connected, the replacing each
K_{i}
by the image of
K\to K_{i}
we can arrange that each
K_{i}
is connected, showing 1.

Claim 2 is a corollary of claim 1. For claims 3 and 4, invoking Lemma
3.13
and using Claim 1, we reduce to showing that if
K
is a connected compact Lie group, then there is a natural isomorphism
H^{3}(BK;\mathbb{Z})\simeq(\pi_{1}(K)_{tors})^{\vee}

and that

H^{4}(BK;\mathbb{Z})

is torsionfree.

We recall that the integral homology of
BK
in low degres is as follows:

H_{0}(BK)=\mathbb{Z},H_{1}(BK)=0,H_{2}(BK)=\pi_{1}K,H_{3}(BK)=0.

Indeed, up to degree 2 this follows from Hurewicz, and to get the answer in degree 3 one can use the Serre spectral sequence for

K/T\to BT\to BK

where
T\subset K
is a maximal torus, plus the fact that the homology of
K/T
is torsionfree and lives in even degrees (e.g. by the Bruhat decomposition). From the universal coefficient theorem we deduce that
H^{3}(BK;\mathbb{Z})=\operatorname{Ext}(\pi_{1}K,\mathbb{Z})=((\pi_{1}K)_{tors})^{\vee}
, giving claim 4, and also that
H^{4}(BK;\mathbb{Z})=\operatorname{Hom}(H_{4}(BK);\mathbb{Z})
, giving claim 5.
∎

In particular, we deduce the following, which suffices for the application to Weil groups. We remark that the hypothetical Langlands group from
[
2
]
will also satisfy that
\pi_{1}(K^{\circ})_{tors}=0
, so the same conclusion would apply to them as well.

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
G
be an almost compact group, and suppose that
G^{\circ}
is abelian. Then
\mathcal{M}_{1}(BG)=\ast
.

Proof.

By the above, it suffices to show that if
K
is a connected compact abelian group, then
\pi_{1}(K)_{tors}=0
. But such a
K
is Pontryagin dual to a torsionfree discrete group, hence
K
is a filtered inverse limit of finite products of copies of
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
, giving the claim.
∎

Having analyzed
\mathcal{M}_{1}(BG)
, we now turn to the homotopy fibers of

\mathcal{M}_{n+1}(BG)\to\mathcal{M}_{n}(BG)

for
n\geq 1
. Recall the anima
\mathcal{X}_{n}(BG)
from Lemma
3.11
: they classify the
n
-truncated condensed anima
X\to BG
such that
\tau_{\leq 1}X\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}BG
and
\pi_{i}X
is compact Hausdorff for
i\geq 2
. By definition,
\mathcal{M}_{n}(BG)
is the full subanima of
\mathcal{X}_{n}(BG)
spanned by those
X\to BG
such that
H^{i}(X^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})=0
for
2\leq i\leq n+1
.

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
G
be a locally compact Hausdorff group, and let
n\geq 1
. Suppose given
t\in\mathcal{M}_{n}(BG)
, corresponding to an
n
-stage Moore anima
t:X\to BG
. Let
X^{\circ}=X\times_{BG}BG^{\circ}
. Then the anima of lifts of
t
to
\mathcal{M}_{n+1}(BG)
identifies with the anima of pairs
(A,\kappa)
where
A
is a compact Hausdorff group with continuous
G/G^{\circ}
-action and
\kappa
is a
G/G^{\circ}
-equiviariant map of condensed anima

\kappa:X^{\circ}\to K(A,n+2)

such that the following condition is satisfied: the natural homomorphism

A^{\vee}\simeq H^{n+2}(K(A,n+2);\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})\overset{\kappa^{\ast}}{\longrightarrow}H^{n+2}(X^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})

is an isomorphism (of condensed abelian groups).

More precisely, this identification sends
(A,\kappa)
to
\operatorname{fib}(\kappa)/(G/G^{\circ})
equipped with its natural identification
\tau_{\leq n}\operatorname{fib}(\kappa)/(G/G^{\circ})\simeq X^{\circ}/(G/G^{\circ})=X
over
BG
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.11
, the anima of lifts of
t
to
\mathcal{X}_{n+1}(BG)
identifies with the anima of pairs
(A,\kappa)
without the condition on
\kappa^{\ast}
. Thus it suffices to show that for such
(A,\kappa)
, the above
\kappa^{\ast}
is an isomorphism if and only if
H^{i}(Y^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})=0
for all
2\leq i\leq n+2
, where
Y^{\circ}=\operatorname{fib}(\kappa)
.

For this, consider the Serre spectral sequence for cohomology with
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
-coefficients associated to the fiber sequence

K(A,n+1)\to Y^{\circ}\to X^{\circ}.

With standard indexing, the
E_{2}
-page is made up of the condensed abelian groups

E_{2}^{i,j}=H^{i}(X^{\circ};H^{j}(K(A,n+1);\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})),

with this
(i,j)
-term contributing to
H^{i+j}(Y^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
at the end of the spectral sequence.

When
j=0
, we have
H^{0}(K(A,n+1);\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
, so by assumption on
X
we have
E_{2}^{i,0}=0
for
2\leq i\leq n+1
. On the other hand for
0<j<n+1
we have
E_{2}^{i,j}=0
for all
i
because
\tau_{\leq n}K(A,n+1)=\ast
. In the edge case
j=n+1
, we have

E_{2}^{0,n+1}=H^{n+1}(K(A,n+1);\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})=A^{\vee}

and

E_{2}^{1,n+1}=H^{1}(X^{\circ};A^{\vee})=\operatorname{Hom}(G^{\circ},A^{\vee})=0

because
G^{\circ}
is connected and
A^{\vee}
is discrete.

It follows that:

1.

for
2\leq p<n+1
, all terms contributing to
H^{p}(Y^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
are
0
;

2.

the only possibly nonzero term contributing to
H^{n+1}(Y^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
is
E_{2}^{0,n+1}=A^{\vee}
;

3.

the only possibly nonzero term contributing to
H^{n+2}(Y^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
is
E_{2}^{n+2,0}=H^{n+2}(X^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
;

4.

the only possibly nonzero differential in this range is

d_{n+2}:E_{2}^{0,n+1}\to E_{2}^{n+2,0}.

We deduce that
H^{n+1}(Y^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})=\operatorname{ker}(d_{n+2})
and
H^{n+2}(Y^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})=\operatorname{coker}(d_{n+2})
, so that
Y\in\mathcal{M}_{n+1}(BG)
if and only if
d_{n+2}
is an isomorphism.

This differential is transgressive, so we can understand it explicitly. Note that our fiber sequence
K(A,n+1)\to Y^{\circ}\to X^{\circ}
is the base-change via
\kappa
of the fiber sequence
K(A,n+1)\to\ast\to K(A,n+2)
. There the analogous transgressive differential in the Serre spectral sequence is necessarily an isomorphism because
\ast
has vanishing cohomology in positive degrees. By functoriality of the Serre spectral sequence, we deduce that
d_{n+2}
is an isomorphism if and only if
\kappa^{\ast}
is an isomorphism, proving our claim.
∎

In particular,
A\simeq\pi_{n+1}(Y^{\circ})\simeq\pi_{n+1}(Y)
, which is the “homotopy group to add” in going from our
n
-stage Moore anima to the
n+1
-stage, is canonically determined by the given
n
-stage Moore anima
X
: namely, as a
G/G^{\circ}
-equivariant compact Hausdorff group it identifies with the Pontryagin dual to
H^{n+2}(X^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
, which must necessarily be discrete if we are to be able to pass to the
n+1
-stage (though note that this discreteness is automatic when
G
is almost compact, Lemma
3.7
).

So we have no choice in the
A
, and the only choice is in
\kappa
. We now analyze the choice of
\kappa
further. It turns out that the following object is fundamental.

Definition 4.13
.

Let
G
be a locally compact Hausdorff group, and let
G^{\circ}
be the connected component of the identity in
G
. Define

c_{G}:=\tau_{\geq-1}R\Gamma(BG^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}),

which we view as an object in
\operatorname{D}_{G/G^{\circ}}(\operatorname{CondAb})
, the derived
\infty
-category of condensed abelian groups with
G/G^{\circ}
-action.

Thus
c_{G}
lives in (homological) degrees
0
and
-1
. We have

H_{0}(c_{G})=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}

and

H_{-1}(c_{G})=\operatorname{Hom}_{cont}(G^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}),

the usual unitary character group of the locally compact Hausdorff group
G^{\circ}
.

Remark 4.14
.

We interpret
c_{G}
as the “ideal” cohomology of
BG^{\circ}
; Moore anima are built to make this ideal a reality.

Now we can give the following translation of Lemma
4.12
, which describes the obstruction theory for Moore anima.

Theorem 4.15
.

Let
G
be a locally compact Hausdorff group, let
n\geq 1
, and let
t\in\mathcal{M}_{n}(BG)
be an
n
-stage Moore anima for
G
, namely
t:X\to BG
is a condensed anima over
BG
satisfying the axioms in Definition
4.4
. Let
H:=H^{n+2}(X^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
be the indicated cohomology group, with its natural
G/G^{\circ}
-action inherited from
X^{\circ}=X\times_{BG} BG^{\circ}
.

Then there is a canonical map

\sigma:H\to c_{G}[n+3]

in
\operatorname{D}_{G/G^{\circ}}(\operatorname{CondAb})
(with
H
in degree
0
) such that the anima of lifts of
t
to
\mathcal{M}_{n+1}(BG)
identifies with the anima of null-homotopies of
\sigma
.

Proof.

Consider
M:=\tau_{\geq 0}R\Gamma(X^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}[n+2])
as an object in
\operatorname{D}_{G/G^{\circ}}(\operatorname{CondAb})
concentrated in degrees
0
through
n+2
. By definition of an
n
-stage Moore anima, we have

\tau_{\geq 1}M=c_{G}[n+2].

On the other hand, by definition of
H
we have

\tau_{\leq 0}M\simeq H.

Thus the boundary map for the cofiber sequence

\tau_{\geq 1}M\to M\to\tau_{\leq 0}M

gives a map

\sigma:H\to c_{G}[n+3]

of the correct form.

To show it has the indicated property, note that a null-homotopy of
\sigma
is the same thing as a splitting of the above cofiber sequence. But this is the same as a map
H\to M
such that the composition with
M\to H_{0}M=H
is the identity map. If we define the compact Hausdorff
G/G^{\circ}
-module
A
by

A:=H^{\vee},

then by Lemma
3.10
this is also the same thing as a map

\kappa:X^{\circ}\to K(A,n+2)

for which
\kappa^{\ast}
as in Lemma
4.12
is an isomorphism. Thus the statement follows from Lemma
4.12
.
∎

Corollary 4.16
.

Suppose
G
is an almost compact group satisfying the following two assumptions:

1.

H^{2}(BG^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})=0
;

2.

For all discrete
G/G^{\circ}
-modules
M
and all
i\geq 3
we have

\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{G/G^{\circ}}(M,c_{G})=0

where

c_{G}=\tau_{\geq-1}R\Gamma(BG^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}).

Then
G
admits a Moore anima, unique up to isomorphism.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.9
, condition 1 implies that
\mathcal{M}_{1}(BG)=\ast
. By Theorem
4.15
, condition 2 implies that the homotopy fibers of the maps

\mathcal{M}_{n+1}(BG)\to\mathcal{M}_{n}(BG)

have
\pi_{i}=\ast
for
i<n
. When we pass to the inverse limit we deduce that the homotopy fibers of

\varprojlim_{n}\mathcal{M}_{n}(BG)\to\mathcal{M}_{1}(BG)

have
\pi_{0}=\ast
. But by the above the target is
\ast
and by Corollary
4.8
the source is
\mathcal{M}(BG)
; the conclusion follows.
∎

Example 4.17
.

Suppose
G
is a totally disconnected locally compact Hausdorff group. Then
G^{\circ}=\{1\}
, so

R\Gamma(BG^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}.

We see inductively that every
H
appearing in Corollary
4.15
is
0
, and consequently
\mathcal{M}_{n+1}(BG)\to\mathcal{M}_{n}(BG)
is an isomorphism for all
n\geq 1
. Moreover
\mathcal{M}_{1}(BG)=\ast
. We deduce that
\mathcal{M}(BG)=\ast
and that
BG
is the (truly unique) Moore anima for
G
.

5
Background on class formations

The notion of class formation arose as an axiomatization of the cohomological approach to class field theory. We recall one form of the classical definition from
[
3
]
:

Definition 5.1
.

A
class formation
is a pair
(\Gamma,A)
where:

1.

\Gamma
is a profinite group;

2.

A
is a discrete abelian group with continuous
\Gamma
-action,

such that the following conditions on group cohomology are satisfied, for every open subgroup
H\subset\Gamma
and every open normal subgroup
N\subset H
:

1.

H^{1}(H/N;A^{N})=0
;

2.

H^{2}(H/N;A^{N})
is cyclic of order
[H:N]
.

The point of the notion of class formation is that if we give ourselves a bit of extra structure, namely a collection of generators
(u_{H,N})_{H,N}
for these cyclic groups
H^{2}(H/N;A^{N})
, then via the Tate-Nakayama lemma (
[
60
]
IX) we deduce that cup product with
u_{H,N}
induces an isomorphism in Tate cohomology

\widehat{H}^{i}(H/N;\mathbb{Z})\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\widehat{H}^{i+2}(H/N;A^{N})

for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. In particular, when
i=-2
we get

(H/N)^{ab}\simeq A^{H}/\operatorname{tr}(A^{N}).

If we assume suitable compatibility properties for these
u_{H,N}
under inflation and restrction (see
[
60
]
XI), then we can pass to the limit over
N
to get the associated
Artin maps

A^{H}\to H^{ab}

which have dense image with respect to the topology on profinite group
H^{ab}
, and satisfy various natural compatibilities as
H
varies over open subgroups of
\Gamma
. In this way we can think of a class formation as providing abelian groups which naturally refine the
H^{ab}
as
H
varies over open subgroups of
\Gamma
.

In practice,
\Gamma
will be an absolute Galois group. In particular, it will not actually be canonically defined as a profinite group, but only as a connected profinite groupoid, or equivalently a
Galois category
in the sense of
[
27
]
. To make our discussions more canonical, we therefore switch to that language.

Definition 5.2
.

A
class formation
(reformulated) is a pair
(\mathcal{C},A)
where:

1.

\mathcal{C}
is a Galois category;

2.

A
is a sheaf of abelian groups on
\mathcal{C}
,

such that the following conditions are satisfied, for every connected object
X\in\mathcal{C}
and every subgroup
G\subset\operatorname{Aut}(X)
:

1.

H^{1}(G;A(X))=0
;

2.

H^{2}(G;A(X))
is cyclic of order
\#G
.

It is clear from Grothendieck Galois theory that, if we fix a fiber functor on the Galois category
\mathcal{C}
, or equivalently, if we fix a profinite group
\Gamma
and an identification of
\mathcal{C}
with the category of finite continuous
\Gamma
-sets, then the notions of class formation in Definitions
5.1
and
5.2
are one-to-one equivalent: the data and axioms line up.

In the following section we aim to explain how to get Weil groups, or rather (to say things more canonically) Weil groupoids, from class formations, essentially retelling part of
[
3
]
. But for that it’s not enough to just have a class formation: one needs extra data. At a first level, one needs chosen compatible generators for the groups
H^{2}(G;A(X))
, exactly as is required to get Artin maps following Tate-Nakayama as above. But actually one needs more, namely some cocycle-level version of such data, in order to canonically build the corresponding “group extensions”, not just up to isomorphism.

In fact, such cocycle level lifts also show up in another place in the theory, at least implicitly, namely in the proof of Tate-Nakayama. Given a finite group
G
, a
G
-module
A
, and a class
u\in H^{2}(G;A)
, then one way to describe the Tate-Nakayama argument is that one interprets
u\in\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{G}(\mathbb{Z},A)
instead; then one represents
u
by a map

\mathbb{Z}\to A[2]

in
\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z}[G])
and passes to the homotopy fiber. For this fiber one has the hypothesis that its Tate cohomology vanishes in two consecutive degrees for all subgroups
H\subset G
, and one wants to conclude it vanishes identically. The point in the end (after some standard devissage to reduce to
p
-groups, then to cyclic groups of order
p
) is that the Tate cohomology of cyclic groups is 2–periodic, so that vanishing in two consecutive degrees is equivalent to vanishing identically.

Now, the datum of a cocycle-level lift of the group cohomology class
u
is the same as the datum of the above map in the derived
\infty
-category of
G
-modules, and it is also the same as the datum of the fiber object, equipped with isomorphisms
H_{0}\simeq\mathbb{Z}
and
H_{1}\simeq A
. All of these (and more) are just different ways of talking about gerbes over
BG
banded by
A
. As usual in mathematics, however, the interpretation which is closest to linear algebra is the easiest one to work with. In the end we are led to the following.

Definition 5.3
.

A
refined class formation
is a triple
(\mathcal{C},F,\alpha)
where:

1.

\mathcal{C}
is a Galois category;

2.

F:\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})
is a
co-sheaf
such that
F(X)
is concentrated in the range of degrees
[0,1]
for all
X\in\mathcal{C}
;

3.

\alpha
is a map
H_{0}F\to\mathbb{Z}
of functors
\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{Ab}
to the constant functor with value
\mathbb{Z}
, such that
\alpha(X)
is an isomorphism for all connected
X\in\mathcal{C}
.

We will show that if
(\mathcal{C},F,\alpha)
is a refined class formation, then
H_{1}F
, which is a co-presheaf on
\mathcal{C}
, also carries the canonical structure of a sheaf via the formalism of
transfer maps
, and that
(\mathcal{C},H_{1}F)
is a class formation. Conversely, we will explain what it takes to promote a class formation to a refined class formation.

For this we use three fairly standard lemmas, for which our basic references will be
[
12
]
and
[
11
]
. The first explicates sheaves/cosheaves on Galois categories attached to finite groups. Every Galois category is canonically the filtered union of Galois categories each of which is equivalent to the Galois category of a finite group, so this is also useful for general Galois categories.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
G
be a finite group, and let
\mathcal{C}(G)
denote the Galois category of finite
G
-sets. Let
\mathcal{D}
be an
\infty
-category which admits all finite coproducts and all
H
-orbit objects for all subgroups
H\subset G
. Then restriction along the inclusion
BG\subset\mathcal{C}(G)
of the
G
-torsors induces an equivalence

\operatorname{CoSh}(\mathcal{C}(G);\mathcal{D})\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\operatorname{Fun}(BG;\mathcal{D})

with inverse given by left Kan extension.

Dually, if
\mathcal{D}
admits all finite products and all
H
-fixed point objects, then restriction along
BG\subset\mathcal{C}(G)^{op}
induces an equivalence

\operatorname{Sh}(\mathcal{C};\mathcal{D})\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\operatorname{Fun}(BG;\mathcal{D})

with inverse given by right Kan extension.

Proof.

It suffices to treat the dual assertion about sheaves and right Kan extensions. Then for
d\in\mathcal{D}
, consider the composition
h_{d}\circ F
, where
h_{d}=\operatorname{Map}(d,-):\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{An}
. By definition
F
is a sheaf if and only if
h_{d}\circ F
is a sheaf for all
d\in\mathcal{D}
. On the other hand the pointwise criterion for right Kan extension shows that
F
is the right Kan extension of its restriction to a given full subcategory if and only if the same is true for
h_{d}\circ F
for all
d\in\mathcal{D}
. Thus we reduce to the case
\mathcal{D}=\operatorname{An}
, where the assertion is given by
[
12
]
Example 4.4.
∎

The second result explains the mechanism of “transfer maps”, which provide a canonical presheaf structure on any cosheaf and dually a canonical pre-cosheaf structure on any sheaf.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a Galois category, and let
\mathcal{D}
be a semi-additive
\infty
-category which admits
G
-orbit and
G
-fixed point objects for all finite groups
G
. Denote by
\operatorname{Span}(\mathcal{C})
the span category (
(2,1)
-category, actually) of
\mathcal{C}
, where the objects are the objects of
\mathcal{C}
, the maps from
X
to
Y
are the spans

X\leftarrow M\to Y

in
\mathcal{C}
, and composition is given by pullback. Note that there are obvious functors

\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{Span}(\mathcal{C})

and

\mathcal{C}^{op}\to\operatorname{Span}(\mathcal{C}),

sending each object to itself and sending a map
X\to Y
to the span
X=X\to Y
(resp.
Y\leftarrow X=X
). Then:

1.

Let
\operatorname{Fun}^{coshf}(\operatorname{Span}(\mathcal{C}),\mathcal{D})
denote the full subcategory of functors
F:\operatorname{Span}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{D}
such that the restriction along
\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{Span}(\mathcal{C})
is a cosheaf. Then we have

\operatorname{Fun}^{coshf}(\operatorname{Span}(\mathcal{C}),\mathcal{D})\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\operatorname{coSh}(\mathcal{C};\mathcal{D})

via restriction along
\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{Span}(\mathcal{C})
.

2.

Let
\operatorname{Fun}^{shf}(\operatorname{Span}(\mathcal{C}),\mathcal{D})
denote the full subcategory of functors
F:\operatorname{Span}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{D}
such that the restriction along
\mathcal{C}^{op}\to\operatorname{Span}(\mathcal{C})
is a sheaf. Then we have

\operatorname{Fun}^{shf}(\operatorname{Span}(\mathcal{C}),\mathcal{D})\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\operatorname{Sh}(\mathcal{C};\mathcal{D})

via restriction along
\mathcal{C}^{op}\to\operatorname{Span}(\mathcal{C})
.

3.

Let
F\in\operatorname{Fun}(\operatorname{Span}(\mathcal{C}),\mathcal{D})
. Any two of the following three properties imply the third:

(a)

F\in\operatorname{Fun}^{coshf}(\operatorname{Span}(\mathcal{C}),\mathcal{D})
;

(b)

F\in\operatorname{Fun}^{shf}(\operatorname{Span}(\mathcal{C}),\mathcal{D})
;

(c)

for all connected
X\in\mathcal{C}
and all subgroups
G\subset\operatorname{Aut}(X)
, the natural “transfer” map
F(X)_{G}\to F(X)^{G}
, associated to the induced
G
-action on
F(X)
, is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Claims 1 and 2 are dual, so it suffices to treat 2. Let
\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{C})
denote the poset of isomorphism classes of connected Galois objects in
\mathcal{C}
, and for
N\in\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{C})
consider the full subcategory
\mathcal{C}_{N}\subset\mathcal{C}
consisting of objects which can be covered by a finite disjoint union of objects in the isomorphism class
N
. The poset structure is such that

N\leq N^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{N^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{N}.

Note that if
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
for a profinite group
\Gamma
, then
\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{C})
is the poset of open normal subgroups of
\Gamma
with poset structure given by inclusion, and
\mathcal{C}_{N}
identifies with the Galois category of finite
\Gamma/N
-sets.

We have a filtered colimit

\mathcal{C}=\varinjlim_{N}\mathcal{C}_{N}

of finitary sites (c.f.
[
12
]
3.1) and
\mathcal{C}_{N}
is equivalent to the Galois category of finite
G
-sets for some finite group
G
. It follows readily that the claim in 2 reduces to the case when
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}(G)
for some finite group
G
. Then by Lemma
5.4
it suffices to show that restriction along
BG\subset\mathcal{C}^{op}\to\operatorname{Span}(\mathcal{C})
induces an equivalence

\operatorname{Fun}^{shf}(\operatorname{Span}(\mathcal{C}),\mathcal{D})\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\operatorname{Fun}(BG;\mathcal{D}).

Note by
[
12
]
Prop. 4.2 that the sheaf property for a presheaf
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{C}^{op}\to\mathcal{D}
breaks into two parts:

1.

\mathcal{F}
preserves finite products;

2.

\mathcal{F}(X/H)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{F}(X)^{H}
for all connected
X\in\mathcal{C}
and all subgroups
H\subset\operatorname{Aut}(X)
.

Assuming 1 (
\mathcal{F}
preserves finite products), we can (following
[
12
]
Prop. 4.11) rephrase 2 as the condition that
\mathcal{F}(X)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{F}(X\times G)^{G}
for all
X\in\mathcal{C}(G)
. It follows that our desired claim
\operatorname{Fun}^{shf}(\operatorname{Span}(\mathcal{C}),\mathcal{D})\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\operatorname{Fun}(BG,\mathcal{D})
, in the case
\mathcal{D}=\operatorname{CMon}(\operatorname{An})
, is given by
[
11
]
Prop. 2.9. To finish the proof of 2 (and 1, by duality) let us explain in the following paragraph why the general case reduces to that one.

Note that since
\mathcal{D}
is semi-additive, by
[
26
]
we have that for every
d\in\mathcal{D}
, the functor
h_{d}=\operatorname{Map}(d,-):\mathcal{D}\to\operatorname{An}
canonically refines to a functor with values in
\operatorname{CMon}(\operatorname{An})
. Thus by the case
\mathcal{D}=\operatorname{CMon}(\operatorname{An})
we can uniquely extend
h_{d}\circ F:BG\to\operatorname{CMon}(\operatorname{An})
to an object
\widetilde{h_{d}\circ F}\in\operatorname{Fun}^{shf}(\operatorname{Span}(\mathcal{C}),\operatorname{CMon}(\operatorname{An}))
. Then it suffices to show that for every
X\in\operatorname{Span}(\mathcal{C})
the additive functor
\mathcal{D}^{op}\to\operatorname{An}
sending

d\mapsto\widetilde{h_{d}\circ F}(X)

is representable. But we can write
X
as a finite disjoint union of orbits and then this follows from the assumption that
\mathcal{D}
has all fixed points for finite group actions.

Thus we have 1 and 2. Now consider 3. If
F
satisfies two of the three conditions (a),(b),(c), then in particular
F
is either a sheaf or a cosheaf, and therefore
F
is additive. Thus we can restrict our attention to additive functors
F\in\operatorname{Fun}^{\oplus}(\operatorname{Span}(\mathcal{C}),\mathcal{D})
. Now, for connected
X\in\mathcal{C}
and
G\subset\operatorname{Aut}(X)
a subgroup, consider the composition

F(X)_{G}\to F(X/G)\to F(X)^{G},

where the first map uses the co-presheaf structure and the second map uses the presheaf structure. Then
F
is a cosheaf if and only if the first map is an isomorphism for all
(X,G)
, and
F
is a sheaf if and only if the second map is an isomorphism for all
(X,G)
. Thus by the 2 of 3 property for isomorphisms it suffices to show that the composition of the above two maps identifies with the transfer as in (c), for any
F\in\operatorname{Fun}^{\oplus}(\operatorname{Span}(\mathcal{C}),\mathcal{D})
. In other words, we need to see that the
G\times G
-equivariant map

F(X)\to F(X/G)\to F(X)

identifies with
\sum_{g\in G}g
. But this follows by definition of composition in the span category.
∎

In particular, given a cosheaf on
\mathcal{C}
, we get a canonical presheaf structure with the same values on objects (uniquely extend the cosheaf to the span category by 1, then restrict along
\mathcal{C}^{op}\to\operatorname{Span}(\mathcal{C})
), and this presheaf will be a sheaf if and only if the transfer-isomorphism condition 3(c) holds. We formalize this as follows.

Definition 5.6
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a Galois category,
\mathcal{D}
a semi-additive
\infty
-category which admits
G
-orbit and
G
-fixed point objects for all finite groups
G
, and let
F\in\operatorname{coSh}(\mathcal{C};\mathcal{D})
. Define the presheaf

F^{tr}\in\operatorname{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{D})

as the image of
F
under the composition

\operatorname{coSh}(\mathcal{C};\mathcal{D})\simeq\operatorname{Fun}^{coshf}(\operatorname{Span}(\mathcal{C}),\mathcal{D})\rightarrow\operatorname{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{D}),

where the first map is the inverse to the isomorphism of 1 of Lemma
5.5
and the second map is composition with the natural map
\mathcal{C}^{op}\to\operatorname{Span}(\mathcal{C})
described in Lemma
5.5
.

Dually, if
F
is a sheaf we get a co-presheaf
F^{tr}
. Note that by construction
F
and
F^{tr}
agree on objects; moreover the association
F\mapsto F^{tr}
is functorial in
\mathcal{C}
(for exact functors) and
\mathcal{D}
(for additive functors), via composition.

Example 5.7
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a Galois category and
A
a sheaf of abelian groups on
\mathcal{C}
. Then
U\mapsto R\Gamma(U;A)
describes the corresponding sheaf on
\mathcal{C}
with values in
\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})
(via the identification of the heart of
\operatorname{Sh}(\mathcal{C};\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z}))
with
\operatorname{Sh}(\mathcal{C};\operatorname{Ab})
). For
U\to V
a map of connected objects of
\mathcal{C}
, consider the induced transfer map

R\Gamma(U;A)\to R\Gamma(V;A)

in
\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})
, from the above definition. We claim that after applying
H_{-i}
, this corresopnds to the classical transfer (co-restriction) map in profinite group cohomology

H^{i}(U;A)\to H^{i}(V;A)

as defined via the mechanism of
\delta
-functors. Indeed, by dimension shift with injective resolutions we reduce to the case
i=0
; thus it suffices to show the claim on the non-derived level, i.e. that the transfer map
A(U)\to A(V)
from Definition
5.6
(with
\mathcal{D}=\operatorname{Ab}
) identifies with the classical transfer map. But
A(V)\subset A(U)
via restriction, so it suffices to see that the composition
A(U)\to A(V)\subset A(U)
is the same in both cases. This follows from the definition of composition in the span category.

Example 5.8
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a Galois category, and consider the cosheaf of abelian groups
\underline{\mathbb{Z}}
which sends
X\in\mathcal{C}
to the free abelian group on the set of components of
X
(so that by definition a refined class formation has
H_{0}=\underline{\mathbb{Z}}
). For a map
X\to Y
of connected objects, the associated transfer map

\underline{\mathbb{Z}}(Y)\rightarrow\underline{\mathbb{Z}}(X),

which is then just a map
\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
,
identifies with multiplication by the degree of
X\to Y
. The resulting presheaf
\mathbb{Z}^{tr}:\mathcal{C}^{op}\to\operatorname{Ab}
can be alternatively be embedded as a sub-presheaf of the constant sheaf with value
\mathbb{Q}
, as follows: for connected
X\in\mathcal{C}
, we map

\mathbb{Z}^{tr}(X)\to\mathbb{Q}

by the multiplication by
\frac{1}{\#X}
map
\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Q}
, thereby identifying
\mathbb{Z}^{tr}(X)
with
\frac{1}{\#X}\mathbb{Z}\subset\mathbb{Q}
. Here
\#X
denotes the degree of
X
over
\ast
(the cardinality of
X
, if we identify
\mathcal{C}
with the category of finite
\Gamma
-sets for some profinite group
\Gamma
).

This map
\mathbb{Z}^{tr}\to\mathbb{Q}
induces an isomorphism of sheafification of
\mathbb{Z}^{tr}
with the constant sheaf with value

\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z}:=\bigcup_{X}\frac{1}{\#X}\mathbb{Z}\subset\mathbb{Q},

the union being over all connected objects in
\mathcal{C}
.

The third standard lemma is a version of the Tate-Nakayama lemma.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
G
be a finite group, and let
\mathcal{D}
a
\mathbb{Z}
-linear stable
\infty
-category equipped with a t-structure and admitting
H
-fixed point and
H
-orbit objects for all subgroups
H\subset G
. Suppose
M\in\operatorname{Fun}(BG;\mathcal{D})
is an object of
\mathcal{D}
equipped with
G
-action such that
M
is concentrated in a bounded range of degrees (with respect to the t-structure). Then the following properties are equivalent:

1.

For all subgroups
H\subset G
, the object
M_{H}
(homotopy orbits for the
H
-action, or
H
-hyperhomology) is concentrated in a bounded range of degrees.

2.

For all subgroups
H\subset G
, the object
M^{H}
(homotopy fixed points for the
H
-action, or
H
-hypercohomology) is concentrated in a bounded range of degrees.

3.

For all subgroups
H\subset G
, the natural transfer map
M_{H}\to M^{H}
is an isomorphism.

Under these conditions, if
M
is in degrees
[a,b]
, then so are
M^{H}
and
M_{H}
for all
H\subset G
.

Proof.

Because 1 and 2 are dual to each other and 3 is self-dual (with respect to
\mathcal{D}\leftrightarrow\mathcal{D}^{op}
), it suffices to show that 2 and 3 are equivalent. Suppose 3 holds. If
M
is in degrees
[a,b]
, then
M^{H}
is in degrees
\leq b
and
M_{H}
is in degrees
\geq a
. As
M_{H}\simeq M^{H}
by hypothesis, this implies
M^{H}
is concentrated in degrees
[a,b]
, i.e. condition 2 holds (moreover we get the final claim). For the converse 2
\Rightarrow 3
, we follow the standard Tate-Nakayama argument.

We want to show 2
\Rightarrow
3. Replacing
\mathcal{D}
by
\operatorname{Ind}(\mathcal{D}_{0})
, where
\mathcal{D}_{0}
is the smallest stable subcategory of
\mathcal{D}
containing all
M^{H}
for
H\subset G
and closed under t-truncation, we can assume that
\mathcal{D}
is presentable. It follows by the multiplicative transfer machinery in
[
11
]
that

M^{tH}:=\operatorname{cofib}(M_{H}\to M^{H})

is a module over
\mathbb{Z}^{tH}\in\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})
. In fact the assignment
G/H\mapsto M^{tH}
organizes into a functor on the span category of finite
G
-sets as in the previous lemma, and even in that context it is a module over
G/H\mapsto\mathbb{Z}^{tH}
which is a ring in a natural way.

Now, first suppose
H
is cyclic. Then the standard resolution implies that
\mathbb{Z}^{tH}
is periodic. But assuming 2,
M^{tH}
vanishes in low enough degrees; therefore, being a module over a periodic ring spectrum, it must be
0
. This proves that 2
\Rightarrow
3 when
G
is cyclic. By the standard induction with a nontrivial central subgroup, we deduce from this that 2
\Rightarrow
3 also holds if
G
is nilpotent. Next, note that for a general finite group
G
and an object
M\in\mathcal{D}
with
G
-action and subgroup
H\subset G
, there are maps
M^{tG}\to M^{tH}
and
M^{tH}\to M^{tG}
such that the composition
M^{tG}\to M^{tG}
is multiplication by
\#[G:H]
. This follows from the multiplicative span category formalism referred to above, using the corresponding (obvious) fact for
G/H\mapsto\mathbb{Z}^{H}
.

In total, we deduce that
M^{tG}
is killed by multiplication by
[G:H]
for all nilpotent subgroups
H\subset G
. By the Sylow theorems there is no prime number which divides all such indices, and it follows that
M^{tG}=0
. Replacing
G
by a subgroup we deduce the claim.
∎

Now we will finally explain the relationship between class formations and refined class formations.

Theorem 5.10
.

For a Galois category
\mathcal{C}
, recall the associated subgroup
\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z}\subset\mathbb{Q}
from Example
5.8
.

Giving a refined class formation
(\mathcal{C},F,\alpha)
is equivalent to giving a class formation
(\mathcal{C},A)
together with a map

\kappa:\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z}\to R\Gamma(\mathcal{C};A[2])

in
\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})
which, on
H_{0}
, induces an isomorphism

\left(\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z}\right)/\mathbb{Z}\simeq H^{2}(\mathcal{C},A).

More precisely, given the latter data
(\mathcal{C},A,\kappa)
we can build a refined class formation as follows: by adjunction we can interpret
\kappa
as a map from the constant sheaf
\underline{\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z}}
to
A[2]
in the derived category of abelian sheaves on
\mathcal{C}
; if
\mathcal{F}
denotes the fiber of this map viewed as a derived sheaf, then
F:=\mathcal{F}^{tr}
(see Definition
5.6
) satisfies that for
X\in\mathcal{C}
connected the map

H_{0}(F(X))=H_{0}(\mathcal{F}(X))\to H_{0}(\underline{\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z}}(X))\overset{\cdot\#X}{\rightarrow}\mathbb{Q}

induces an isomorphism
\alpha:H_{0}(F(X))\simeq\mathbb{Z}
such that

(\mathcal{C},F,\alpha)

is a refined class formation, and this describes an equivalence from the 2-groupoid of data
(\mathcal{C},A,\kappa)
as above to the 2-groupoid of honest class formations. (We explicitly describe the inverse equivalence in the proof.)

Proof.

Let us first describe what will be the inverse to the above association. Suppose
(\mathcal{C},F,\alpha)
is a refined class formation. Combining Lemmas
5.5
and
5.9
, we see that
F^{tr}:\mathcal{C}^{op}\to\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})
is a sheaf. Its top nonvanishing homology group
A=H_{1}(F^{tr})
is therefore a sheaf of abelian groups.

Now we show that
(\mathcal{C},A)
is a class formation, i.e. that it satisfies the cohomology axioms 1 and 2 from Definition
5.2
. Let
X\in\mathcal{C}
be connected and
G\subset\operatorname{Aut}(X)
a subgroup. Since
F(X)_{H}=F(X/H)
lives in degrees
[0,1]
for all subgroups
H\subset G
, we deduce from Lemma
5.9
that
F(X)_{G}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}F(X)^{G}
, or in other words the Tate (hyper)cohomology of
F(X)
vanishes. As
F(X)
lives in degrees
[0,1]
with
H_{1}F(X)=A(X)
and
H_{0}F(X)=\mathbb{Z}
, we deduce that the Tate cohomology of
\mathbb{Z}
and
A(X)
differ by a shift of 2. The conclusion follows because
\widehat{H}^{0}(G,\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}/\#G\mathbb{Z}
and
\widehat{H}^{-1}(G,\mathbb{Z})=0
.

Next we show that one also gets a natural map

\kappa:\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z}\to R\Gamma(\mathcal{C};A[2])

from the refined class formation
(\mathcal{C},F,\alpha)
. For this, consider the sheafified Postnikov tower of the derived sheaf
F^{tr}
. On the presheaf level we have
H_{1}F^{tr}=A
which is already a sheaf, whereas
H_{0}F^{tr}=\mathbb{Z}^{tr}
because the formation of
(-)^{tr}
commutes with additive functors. By Example
5.8
we can identify the sheafification of this with the constant sheaf on
\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z}
. Thus, by adjunction, the Postnikov tower of
F^{tr}
as a derived sheaf is classified by a map
\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z}\to R\Gamma(\mathcal{C};A[2])
of the desired form.

Next we should check that this map has the announced property that it induces
\left(\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z}\right)/\mathbb{Z}\simeq H^{2}(\mathcal{C},A)
. By construction there is a fiber sequence

F(\ast)\to R\Gamma(\mathcal{C};\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z})\to R\Gamma(\mathcal{C};A[2])

where the second map comes from our map
\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z}\to R\Gamma(\mathcal{C};A[2])
. As
H_{0}F(\ast)=\mathbb{Z}
maps to
H_{0}(\mathcal{C};\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z})=\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z}
via inclusion (by construction), and
H_{-1}F(\ast)=0
by hypothesis, we deduce the claim from the long exact sequence in homology.

Now we explain the converse: supposing given a class formation
(\mathcal{C},A)
and a map

\kappa:\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z}\to R\Gamma(\mathcal{C};A[2])

inducing an isomorphism
\left(\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z}\right)/\mathbb{Z}\simeq H^{2}(\mathcal{C},A)
, let us build a refined class formation. The given map classifies the Postnikov tower of a derived sheaf
\mathcal{F}
on
\mathcal{C}
with sheafified
H_{0}
fixed as the constant sheaf
\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z}
, with
H_{1}
fixed as
A
, and all other sheafified homology groups
0
. More precisely, we have a fiber sequence

\mathcal{F}\to R\Gamma(-;\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z})\to R\Gamma(-;A[2])

in
\operatorname{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{op};\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z}))
. We claim:

1.

\mathcal{F}\to R\Gamma(-;\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z})
is injective on presheaf-level
H_{0}
and identifies
H_{0}\mathcal{F}
with
\mathbb{Z}^{tr}
(as a sub-presheaf of the constant sheaf on
\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z}
via the association of Example
5.8
);

2.

H_{i}\mathcal{F}=0
on the presheaf level for all
i\neq 0,1
.

Assuming this, we deduce from Lemmas
5.5
and
5.9
that
F:=\mathcal{F}^{tr}
is a cosheaf living in degrees
[0,1]
. Moreover its
H_{0}
is a cosheaf of abelian groups such that applying
(-)^{tr}
gives
\mathbb{Z}^{tr}
, and the only possibility for that is the constant cosheaf
\mathbb{Z}
. Thus we get a refined class formation. Moreover, the two procedures (going from refined class formations to class formations plus the extra map, and going backwards) are inverse because
(-)^{tr}
is involutive (when it is well-defined) by construction, and this will finish the proof.

Thus it suffices to show claims 1 and 2 above. For this, we note the following simple consequence of the class formation axioms: for any connected
X\in\mathcal{C}
, we have:

1.

H^{1}(X;A)=0
;

2.

the restriction map

H^{2}(\mathcal{C};A)\to H^{2}(X;A)

is surjective, and its kernel is the unique subgroup of order
\#X
in
H^{2}(\mathcal{C};A)
.

Granting this, from the long exact sequence associated to
\mathcal{F}(X)\to R\Gamma(X;\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z})\to R\Gamma(X;A[2])
we deduce that
H_{0}\mathcal{F}(X)
identifies with the kernel of the map from
\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z}
to the quotient of
\left(\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z}\right)/\mathbb{Z}
by its
\#X
-torsion subgroup
\frac{1}{\#X}\mathbb{Z}/\mathbb{Z}
, which gives desired claim 1 above.

The remaining claim is that
\mathcal{F}
lives in degrees
[0,1]
also on the presheaf level, or equivalently, that the presheaf level
\tau_{\geq 0}\mathcal{F}(-)
is a sheaf. Thus we need to see that if
X\in{C}
is connected and
G\subset\operatorname{Aut}(X)
is a subgroup, then

\tau_{\geq 0}\mathcal{F}(X/G)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\left(\tau_{\geq 0}\mathcal{F}(X)\right)^{G}.

Comparing with the known result
\mathcal{F}(X/G)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{F}(X)^{G}
, we see that it suffices to show that
\left(\tau_{\geq 0}\mathcal{F}(X)\right)^{G}
lives in degrees
\geq 0
. For that, by Lemma
5.9
, it suffices to show that the
G
-Tate hypercohomology of
\tau_{\geq 0}\mathcal{F}(X)
vanishes, for all
X\in\mathcal{C}
connected and all subgroups
G\subset\operatorname{Aut}(X)
.

However, note that by the above analysis, the class
u_{G,X}\in H^{2}(G;A(X))
which classifies Postnikov tower of the
G
-object
\tau_{\geq 0}\mathcal{F}(X)
is uniquely characterized as follows: its inflation

\operatorname{infl}(u_{G,X})\in H^{2}(X/G;A)

is equal to the image of
\frac{1}{\#G}\mathbb{Z}
via the composition

\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z}\to H^{2}(\mathcal{C};A)\to H^{2}(X/G;A)

where the first map is induced by our given data and the second map is restriction.

In particular,
u_{G,X}
generates the cyclic group
H^{2}(G;A(X))
of order
\#G
. By the class field axioms it follows that the Tate hypercohomology we’re interested vanishes in two consecutive degrees (for all
G
), therefore it vanishes in all degrees by Tate-Nakayama, finishing the proof.
∎

Corollary 5.11
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},A)
be a class formation. Denote by
\mathcal{G}
the 2-groupoid of lifts of
(\mathcal{C},A)
to a refined class formation. Then for the homotopy sets of
\mathcal{G}
we have the following:

1.

\pi_{0}\mathcal{G}=\operatorname{InjHom}(H^{2}(\mathcal{C};A),\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})
;

2.

\pi_{1}\mathcal{G}\simeq\operatorname{Ext}(\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z},\Gamma(\mathcal{C};A))
;

3.

\pi_{2}\mathcal{G}\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z},\Gamma(\mathcal{C};A))
.

Here
\operatorname{InjHom},\operatorname{Ext},\operatorname{Hom}
stand for respectively the sets of injective homomorphisms, isomorphism classes of extensions, and homomorphisms, all in the category of abelian groups.

Proof.

By Theorem
5.10
,
\mathcal{G}
is a union of components of

\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\operatorname{Map}_{\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})}(\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z},R\Gamma(\mathcal{C};A[2])),

namely it is the full sub-2-groupoid on those maps which are surjective on
H_{0}
with kernel equal to
\mathbb{Z}
. Because
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}=0
for
i>1
in the category of abelian groups, and
H^{1}(\mathcal{C};A)=0
, we get that

\pi_{0}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\operatorname{Hom}(\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z},H^{2}(\mathcal{C};A)),\pi_{1}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\operatorname{Ext}(\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z},\Gamma(\mathcal{C};A)),\pi_{2}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\operatorname{Hom}(\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z},\Gamma(\mathcal{C};A)).

To get the homotopy groups of
\mathcal{G}
we just impose the condition on
\pi_{0}
that the homomorphism
\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z}\to H^{2}(\mathcal{C};A)
be surjective with kernel
\mathbb{Z}
. Thus

\pi_{0}\mathcal{G}=\operatorname{Iso}(\left(\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z}\right)/\mathbb{Z},H^{2}(\mathcal{C};A)).

Given such an isomorphism we obviously get an injective homomorphism

H^{2}(\mathcal{C};A)\to\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}

by taking the inverse; conversely, using that

H^{2}(\mathcal{C};A)=\cup_{X}H^{2}(G;A(X))

via the inflation maps, where
X
runs over all Galois objects in
\mathcal{C}
with group
G
, we deduce that the image any injection
H^{2}(\mathcal{C};A)\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
must be
\left(\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z}\right)/\mathbb{Z}
, thereby establishing that

\operatorname{Iso}(\left(\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z}\right)/\mathbb{Z},H^{2}(\mathcal{C};A))\simeq\operatorname{InjHom}(H^{2}(\mathcal{C};A),\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})

and hence finishing the proof.
∎

Note that the
\pi_{0}
-level datum here, namely the datum of an isomorphism
H^{2}(\mathcal{C};A)\simeq\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z}/\mathbb{Z}
, exactly corresponds to the usual data of chosen compatible generators for the
H^{2}(G;A(X))
in the class formation, or equivalently compatible systems of
\operatorname{inv}
maps as in
[
60
]
XI.

Example 5.12
.

Suppose
(\mathcal{C},F,\alpha)
is a refined class formation, with corresponding class formation
(\mathcal{C},A)
plus map

\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z}\overset{\kappa}{\rightarrow}R\Gamma(\mathcal{C};A[2]),

from Theorem
5.10
.

We have the following canonical ways to build new refined class formations from
(\mathcal{C},F,\alpha)
, and their interpretations via Theorem
5.10
:

1.

For any connected object
X\in\mathcal{C}
, by restriction we get a refined class formation

(\mathcal{C}_{/X},F|_{\mathcal{C}_{/X}},\alpha|_{\mathcal{C}_{/X}}).

(Note that if
\mathcal{C}
corresponds to a profinite group
\Gamma
, then
\mathcal{C}_{/X}
corresponds to an open subgroup
H\subset\Gamma
up to conjugation.)

This corresponds to the class formation
(\mathcal{C}_{/X},A|_{\mathcal{C}_{/X}})
together with the map

\kappa^{\prime}:\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}_{/X}}\mathbb{Z}\to R\Gamma(\mathcal{C}_{/X};A|_{\mathcal{C}_{/X}}[2])

given as follows:

\kappa^{\prime}(\alpha)=\operatorname{res}(\kappa\left(\frac{1}{\#X}\alpha\right)),

where
\operatorname{res}:R\Gamma(\mathcal{C};A[2])\rightarrow R\Gamma(\mathcal{C}_{/X};A|_{\mathcal{C}_{/X}}[2])
is the usual restriction map.

2.

For any full subcategory
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{C}
closed under finite limits and colimits (which is then a Galois category in its own right), we get a refined class formation

(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},F|_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}},\alpha|_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}).

(Note that if
