\mathcal{C}
corresponds to a profinite group
\Gamma
, then
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
corresponds to a quotient
\Gamma\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma^{\prime}
.)

This corresponds to the class formation
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},A|_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}})
together with the map

\kappa^{\prime}:\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}\mathbb{Z}\to R\Gamma(\mathcal{C}^{\prime};A|_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}[2])

which is uniquely determined as follows: the inflation map

\operatorname{infl}:R\Gamma(\mathcal{C}^{\prime};A|_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}[2])\to R\Gamma(\mathcal{C};A[2])

is an isomorphism on
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
and injective on
\pi_{0}
, hence on maps from any abelian group in degree
0
it gives a fully faithful functor of 2-groupoids, so it suffices to characterize
\operatorname{infl}\circ\kappa^{\prime}
, and this is given by

\operatorname{infl}\circ\kappa^{\prime}=\kappa|_{\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}\mathbb{Z}}

using that
\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}\mathbb{Z}\subset\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z}
.

3.

Suppose given a subsheaf
K\subset A
of abelian groups which is
cohomologically trivial
, i.e.
H^{i}(X;K)=0
for all
X\in\mathcal{C}
connected and all
i>0
, or equivalently (see
[
57
]
, appendix of Tate) the
G
-Tate cohomology of
K(X)
vanishes for all connected
X\in\mathcal{C}
and all subgroups
G\subset\operatorname{Aut}(X)
.

Then the quotient
A/K
on the presheaf level is a sheaf and
(\mathcal{C},A/K)
is a class formation. The composition

\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z}\overset{\kappa}{\rightarrow}R\Gamma(\mathcal{C};A[2])\to R\Gamma(\mathcal{C};A/K[2])

corresponds to the refined class formation given by
(\mathcal{C},F^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
where
F^{\prime}
is defined by

F^{\prime}(X):=\operatorname{cofib}(K^{tr}[1]\to F)

where the map
K^{tr}[1]\to F
is uniquely characterized on
H_{1}
where it is given by applying
(-)^{tr}
to the inclusion
K\to A
, and
\alpha^{\prime}
is uniquely induced by
\alpha
via the map
F\to F^{\prime}
which is an isomorphism on
H_{0}
.

Remark 5.13
.

We can summarize points 1 and 2 as follows: a (refined) class formation on a profinite group canonically induces (refined) class formations on any open subgroup or quotient group. Note that, in fact, the data of a (refined) class formation on a profinite group is
equivalent
to the data of compatible (refined) class formations on each of its finite quotients; this is obvious from the definitions.

For the class formations
(\mathcal{C},A)
arising in number theory,
A
will not just be a sheaf of abstract abelian groups, rather it will be a sheaf of topological (or condensed) abelian groups. This extra structure is important to remember for discussing the Weil groups. We have the following obvious condensed analog of Definition
5.3
.

Definition 5.14
.

A
condensed refined class formation
is a triple
(\mathcal{C},F,\alpha)
consisting of a Galois category
\mathcal{C}
, a cosheaf

F:\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{CondAb})

which is sectionwise concentrated in degrees
[0,1]
, and a natural transformation

\alpha:H_{0}F\to\mathbb{Z}

such that for all connected
X\in\mathcal{C}
, the map
\alpha:H_{0}F(X)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\mathbb{Z}
is an isomorphism.

Note that if
(\mathcal{C},F,\alpha)
is a condensed refined class formation, then we get an underlying refined class formation, together with the structure of condensed sheaf on the “class group” sheaf
A
, produced by the formalism of transfers exactly as in the non-condensed case.

Conversely:

Lemma 5.15
.

Suppose given:

1.

a refined class formation, corresponding to data
(\mathcal{C},A,\kappa)
as in Theorem
5.10
;

2.

a promotion
A^{cond}
of
A
to a sheaf of condensed abelian groups.

Assume that
A^{cond}(X)
is almost compact (Definition
3.1
) for all connected
X\in\mathcal{C}
. Then there is a unique condensed refined class formation inducing the data 1,2 above via the obvious forgetful functor.

Proof.

View the datum
\kappa
as a map

\mathbb{Z}^{tr}\to A[1]

in
\operatorname{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z}))
(this classifies the Postnikov tower of
F^{tr}
viewed as a presheaf). Because
\mathbb{Z}
is discrete, by the datum 2 this is equivalent to giving a map

\mathbb{Z}^{tr}\to A^{cond}[1]

in
\operatorname{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{CondAb}))
. Let
\mathcal{F}
denote the fiber of this map. Then it suffices to show that
\mathcal{F}
is a sheaf (as then
\mathcal{F}^{tr}
will be a cosheaf and hence will give the required condensed refined class formation). Thus, let
X\in\mathcal{C}
be connected and let
G\subset\operatorname{Aut}(X)
be a subgroup, with
Y:=X/G
. Consider the map

\mathcal{F}(Y)\to\mathcal{F}(X)^{G};

we need to show it’s an isomorphism. Note that we know this to be true on underlying objects of
\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})
, i.e. after applying
(-)(\ast)
.

Let
K(X)
denote the largest compact subgroup of
A^{cond}(X)
, so that we have a short exact sequence of
G
-objects of
\operatorname{CondAb}

0\to K(X)\to A^{cond}(X)\to C(X)\to 0

where
C(X)
is either
0
,
\mathbb{Z}
, or
\mathbb{R}
with some undetermined action of
G
. The induced homomorphism
A^{cond}(X)^{G}\to C(X)^{G}
(of locally compact Hausdorff abelian groups, with target isomorphic to either
0
,
\mathbb{Z}
, or
\mathbb{R}
) has image containing
\#G\cdot C(X)^{G}
; it follows that the cokernel is a finite (discrete) abelian group. Then from the long exact sequence in cohomology and the fact that compact Hausdorff abelian groups are an abelian subcategory of
\operatorname{CondAb}
closed under extensions, we deduce that
H^{i}(G;A^{cond}(X))
is compact Hausdorff for all
i>0
.

In particular, we deduce that
H^{1}(G;A^{cond}(X))=0
as a condensed group and
H^{2}(G;A^{cond}(X))
is cyclic of order
\#G
as a condensed group, from the corresponding facts about the underlying groups. Then using the long exact sequence in
G
-cohomology associated to

A^{cond}(X)[1]\to\mathcal{F}(X)\to\mathbb{Z}[0]

we see that
H_{0}(\mathcal{F}(X)^{G})\simeq\mathbb{Z}
as a condensed abelian group and so our claim in degree
0
follows from the claim on underlying abelian groups. Similarly we deduce
H_{i}(\mathcal{F}(X)^{G})
is compact Hausdorff for
i<0
hence vanishes by the corresponding fact on underlying abelian groups. All that remains is the claim in degree
1
, but that amounts to the assumption that
A^{cond}
is a sheaf.
∎

Now let us discuss the usual class formations from number theory, paying special attention to the question of how canonically we can promote them to refined class formations.

Example 5.16
.

1.

Let us start with the most important example, the class formation for the rational number field
\mathbb{Q}
. The Galois category is
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Q}}
, the finite etale site of
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Q})
. The sheaf of condensed abelian groups
A
on
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Q}}
is provided by the idele class groups, so

A(K)=\mathbb{A}_{K}^{\times}/K^{\times}

for
K
a number field. Note that
A(K)
is almost compact: the usual norm map

N:A(K)\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}

is surjective with compact kernel
A(K)^{1}
. The class field axioms, plus the sheaf axiom for
A
, are satisfied by global class field theory for number fields. Thus, by Lemmas
5.11
and
5.15
, the 2-groupoid of lifts of
(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Q}},A)
to a condensed refined class formation has:

(a)

\pi_{0}=\operatorname{Inj}(H^{2}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Q}};A),\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})
;

(b)

\pi_{1}=\operatorname{Ext}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{A}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}^{\times})=0
;

(c)

\pi_{2}=\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{A}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}^{\times})=\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

Again by global class field theory, there is a canonical point in
\pi_{0}
, corresponding to the usual isomorphism

\operatorname{inv}:H^{2}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Q}};A)\simeq\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z},

or, equivalently, the usual system of fundamental classes, or again equivalently (see
[
65
]
), the usual system of Artin maps. (Actually there are two canonical choices, differing by sign; let us say that we take the convention that uniformizers go to arithmetic Frobenius elements via the associated Artin maps.)

Thus, any two refined (condensed) class formations, each inducing the usual global class field theory for number fields, are isomorphic by an isomorphism which is unique up to homotopy; but that homotopy is not unique: it has an
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
-ambiguity.

We can get rid of this ambiguity as follows: we replace
A
by its subsheaf
A^{1}
(the kernel of the norm map). Note that

\mathbb{R}_{>0}\oplus A^{1}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}A

via the map given by the inclusion on the second factor and the map induced by the inclusion of the positive reals into the archimedean factor of
\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}^{\times}
on the first factor. Repeating the above analysis for the class formation
(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Q}},A^{1})
we find that
\pi_{1}=\pi_{2}=0
so there is a completely unique condensed refined class formation
(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Q}},F^{1},\alpha)
inducing
(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Q}},A^{1})
and the usual system of Artin maps for number fields. Then setting

F=\mathbb{R}_{>0}[1]\oplus F^{1},

we find that

(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Q}},F,\alpha)

gives a
canonical
condensed refined class formation refining the usual class formation and Artin maps of class field theory for number fields.

2.

Let
K
be a number field. Then applying Example
5.12
to
\operatorname{Spec}(K)\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Q}}
, we deduce that the restriction of
(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Q}},F,\alpha)
to
\mathcal{C}_{K}=(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Q}})_{/\operatorname{Spec}(K)}
gives a canonical condensed refined class formation (refining class field theory over
K
).

3.

More generally, let
K
be a number field and
S
a set of places of
K
, which we will eventually assume to be nonempty. Define a finite extension
L/K
to be
unramified outside
S
if for all places
\nu
of
K
not in
S
and all places
\mu
of
L
above
\nu
, the extension of local fields
L_{\mu}/K_{\nu}
is:

(a)

unramified in the usual sense, if
\nu
is nonarchimedean;

(b)

trivial, if
\nu
is archimedean.

Then the full subcategory
\mathcal{C}_{K,S}\subset\mathcal{C}_{K}
of finite etale
K
-algebras which are products of field extensions
L/K
unramified outside
S
is a Galois category. (If
S
contains all archimedean places, then this is the finite etale site of
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{K,S})
.)

Consider the sheaf
U
on
\mathcal{C}_{K,S}
given by

U(L)=\prod_{\mu\not\in S_{L}}(L_{\mu}^{\times})^{1}

for any finite extension
L/K
unramified outside
S
, where
S_{L}
denotes the set of places of
L
above
S
and
(L_{\mu}^{\times})^{1}\subset L_{\mu}^{\times}
denotes the maximal compact subgroup (group of elements of norm 1). Then
U
is cohomologically trivial. Moreover, if
S\neq\emptyset
, then the map

U(L)\subset\mathbb{A}_{L}^{\times}\to A(L)=\mathbb{A}_{L}^{\times}/L^{\times}

is injective for all
L
as above. Thus, by Example
5.12
we deduce that when
S\neq\emptyset
,

(\mathcal{C}_{K,S},A/U)

is a class formation, and

(\mathcal{C}_{K,S},\operatorname{cofib}(U[1]\to F),\alpha)

is a canonical condensed refined class formation refining it.

Note that although the Galois category
\mathcal{C}_{K,S}
does not change if we add or subtract complex places from
S
, the class formation does change, and so will the associated Weil group.

More explicitly, the class groups in this class formation are as follows: for
L/K
unramified outside
S
, we have

A_{L,S_{L}}=L^{\times}\backslash\mathbb{A}_{L}^{\times}/\prod_{\mu\not\in S_{L}}(L_{\mu}^{\times})^{1}.

Suppose
S
is finite and contains all archimedean places, so that
L/K
corresponds to the finite etale
\mathcal{O}_{K,S}
-algebra
\mathcal{O}_{L,S_{L}}
. By the usual adelic descent, we can reinterpret the above class group as being the “compactly supported Picard group” of
\mathcal{O}_{L,S_{L}}
, that is the abelian group of line bundles on
\mathcal{O}_{L,S_{L}}
equipped with a trivialization on base-change to
\prod_{\mu\in S_{L}}L_{\mu}
. (Such objects have no automorphisms, so this
\operatorname{Pic}_{c}(\mathcal{O}_{L,S_{L}})
is legitimately an abelian group.) This interpretation gives us an exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{O}_{L,S_{L}}^{\times}\to\prod_{\mu\in S_{L}}L_{\mu}^{\times}\to A_{L,S_{L}}\to\operatorname{Pic}(\mathcal{O}_{L,S_{L}})\to 0

which is often convenient for analyzing
A_{L,S_{L}}
.

4.

In the above examples with number fields, we managed to find
canonical
refined class formations refining the usual class formations. But now we consider the ostensibly much simpler setting of
finite fields
, where, strangely, there is seemingly no canonical choice.

Let
p
be a prime with associated finite field
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, and let
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
denote the Galois category of finite etale
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-schemes. Then

(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}},\mathbb{Z})

is a class formation, where we mean the constant sheaf on
\mathbb{Z}
. Moreover, there is a unique isomorphism

\operatorname{inv}:H^{2}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}};\mathbb{Z})\simeq\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}

such that the induced Artin map

\mathbb{Z}\to G_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}^{ab}

sends
1
to the element given by the (arithmetic) Frobenius automorphism
x\mapsto x^{p}
. By
5.11
the 2-groupoid of lifts to a refined class formation, inducing the above
\operatorname{inv}
, has:

(a)

\pi_{0}=\ast
;

(b)

\pi_{1}=\operatorname{Ext}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{Z})=\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}/\mathbb{Z}
;

(c)

\pi_{2}=\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{Z})=0.

Thus any two choices of refined class formation are isomorphic, but not uniquely: there is a
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}/\mathbb{Z}
-ambiguity in the choice of isomorphism (but no higher ambiguity).

Explicitly, suppose we choose an algebraic closure
\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}
. This determines an equivalence of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
with the Galois category of finite
\operatorname{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}}/\mathbb{F}_{p})
-sets, or an isomorphism of the Galois groupoid of
\mathbb{F}_{p}
with
B\operatorname{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}}/\mathbb{F}_{p})
. But we also have a canonical homomorphism
\mathbb{Z}\to\operatorname{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}}/\mathbb{F}_{p})
given by the powers of Frobenius, which gives an associated map of groupoids

B\mathbb{Z}\to\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}.

Under the equivalence of refined class formations with Weil groupoids described in the following section, this gives rise to a refined class formation which refines
(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}},\mathbb{Z})
.

From the perspective of the definition of a refined class formation, this same situation can be described as follows. Suppose again that we fix an
\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
, and let
\varphi
denote Frobenius. Then we get a refined class formation given by a cosheaf
F
on the finite etale site of
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{F}_{p})
defined as follows: we send
X\in\operatorname{fEt}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
to the object in
\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})
represented by the complex (in homological degrees
1
and
0
) given by

\mathbb{Z}[X(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}})]\overset{\varphi-1}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{Z}[X(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}})],

with the augmentation
\alpha:F\to\mathbb{Z}
being induced by the obvious augmentation on the
0^{th}
term of the above complex. The isomorphism
H_{1}F\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{tr}
(denoting by
\mathbb{Z}
the constant sheaf) is given by the diagonal inclusion of
\mathbb{Z}
in the first term in the above complex.

An isomorphism between two algebraic closures induces an isomorphism of refined class formations, inducing the identity on underlying class formations; this map from isomorphisms of algebraic closures to isomorphisms of honest class formations is surjective, and two isomorphisms induce (canonically) equivalent isomorphisms of refined class formations if and only if they differ by a power of Frobenius. This explains the
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}/\mathbb{Z}
-ambiguity.

It is unclear to the author whether one can expect a canonical choice of refined class formation in this setting, or equivalently, if one can expect a canonical choice of algebraic closure of
\mathbb{F}_{p}
“up to powers of Frobenius”. (It is also unclear to the author how to make the word “canonical” precise, so as to turn this into a well-defined mathematical question.)

5.

Suppose now
k
is a general finite field, of characteristic
p
. Then
k/\mathbb{F}_{p}
in a unique manner. Thus if we fix a refined class formation for
\mathbb{F}_{p}
(e.g. by fixing an algebraic closure, as above), then from Example
5.12
we get in a canonical way an induced refined class formation for
k
(even if we don’t embed
k
into our choice of
\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
), simply by restriction on the level of cosheaves.

6.

Now let
K
be a nonarchimedean local field, with residue field
k
, a finite field. By local class field theory, there is a canonical condensed class formation given by
(\mathcal{C}_{K},\mathbb{G}_{m})
where
\mathcal{C}_{K}
is the Galois category of finite etale
K
-schemes and
\mathbb{G}_{m}
is defined as usual by

\mathbb{G}_{m}(L)=L^{\times}

for a finite separable extension
L/K
. Moreover, there is a unique isomorphism

\operatorname{inv}:H^{2}(\mathcal{C}_{K};\mathbb{G}_{m})\simeq\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}

such that the associated Artin map

K^{\times}\to G_{K}^{ab}

sends every uniformizer to some Galois element inducing (arithmetic) Frobenius on each unramified extension. The 2-groupoid of lifts to a condensed refined class formation inducing the above
\operatorname{inv}
has:

(a)

\pi_{0}=\ast
;

(b)

\pi_{1}=\operatorname{Ext}(\mathbb{Q},K^{\times})=\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}/\mathbb{Z}
;

(c)

\pi_{2}=\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{Q},K^{\times})=0
.

Note that this is exactly the same ambiguity as in the finite field case. This can be explained as follows. Suppose we choose a refined class formation inducing
(\mathcal{C}_{K},\mathbb{G}_{m},\operatorname{inv})
. Note that we have a fully faithful embedding
\mathcal{C}_{k}\subset\mathcal{C}_{K}
giving the unramified extensions, and the norm-1 subgroup
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{1}
is cohomologically trivial on
\mathcal{C}_{k}
, with quotient
\mathbb{G}_{m}/\mathbb{G}_{m}^{1}\simeq\mathbb{Z}
via the normalized valuation (uniformizer
\mapsto 1
). Applying Example
5.12
, we get an associated refined class formation on
\mathcal{C}_{k}
with underlying sheaf
\mathbb{Z}
. This induces an equivalence of 2-groupoids between the refinements of class field theory for
K
and for
k
. In other words, if we fix our refined class formation for
k
as above, we canonically get an induced refined class formation for
K
as well.

This is more clear from the perspective of Weil groupoids: a choice of Weil groupoid
\mathcal{W}_{k}\to\mathcal{G}_{k}
for
k
canonically induces one for
K
by taking
\mathcal{W}_{K}:=\mathcal{W}_{k}\times_{\mathcal{G}_{k}}\mathcal{G}_{K}
. But we can also see it from the perspective of refined class formations. To take the most basic example of
K=\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, suppose we fix an
\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
as in the discussion of finite fields. Then we can write down a refined class formation for
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
as follows: It sends a finite extension
K
of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
to the two-term complex of abelian groups

(K\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}W(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}}))^{\times}\overset{\varphi-1}{\longrightarrow}(K\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}W(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}}))^{\times},

with functoriality given by norm maps. The augmentation to
\mathbb{Z}
is given by taking
p
-adic valuation on the term in degree
0
(for
K=\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, then in general by composing with the norm functoriality). The isomorphism with
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{tr}
in degree
1
is induced by the obvious inclusion
K^{\times}\subset(K\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}W(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}}))^{\times}
.

Note that if we fix the refined class formations on finite fields via the above-discussed procedure of first fixing them for
\mathbb{F}_{p}
then inducing them on any finite field, then fix them on nonarchimedean local fields by compatibility with the finite field case using unramified extensions as above, then we automatically get compatibility under extensions of nonarchimedean local fields; this is again most clear from the Weil groupoid perspective.

Yet another perspective on refinements of local class field theory for non-archimedean local fields is as follows. Recall from Theorem
5.10
that refining
(\mathcal{C}_{K},\mathbb{G}_{m},\operatorname{inv})
is equivalent to giving a map in
\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})

\mathbb{Q}\to R\Gamma(\mathcal{C}_{K};\mathbb{G}_{m}[2])

which induces
\operatorname{inv}
in degree
0
. This data can be reinterpreted as that of an pro-etale
\widetilde{\mathbb{G}_{m}}
-gerbe over
\operatorname{Spec}(F)
where
\widetilde{\mathbb{G}_{m}}=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{G}_{m})
, or equivalently (see
[
47
]
) as that of an
K
-linear
\mathbb{Q}
-graded Tannakian category
\mathcal{T}
, such that
\mathcal{T}
is pro-etale locally isomorphic to the
\mathbb{Q}
-graded Tannakian category of
\mathbb{Q}
-graded vector spaces. In these terms, if we fix an algebraic closure of
k
with induced maximal unramified extension
K^{nr}
of
K
, then the
\mathbb{Q}
-graded Tannakian category corresponding to the above-described refined class formation is the usual isocrystal-type category, also known as the local Kottwitz category, c.f.
[
34
]
,
[
31
]
: for
K=\mathbb{Q}_{p}
it is the
\otimes
-category of finite dimensional
W(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}})[\frac{1}{p}]
-vector spaces equipped with a
\varphi
-semilinear automorphism
\sigma
, with the
\mathbb{Q}
-grading coming from the Dieudonne-Manin classification: for
\lambda=\frac{r}{s}\in\mathbb{Q}
written as a reduced fraction with
s>0
, the
\lambda
-graded piece is the full subcategory of objects with
\sigma^{s}=p^{r}
.

7.

Now consider archimedean local fields. Again, it suffices to consider
K=\mathbb{R}
, by passing to finite extensions. The class formation from local class field theory for
K=\mathbb{R}
can be described exactly as for nonarchimedean local fields:
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{R}}
is the Galois category of finite etale
\mathbb{R}
-schemes, and the sheaf on
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{R}}
is given by
\mathbb{G}_{m}
. Here there is a unique

\operatorname{inv}:H^{2}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{R}};\mathbb{G}_{m})\simeq\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}\right)/\mathbb{Z},

as there is a unique isomorphism between any two groups of order two. The 2-groupoid of refinements of this class formation has:

(a)

\pi_{0}=\ast
;

(b)

\pi_{1}=\operatorname{Ext}(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z};\mathbb{R}^{\times})=0
;

(c)

\pi_{2}=\operatorname{Hom}(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z};\mathbb{R}^{\times})=\mathbb{R}^{\times}
.

Again using the trick of passing to
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{1}
using
\mathbb{R}_{>0}\oplus\mathbb{G}_{m}^{1}=\mathbb{G}_{m}
as in the case
F=\mathbb{Q}
above, we can reduce the
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
-ambiguity to a
\pm 1
-ambiguity. Thus we get a 2-groupoid of refinements of class field theory over
\mathbb{R}
which is equivalent to
B^{2}\pm 1
: any two objects are isomorphic via an isomorphism which is unique up to homotopy, but that homotopy between isomorphisms is only unique up to sign.

Another way to understand this ambiguity is as follows. Suppose we fix a choice of nontrivial division algeabra
\mathbb{H}
over
\mathbb{R}
. Then we get an induced Weil groupoid (hence refined class formation, as we will see in the next section) as follows: the objects are the pairs
(\mathbf{C},\alpha)
where
\mathbf{C}
is an algebraic closure of
\mathbb{R}
and
\alpha
is a Morita equivalence
\mathbb{H}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbf{C}\sim\mathbf{C}
(i.e. a
\mathbf{C}
-linear equivalence of categories of modules); morphisms are obvious. Clearly this groupoid only depends on the Morita class of
\mathbb{H}
, and via this we see that giving a refined class formation for
\mathbb{R}
is equivalent to giving a representative for the non-trivial Morita class over
\mathbb{R}
. To account for the reduction to
\pm 1
, we can incorporate Hermitian data in the categories.

Again there is a (closely related) Tannakian perspective: the data of a refinement of class field theory over
\mathbb{R}
is equivalent to the data of a
\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
-graded Tannakian category over
\mathbb{R}
which is locally but not globally isomorphic to
\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
-graded vector spaces. As in the non-archimedean case, there is an isocrystal-style description of such a Tannakian category, provided we fix an algebraic closure
\mathbb{C}
of
\mathbb{R}
. Namely it is the category of
\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
-graded
\mathbb{C}
-vector spaces
V
equipped with a semi-linear automorphism
\sigma
such that
\sigma^{2}=(-1)^{2\lambda}
in graded degree
\lambda
. Again this is also known as the local Kottwitz category for
\mathbb{R}
, see
[
34
]
,
[
31
]
.

8.

Finally, there is the case of function fields
K
over finite fields (and their “restricted ramification” variants). This is not our focus so we do not go into details, but the situation is basically the same as that of nonarchimedean local fields: the ambiguity in choice of refined class formation for
K
reduces to that for its finite field of constants
k
.

One thing missing from the above discussion of examples is the quite nontrivial question of local-global compatibility in the number field case (in the function field case it is trivial). To address this we need to explain the notion of a morphism of refined class formations.

Definition 5.17
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},F,\alpha)
and
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},F^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
be two refined class formations (condensed or not: the discussion is the same in either case). A morphism

f:(\mathcal{C},F,\alpha)\to(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},F^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})

consists of the following data:

1.

An exact functor
f^{-1}:\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{C}
;

2.

A natural transformation
f_{\natural}:f_{\ast}F\to F^{\prime}
;

subject to the condition that
\alpha^{\prime}\circ f_{\natural}
should agree with the map induced on
f_{\ast}
by
\alpha:F\to\mathbb{Z}
.

Here
f_{\ast}G:=G\circ f^{-1}
for any functor
G
from
\mathcal{C}
to some target
\infty
-category; note in particular that
f_{\ast}
sends (co)sheaves to (co)sheaves, and sends constant functors to constant functors. (It also commutes with the
(-)^{tr}
operation from Definition
5.6
, since
f^{-1}
, being exact, also induces an additive functor on span categories.)

Remark 5.18
.

An obvious elaboration of this definition makes refined class formations into a
(2,1)
-category.

Recall from Theorem
5.10
that a refined class formation
(\mathcal{C},F,\alpha)
can be equivalently encoded as the data
(\mathcal{C},A,\kappa)
of a class formation plus a map

\kappa:\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z}\to R\Gamma(\mathcal{C};A[2]),

and similarly
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},F^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
can be encoded as
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},A^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
. We can analyze what it means, in these terms, to give a map of refined class formations, by passing to
(-)^{tr}
and considering the sheafified Postnikov tower, as in the proof of Theorem
5.10
. The result is the following: to give a map
f:(\mathcal{C},A,\kappa)\to(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},A^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
amounts to giving the following:

1.

An exact functor
f^{-1}:\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{C}
;

2.

A map
f_{\natural}:f_{\ast}A\to A^{\prime}
of sheaves on
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
;

3.

A homotopy between two specific maps

\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}:\frac{1}{\#\operatorname{im}(f)}\mathbb{Z}\to\varprojlim_{(x,U)}R\Gamma(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{/U};A^{\prime}[2]).

which are canonically defined in terms of the given data.

Here
(x,U)\mapsto U
is the filtered inverse system of connected objects of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
parametrized by pairs
(x,U)
with
U
a connected object of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and
x:\ast\to f^{-1}U
is a monomorphism, the transition maps in the inverse system on the target of our maps
\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}
are given by transfer maps, and
\operatorname{im}(f)
is the Galois category
\varinjlim_{(x,U)}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{/U}
where the transition functors are given by base-change. (Under Grothendieck Galois theory, this corresponds to the image of the continuous homomorphism of profinite groups corresponding to
f^{-1}
.)

The map
\varphi^{\prime}
is gotten as follows: note that
\kappa^{\prime}:\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}\mathbb{Z}\to R\Gamma(\mathcal{C}^{\prime};A^{\prime}[2])
naturally extends to a map of filtered inverse systems parametrized by
(x,U)
, where the source is
\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{/U}}\mathbb{Z}
with transition maps the inclusions and the target is
R\Gamma(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{/U};A^{\prime}[2])
with transition maps the transfer maps; then we pass to the inverse limits to get

\varphi^{\prime}:\frac{1}{\#\operatorname{im}(f)}\mathbb{Z}\to\varprojlim_{(x,U)}R\Gamma(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{/U};A^{\prime}[2]).

The map
\varphi
is gotten as follows: we can embed
\operatorname{im}(f)
as a full Galois subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
via the functor induced by
f^{-1}
; then as in Example
5.12
, the sheaf
A
can be resricted to
\operatorname{im}(f)
, and
\kappa
induces

\frac{1}{\#\operatorname{im}(f)}\mathbb{Z}\to R\Gamma(\operatorname{im}(f);A[2]),

but on the other hand
f_{\natural}
from 2 induces a natural map

R\Gamma(\operatorname{im}(f);A[2])\to\varprojlim_{(x,U)}R\Gamma(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{/U};A^{\prime}[2]),

and by composing these two maps we get
\varphi
.

Remark 5.19
.

Exactly as in
5.15
, morphisms of condensed refined class formations are the same as morphisms of underlying abstract refined class formations together with a promotion of
f_{\natural}
to a map of sheaves of condensed abelian groups.

From this discussion we conclude the following.

Corollary 5.20
.

Suppose given two refined class formations, encoded in data
(\mathcal{C},A,\kappa)
and
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},A^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
as in Theorem
5.10
. Suppose also given an exact functor
f^{-1}:\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{C}
and a map of sheaves
f_{\natural}:f_{\ast}A\to A^{\prime}
. Finally, suppose the following, with notations as above:

1.

R^{1}\varprojlim_{(x,U)}A^{\prime}(U)=0
;

2.

R^{2}\varprojlim_{(x,U)}A^{\prime}(U)=0.

Then there exists a morphism of refined class formations
(\mathcal{C},A,\kappa)\to(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},A^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
inducing the given data if and only if the associated invariant maps

\operatorname{inv}:H^{2}(\mathcal{C};A)\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z},\text{ }\operatorname{inv}^{\prime}:H^{2}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime};A^{\prime})\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}

agree when restricted to
H^{2}(\operatorname{im}(f);A)
(which naturally maps to both of the above
H^{2}
groups, via respectively inflation and the map on cohomology induced by
f_{\natural}
.)

If this condition is satisfied, then the groupoid of maps of refined class formations inducing the given data has:

1.

\pi_{0}\simeq\operatorname{Ext}\left(\frac{1}{\#\operatorname{im}(f)}\mathbb{Z},\varprojlim_{(x,U)}A^{\prime}(U)\right);

2.

\pi_{1}\simeq\operatorname{Hom}\left(\frac{1}{\#\operatorname{im}(f)}\mathbb{Z},\varprojlim_{(x,U)}A^{\prime}(U)\right)
.

Let us now apply this to the question of local-global compatibility. Fix the canonical refined class formation
(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Q}},F_{\mathbb{Q}},\alpha_{\mathbb{Q}})
for global class field theory for number fields form Example
5.16
. Fix also some choice of refined class formation for
\mathbb{F}_{p}
(e.g. by choosing an
\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
) and use this to get an induced refined class formation
(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}},F_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}},\alpha_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})
for
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
as in Example
5.16
. We have the obvious exact functor

f^{-1}:\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}

from the finite etale site of
\mathbb{Q}
to that of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, induced by base-change, and we have the obvious map of sheaves of condensed abelian groups

f_{\natural}:f_{\ast}A_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\to A_{\mathbb{Q}}

given by the inclusion of the
p
-adic components of the ideles followed by projection to the idele class group. Note that in this case
\operatorname{im}(f)
identifies with
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
(classically,
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\hookrightarrow G_{\mathbb{Q}}
after fixing an algebraic closure of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
); moreover the relevant pro-system of
(x,U)
as above identifies with the pro-system of pairs
(\nu,\operatorname{Spec}(K))
where
K
is a number field and
\nu
is a place of
K
such that
K_{\nu}\simeq\mathbb{Q}_{p}
(such an isomorphism being unique if it exists).

Classical local-global compatibility in cohomological class field theory shows that the global
\operatorname{inv}
restricts to the local
\operatorname{inv}
under this map
f_{\natural}
. Moreover we have the required
R^{i}\operatorname{lim}
vanishing because idele class groups are almost compact with non-compact part equal to
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, and the vanishing is automatic in the compact case by Lemma
2.13
. Thus we can apply the above analysis to conclude that the groupoid of maps of condensed refined class formations
(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}},F_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}},\alpha_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})\to(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Q}},F_{\mathbb{Q}},\alpha_{\mathbb{Q}})
inducing the above
(f^{-1},f_{\natural})
on underlying class formations has:

1.

\pi_{0}\simeq\operatorname{Ext}(\mathbb{Q},\varprojlim_{(\nu,K)}\mathbb{A}_{K}^{\times}/F^{\times})=\ast
;

2.

\pi_{1}\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{Q},\varprojlim_{(\nu,K)}\mathbb{A}_{K}^{\times}/K^{\times})
.

Thus there is a map of refined class formations witnessing local-global compatibility, and such a map is unique up to homotopy, but such a homotopy is highly non-unique (coming from the connected components of the identity in the idele class groups).

Similarly, after fixing a condensed refined class formation
(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{R}},F_{\mathbb{R}},\alpha_{\mathbb{R}})
we can analyze the groupoid of maps

(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{R}},F_{\mathbb{R}},\alpha_{\mathbb{R}})\to(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Q}},F_{\mathbb{Q}},\alpha_{\mathbb{Q}}).

inducing the obvious map of underlying class formations exactly as in the
p
-adic case. It has

1.

\pi_{0}\simeq\operatorname{Ext}(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z},\varprojlim_{(\nu,K)}\mathbb{A}_{K}^{\times}/K^{\times})=\ast
;

2.

\pi_{1}\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z},\varprojlim_{(\nu,K)}\mathbb{A}_{K}^{\times}/K^{\times}).

Here the limit is taken along norm maps and runs over number fields
K
equipped with a real place
\nu
. Thus, just as in the case of
p
-adic local-global compatibility, there is a choice which is unique up to a highly non-unique homotopy.

Remark 5.21
.

For each prime
p
, let us fix such a map of condensed refined class formations

f_{p}:(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}},F_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}},\alpha_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})\to(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Q}},F_{\mathbb{Q}},\alpha_{\mathbb{Q}})

given by a map
(f_{p})_{\natural}:(f_{p})_{\ast}F_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\to F_{\mathbb{Q}}
in
\operatorname{Fun}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Q}},\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{CondAb}))
, and similarly let us fix

f_{\infty}:(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{R}},F_{\mathbb{R}},\alpha_{\mathbb{R}})\to(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Q}},F_{\mathbb{Q}},\alpha_{\mathbb{Q}}).

Then for any number field
K
and any place
\nu
of
K
, we get an induced map of condensed refined class formations

f_{\nu}:(\mathcal{C}_{K_{\nu}},F_{K_{\nu}},\alpha_{F_{\nu}})\to(\mathcal{C}_{K},F_{K},\alpha_{K})

simply by restriction, and there are the evident compatibilities between these local-global maps as
K
varies, again just by restriction. Moreover, there is another compatibility, that we can produce “for free”: if
p
is a prime, we have on the one hand the refined class formation

(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}},F_{\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}},\alpha_{\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}})

for number fields unramified over
p
, and on the other hand the refined class formation

(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}},F_{\mathbb{F}_{p}},\alpha_{\mathbb{F}_{p}})

for
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, both discused in Example
5.16
. Then using our fixed
f_{p}
we can make a map of refined class formations

(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}},F_{\mathbb{F}_{p}},\alpha_{\mathbb{F}_{p}})\to(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}},F_{\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}},\alpha_{\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}}):

namely, we restrict
(f_{p})_{\natural}
from
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Q}}
to
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}}
then take the cone of the natural map from the norm 1 subgroup of the local unit group shifted by 1. From this construction we tautologically get a commutative square of (condensed) refined class formations comparing this map to
f_{p}
. There is a similar situation for any nonarchimedean place of any number field, again obtained by restriction.

Remark 5.22
.

We take all the data fixed as above: refined class formations for both local and global fields, together with the local-global maps. Taking the direct sum over all places of
\mathbb{Q}
, we deduce a map of
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{CondAb})
-valued cosheaves

(f_{\infty})_{\ast}F_{\mathbb{R}}\oplus\bigoplus_{p}(f_{p})_{\ast}F_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\to F_{\mathbb{Q}}.

Note that the source satisfies that on
H_{1}
it gives the co-presheaf sending a number field
K
to
\oplus_{\nu}K_{\nu}^{\times}
(with morphisms given by the transfer/norm maps). On
H_{1}
, the above map factors as

\oplus_{\nu}K_{\nu}^{\times}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}_{K}^{\times}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{A}_{K}^{\times}/K^{\times}.

Pushing out via the first inclusion, we produce from
(f_{\infty})_{\ast}F_{\mathbb{R}}\oplus\bigoplus_{p}(f_{p})_{\ast}F_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
a new co-presheaf
F_{\mathbb{A}}
with
H_{0}=\mathbb{Z}
and
H_{1}
given by the idele groups (with norm map functoriality), such that we can uniquely extend the above map of cosheaves to a map

F_{\mathbb{A}}\to F_{\mathbb{Q}}

of co-presheaves inducing the above factorization on
H_{1}
. In fact
F_{\mathbb{A}}
is also a cosheaf, because the quotient
K\mapsto\mathbb{A}_{K}^{\times}/\oplus_{\nu}K_{\nu}^{\times}
is easily seen to be cohomologically trivial. Thus the fiber
F_{\operatorname{Kott}}
of this map
F_{\mathbb{A}}\to F_{\mathbb{Q}}
is a cosheaf, and we can apply
(-)^{tr}
to it (Defintion
5.6
) to get a
\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})
-valued sheaf
(F_{\operatorname{Kott}})^{tr}
on
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Q}}
with the following sheafified homology groups:

H^{shf}_{0}(F_{\operatorname{Kott}}^{tr})(K)=\operatorname{ker}\left(\bigoplus_{\nu\in S_{f}(K)}\mathbb{Q}\oplus\bigoplus_{\nu\in S_{\infty}(K)}\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Q}\right)

H_{1}(F_{\operatorname{Kott}}^{tr})=\mathbb{G}_{m}.

Here
K
is a number field and
S_{f}(K)
(resp.
S_{\infty}(K)
) is the set of non-archimedean (resp. archimedean) places of
K
. Note that
H^{shf}_{0}(F_{\operatorname{Kott}}^{tr})
is a sheaf of torsionfree abelian groups on
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Q}}
, hence there is a canonically determined pro-torus over
\mathbb{Q}
with character group
H^{shf}_{0}(F_{\operatorname{Kott}}^{tr})
, call it
\mathbb{D}_{\mathbb{Q}}
. The datum of a sheaf
F^{tr}_{Kott}
equipped with the above identification of homology groups is equivalent to the datum of a pro-etale
\mathbb{D}_{\mathbb{Q}}
-gerbe over
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Q})
. This matches the gerbe constructed by Kottwitz in
[
34
]
, by direct comparison.

We already saw above that the datum of the local Kottwitz gerbes is equivalent to the datum of refined local class formations (or equivalently local Weil groupoids); now we see that the datum of global Kottwitz gerbes does
not
correspond directly to that of refined global class formations (or global Weil groupoids): rather, the data of global Kottwitz gerbes plus compatibility with the local Kottwitz gerbes is equivalent to the data of global refined class formation plus compatibility with the local refined class formations. In the above presentation, the local data is contained in
F_{\mathbb{A}}
, the global Kottwitz data together with compatibility is encoded in the map
F_{Kott}\to F_{\mathbb{A}}
, the global Weil data together with compatibility is encoded in the map
F_{\mathbb{A}}\to F_{\mathbb{Q}}
, and the equivalence between the Kottwitz and Weil data in total is an instance of the axiom of a stable
\infty
-category that cofiber and fiber sequences are the same thing. Of course in some sense this all goes back to Nakayama-Tate,
[
64
]
. For a more explicit cocycle-based discussion of the same situation, see also
[
54
]
.

Remark 5.23
.

It causes some philosophical discomfort that the above data appears to not be canonical. It’s also annoying that the “construction” of this data is so indirect. Here is a clean statement which would automatically give all of the above structure. Let
\operatorname{NRing}
denote the full subcategory of locally compact Hausdorff commutative rings spanned by the rings commonly used in number theory: the global and local fields, their rings of (
S
-)integers, and the finite fields. Then all of the above-discussed structure (refined class field theory for all number-theoretic rings, plus local-global compatibility) amounts to the following data:

1.

A functor
F:\operatorname{NRing}^{op}\to\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})
with values concentrated in degrees
[0,1]
;

2.

An isomorphism
H_{0}F\simeq\mathbb{Z}
to the constant functor with value
\mathbb{Z}
;

3.

An isomorphism
H_{1}F(R)\simeq C_{R}
to the usual class group assigned to
R
, such that the functoriality in
R
gives the “usual” maps between class groups.

This functor
F
should satisfy the property that it gives a cosheaf on the finite etale site of any number ring, and that its restriction to such a finite etale site recovers “the” refined class formations as discussed above.

Wouldn’t it be nice if there were some direct construction of such a functor
F
? The article
[
13
]
goes part of the way towards this, by showing that
R\mapsto K_{1}(\operatorname{lc}_{R})
(the algebraic
K_{1}
-group of the category of locally compact Hausdorff
R
-modules) serves as the appropriate
H_{1}F
(see also
[
8
]
). However
K_{0}(\operatorname{lc}_{R})
is far from being constant with value
\mathbb{Z}
, so a serious modification of this idea is required to realize the above hope.

Possibly this is too much to expect, even just for finite fields. But for number fields there
is
a posteriori a canonical such functor by Example
5.16
, so it makes sense to ask to describe it directly, without resorting to cohomological analysis. This is surely a difficult problem, but one could start just with cyclotomic fields where special features may be helpful.

Remark 5.24
.

For those who prefer sheaves to cosheaves, it may make sense to remember the condensed structure on
F
and pass to the Pontryagin dual. Then you’re looking for a derived sheaf with
H_{0}=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
and
H_{-1}
the Pontryagin dual of the class group. Note that this derived sheaf is almost discrete. Actually, in the number field setting, if we use the compact form of the class groups and take the fiber of the map from
\mathbb{R}
in degree
0
, we see that the data we’re looking for is just a derived sheaf with
\mathbb{Z}
in degree
1
and the (discrete) dual to the norm-1 idele class group in degree
-1
: entirely discrete, but it completely recovers global class field theory.

6
From refined class formations to Weil groupoids

Now we turn to the problem of assigning Weil groupoids to refined class formations, essentially redoing the analysis in
[
3
]
from a slightly different perspective. First let’s discuss a more basic instance of the same idea: associating Galois groupoids to Galois categories. We know from Grothendieck Galois theory that that the
2
-category of Galois categories (objects: Galois categories; 1-morphisms: exact functors; 2-morphisms: natural transformations) is a
(2,1)
-category which identifies with the opposite of the
(2,1)
-category whose objects are the profinite groups
G
, whose morphisms are the continuous homomorphisms
G\to G^{\prime}
, and whose set of
2
-morphisms

\varphi_{0}\simeq\varphi_{1}:G\to G^{\prime}

between two such homomorphisms is the set of
g^{\prime}\in G^{\prime}
such that
\varphi_{1}=g^{\prime}\varphi_{0}(g^{\prime})^{-1}
. This same
2
-category can also be realized as the full subcategory of
\operatorname{CondAn}
spanned by the objects of the form
BG
where
G
is a profinite group.

Our first order of business is to give a more direct description of this fully faithful embedding. This is based on the following standard definition.

Definition 6.1
.

A map of condensed anima
f:X\to Y
is
finite etale
if, locally on
Y
in the condensed Grothendieck topology, it is isomorphic to a map of the form
I\times Y\to Y
where
I
is a finite set.

Remark 6.2
.

It follows from descent theory that finite etale maps
X\to Y
in
\operatorname{CondAn}
are classified by maps

Y\to\operatorname{fSet}^{\simeq}\simeq\bigsqcup_{n\geq 0}\ast/\Sigma_{n}

in
\operatorname{CondAn}
.

Example 6.3
.

1.

Suppose
S
is an extremally disconnected profinite set. By definition every cover of
S
in the condensed Grothendieck topology is refined by a finite disjoint union decomposition of
S
. It follows that the finite etale maps
X\to S
are exactly the finite disjoint unions of clopen subsets of
S
.

2.

Suppose
S
is a compact Hausdorff space. By non-abelian cohomological descent applied to a hypercover by extremally disconnected profinite sets, we deduce that maps
S\to BG
in
\operatorname{CondAn}
for any group
G

are the same as global sections
\Gamma(S;BG)
in the sense of non-abelian sheaf cohomology. It follows that every finite etale cover of
S
is locally trivial in the open cover topology of
S
, and hence that finite etale maps to
S
correspond to finite-sheeted covering spaces in the usual sense from point-set topology.

3.

Suppose
G
is a locally compact Hausdroff group. Then finite etale maps to
BG
identify with finite sets with continuous
G
-action. This is obvious by descent along
\ast\to BG
.

4.

For a general condensed anima
S
, every finite etale map
X\to S
uniquely descends to the Postnikov truncation
\tau_{\leq 1}S
.

For
S\in\operatorname{CondAn}
, the full sub-
\infty
-category

\operatorname{fEt}_{S}\subset\operatorname{CondAn}_{/S}

is a
1
-category which admits all finite limits and colimits. (The above inclusion preserves finite limits; for colimits in general one has to apply the relative Postnikov truncation
\tau_{\leq 1}
to the colimit in
\operatorname{CondAn}_{/S}
, but for quotients by finite groups acting faithfully this last truncation is unnecessary.) If
T\to S
is a map, then pullback induces a functor

\operatorname{fEt}_{S}\to\operatorname{fEt}_{T}

which is exact, i.e. preserves all finite limits and colimits. This category
\operatorname{fEt}_{S}
satisfies all the same exactness properties as the category of finite sets, if these are expressed in terms of interactions between finite limits and finite colimits. All of this is general topos-theory nonsense, using descent and the fact that base-change functors preserve finite limits, all colimits, and relative Postnikov truncations (which can be built in terms of finite limits and geometric realizations).

The only thing preventing
\operatorname{fEt}_{S}
from being a Galois category is the potential lack of fiber functor. In other words,
\operatorname{fEt}_{S}
is the more general kind of Galois-type category, which corresponds to a profinite groupoid not necessarily having a unique isomorphism class of objects. We will not need to go into the details of this; we simply make the following defintion.

Definition 6.4
.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}
is a small category admitting all finite limits and colimits. Define a condensed anima
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
by sending an extremally disconnected
S
to the groupoid of exact functors

\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{fEt}_{S}.

Remark 6.5
.

By descent, we actually get a description of the whole functor of points of
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
: for an arbitrary
S\in\operatorname{CondAn}
we have

\underline{\mathcal{C}}(S)=\operatorname{Fun}^{ex}(\mathcal{C},\operatorname{fEt}_{S}).

Proposition 6.6
.

The above functor restricted to those
\mathcal{C}
which are Galois categories,

\mathcal{C}\mapsto\underline{\mathcal{C}}:\operatorname{GalCat}^{op}\to\operatorname{CondAn},

has the following properties:

1.

It is fully faithful.

2.

For a profinite group
\Gamma
, its value on the Galois category of finite continuous
\Gamma
-sets identifies with
B\Gamma
.

3.

On the essential image of this functor (which is the full subcategory of
\operatorname{CondAn}
spanned by the above
B\Gamma
) the inverse functor is given by sending
S\mapsto\operatorname{fEt}_{/S}
.

Proof.

It is tautological from descent that the functor
\mathcal{C}\mapsto\underline{\mathcal{C}}
admits the left adjoint

S\mapsto\operatorname{fEt}_{S}.

In particular, for a small category with finite limits and colimits
\mathcal{C}
we have an obvious exact comparison functor

\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{fEt}_{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}

and for a condensed anima
X
we have an obvious map

X\to\underline{\operatorname{fEt}_{X}}.

Consider now the case of the Galois cateogry
\mathcal{C}_{\Gamma}
of fintie continuous
\Gamma
-sets for a profinite group
\Gamma
. As discussed above we have
\operatorname{fEt}_{B\Gamma}\simeq\mathcal{C}_{\Gamma}
, which gives a natural comparison functor

B\Gamma\to\underline{\mathcal{C}_{\Gamma}},

and to prove all the claims it suffices to show this is an isomorphism for all
\Gamma
.
Note that both functors
\Gamma\mapsto B\Gamma
and
\Gamma\mapsto\underline{\mathcal{C}_{\Gamma}}
commute with filtered inverse limits. For the latter this is clear, and for the former see Lemma
2.14
. Thus we can reduce to the case where
\Gamma
is a finite group, where we need to see that giving a
\Gamma
-torsor over a profinite set
S
is the same as giving an exact functor from finite
\Gamma
-sets to
\operatorname{fEt}_{/S}
. This is standard Galois theory.
∎

Thus we have our direct description of the fully faithful contravariant embedding of Galois categories into condensed anima. Now we add refined class formations into the picture. To do this, it will be convenient to “de-linearize” refined class formations using the following.

Lemma 6.7
.

Consider the
\infty
-category
\mathcal{A}
of pairs
(M,\alpha)
where
M\in\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{CondAb})
lives in degrees
[0,1]
and
\alpha:H_{0}(M)\simeq\mathbb{Z}
. Then:

1.

There is a fully faithful embedding
\mathcal{A}\to\operatorname{CondAn}
, sending

(M,\alpha)\mapsto(\Omega^{\infty}M)\times_{\mathbb{Z}}\{1\},

where the map
\Omega^{\infty}M\to\mathbb{Z}
is induced by
\alpha.

2.

The essential image of this functor consists of the
abelian gerbes
: namely, those condensed anima isomorphic to
BA
for some condensed abelian group
A
.

3.

The inverse to this functor sends

X\mapsto(\tau_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{Z}[X]),\epsilon);

for
X
an abelian gerbe, where
\epsilon:\tau_{\leq 1}\mathbb{Z}[X]\to\mathbb{Z}
is induced by
X\to\ast
. More generally, for
X\in\operatorname{CondAn}
arbitrary and
(M,\alpha)\in\mathcal{A}
, we have that maps

(\tau_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{Z}[X]),\epsilon)\to(M,\alpha)

(in the obvious sense) are in natural equivalence with maps

X\to(\Omega^{\infty}M)\times_{\mathbb{Z}}\{1\}.

4.

For any
X\in\operatorname{CondAn}
with
\pi_{0}X=\ast
, there is an initial abelian gerbe
X^{ab}
with a map
X\to X^{ab}
, namely that given by

X^{ab}=(\tau_{\leq 1}\mathbb{Z}[X])\times_{\mathbb{Z}}\{1\}.

Proof.

In fact this works in any
\infty
-topos: one just has to say “locally isomorphic to some
BA
” as the definition of abelian gerbe in 2. (In
\operatorname{CondAn}
every cover of
\ast
splits so locally implies globally.)

Let’s first prove the last claim in 3. By adjunction, maps
X\to\Omega^{\infty}M
are the same as maps
\mathbb{Z}[X]\to M
. It is clear that the condition that the first map land in the
\alpha=1
-component is the same as the condition that the second map intertwine
\epsilon
and
\alpha
. This gives the last claim in 3.

If
(M,\alpha)\in\mathcal{A}
, then clearly

\pi_{0}(\Omega^{\infty}M)\times_{\mathbb{Z}}\{1\}=\ast

and after any choice of basepoint
x\in(\Omega^{\infty}M)\times_{\mathbb{Z}}\{1\}
we get, by subtracting
x
,

\pi_{1}(\Omega^{\infty}M\times_{\mathbb{Z}}\{1\},x)\simeq\pi_{1}(\Omega^{\infty}M,0)=H_{1}(M)\in\operatorname{CondAb},

and thus
\Omega^{\infty}\times_{\mathbb{Z}}\{1\}\simeq BH_{1}(M)
is an abelian gerbe as claimed. (The only thing that changes in a general
\infty
-topos is that it’s not true that
\pi_{0}=\ast
implies the existence of a basepoint: it only gives basepoints locally.)

Conversely,

BA\to\ast

induces the isomorphism

\epsilon:H_{0}(\mathbb{Z}[BA])\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\mathbb{Z}

as
\pi_{0}BA=\ast
, whereas

H_{1}(\mathbb{Z}[BA])\simeq A^{ab}=A

so that
(\tau_{\leq 1}\mathbb{Z}[BA],\epsilon)\in\mathcal{A}
. To finish the proofs of 1 and 2 we just need to see that the unit and counit are isomorphisms, i.e. that

BA\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}(\tau_{\leq 1}\mathbb{Z}[BA])\times_{\mathbb{Z}}\{1\}

and

\tau_{\leq 1}\mathbb{Z}[(\Omega^{\infty}M)\times_{\mathbb{Z}}\{1\})]\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}M,

but this is clear by checking on
\pi_{0}
and
\pi_{1}
in the first case and
H_{0}
and
H_{1}
in the second case. Finally, 4 follows immediately from 1,2,3.∎

From this we get the following.

Proposition 6.8
.

For a Galois category
\mathcal{C}
, the data
(F,\alpha)
of a condensed refined class formation on
\mathcal{C}
is equivalent to the datum of a functor

\mathcal{G}:\mathcal{C}_{conn}\to\operatorname{CondAn}

where
\mathcal{C}_{conn}\subset\mathcal{C}
is the full subcategory of connected objects, satisfying the following properties:

1.

\mathcal{G}
lands in the full subcategory of abelian gerbes;

2.

For all
X\in\mathcal{C}_{conn}
and all subgroups
G\subset\operatorname{Aut}(X)
, the map in
\operatorname{CondAn}

\mathcal{G}(X)_{G}\to\mathcal{G}(X/G)

induces an isomorphism on abelianization

(\mathcal{G}(X)_{G})^{ab}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{G}(X/G).

3.

For all
X\in\mathcal{C}_{conn}
and all subgroups
G\subset\operatorname{Aut}(X)
, the transfer map of condensed abelian groups

H_{1}\mathcal{G}(X/G)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\left(H_{1}\mathcal{G}(X)\right)^{G}

(defined by passing through Lemma
6.7
using 2) is an isomorphism.

In other words,
\mathcal{G}
is the datum of a cosheaf of abelian gerbes on
\mathcal{C}_{conn}
satisfying the extra condition about the transfer map being an isomorphism.

Moreover, given a condensed refined class formation
C
encoded in such data, and another
C^{\prime}
similarly encoded, then giving a map of condensed refined class formations
C\to C^{\prime}
(Definition
5.17
) is the same as giving an exact functor
f^{-1}:\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{C}
plus a natural transformation

f_{\natural}:f_{\ast}\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}.

Proof.

As we already reviewed in the proof of Lemma
5.5
, the datum of a cosheaf
F:\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{CondAb})
is the same as the datum of a functor (its restriction to connected objects)

F:\mathcal{C}_{conn}\to\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{CondAb})

such that
F(X)/G\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}F(X/G)
for all
(X,G)
as in 2. But by definition, when
F
is a refined class formation this restricted
F
together with its
\alpha
lands inside the full subcategory denoted
\mathcal{A}
in Lemma
6.7
, and so from Lemma
6.7
we get the the data of
(F,\alpha)
is the same as the data of
\mathcal{G}
. But we have to translate the conditions carefully: under Lemma
6.7
, the cosheaf condition on
\mathcal{G}
corresponds to saying that
F
is a cosheaf with values in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{CondAb})_{[0,1]}
, but the actual condition is that
F
be a cosheaf with values in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{CondAb})
which happens to take values in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{CondAb})_{[0,1]}
. We already know from the transfer formalism that this stronger condition on
F
implies the condition on the transfer maps being isomorphisms in 2. What remains is to show the converse, i.e. that if

F:\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{CondAb})

is a copresheaf taking values in degrees
[0,1]
such that for all
(X,G)
as in 2, the map

F(X)_{G}\to F(X/G)

induces

\tau_{\leq 1}(F(X)_{G})\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}F(X/G)

and that the transfer map

H_{1}(X/G)\to(H_{1}F(X))^{G}

(defined via the additive category
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{CondAb})_{[0,1]}
) is an isomorphism, we need to see that
F(X)_{G}
already lives in degrees
[0,1]
for all
(X,G)
.

For this, consider the diagram

F(X)_{G}\to F(X/G)\overset{tr}{\rightarrow}F(X)^{G}

where the composition is the transfer map for the
G
-action on
F(X)
. The assumptions imply that the composition is injective on
H_{0}
, an isomorphism on
H_{1}
, and surjective on
H_{2}
, and this is also so for any subgroup
H\subset G
. Thus, by Tate-Nakayama (applied to
S
-valued points, for all extremally disconnected
S
), the composition is an isomorphism. A fortiori
F(X)_{G}
lives in degrees
[0,1]
as desired.
∎

Note that if
S\in\operatorname{CondAn}
, then we have a tautological cosheaf of condensed anima on
\operatorname{fEt}_{S}
, namely the one sending

(T\to S)\mapsto T.

We denote this cosheaf by
\tau_{S}
. Then we make the following definition expressing how to “realize” refined class formations in condensed anima.

Definition 6.9
.

Let
C=(\mathcal{C},F,\alpha)
be a condensed refined class formation, and suppose
\mathcal{G}:\mathcal{C}_{conn}\to\operatorname{CondAn}
is the corresponding cosheaf of abelian gerbes, as in Proposition
6.8
. Define a condensed anima
\mathcal{W}_{C}
by setting, for
S
extremally disconnected profinite,
\mathcal{W}_{C}(S)
to be the anima of pairs
(f^{-1},f_{\natural})
where:

1.

f^{-1}
is an exact functor
\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{fEt}_{S}
;

2.

f_{\natural}
is a natural transformation
f_{\ast}\tau_{S}\to\mathcal{G}
, i.e. a family of maps of condensed anima

f^{-1}(U)\to\mathcal{G}(U)

natural in
U\in\mathcal{C}_{conn}
.

Remark 6.10
.

Again, just as in the discussion of Galois categories above, it follows from descent that
\mathcal{W}_{C}(S)
admits the same description for arbitrary
S\in\operatorname{CondAn}
.

Remark 6.11
.

Evidently, this condensed anima
\mathcal{W}_{C}
is 1-truncated (i.e. it is a condensed groupoid) and comes with a natural map

\mathcal{W}_{C}\to\underline{\mathcal{C}}

to the condensed groupoid
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
from Definition
6.4
.

Remark 6.12
.

If we wish to stick to the original perspective of
(F,\alpha)
and avoid abelian gerbes, then via Lemma
6.7
we can rephrase the datum
f_{\natural}
as follows: we give a map in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{CondAn})

\mathbb{Z}[f^{-1}(U)]\to F(U)

natural in
U
which intertwines the augmentation
\epsilon
on
\mathbb{Z}[f^{-1}U]
(induced by
f^{-1}U\to\ast
) with
\alpha:F\to\mathbb{Z}
. Here we can take
U\in\mathcal{C}_{conn}
or
U
arbitrary; it doesn’t matter by the fact the everything is a cosheaf, sending finite disjoint unions to direct sums.

To analyze this condensed groupoid
\mathcal{W}_{C}
, recall that
\mathcal{C}
can canonically be written as a filtered colimit of Galois subcategories, each of which is equivalent the Galois category associated to a finite group (or, canonically, a connected finite groupoid), namely

\mathcal{C}=\cup_{N}\mathcal{C}_{N}

where the indexing poset consists of all isomorphism classes of connected Galois objects of
\mathcal{C}
. From Example
5.12
, we can restrict any refined class formation to these subcategories
\mathcal{C}_{N}
, and the datum of a refined class formation on
\mathcal{C}
is equivalent to the data of compatible refined class formations on each
\mathcal{C}_{N}
. We deduce the following:

Lemma 6.13
.

Let
C
be a condensed refined class formation, and for
N
indexing a finite “quotient”
\mathcal{C}_{N}\subset\mathcal{C}
as above, let
C_{N}
denote the condensed refined class formation obtained by restricting the data to
\mathcal{C}_{N}
. Then we have

\mathcal{W}_{C}=\varprojlim_{N}\mathcal{W}_{C_{N}}

in
\operatorname{CondAn}
.

Proof.

This is immediate from
\mathcal{C}=\cup_{N}\mathcal{C}_{N}
.
∎

In this way the study of
C
reduces to the case where the Galois category
\mathcal{C}
corresponds to a connected finite groupoid, and to the study of filtered inverse limits. We start with the first.

In fact, the situation is quite clear in the abstract: a condensed refined class formation
C
on
\mathcal{C}\simeq\mathcal{C}_{\Gamma}
for a finite group
\Gamma
is the same as a certain kind of abelian gerbe over
B\Gamma
, or to say things more canonically, an abelian gerbe over
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
. Then the definitions are set up so that this equivalence is precisely implemented by
C\mapsto(\mathcal{W}_{C}\to\underline{\mathcal{C}})
. In other words:

Lemma 6.14
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a Galois category equivalent to finite
\Gamma
-sets for some finite group
\Gamma
, and suppose given a refined condensed class formation
C
on
\mathcal{C}
. Then the natural map of condensed groupoids

\mathcal{W}_{C}\to\underline{\mathcal{C}}

is an abelian gerbe, and the refined class formation is in turn recovered from this gerbe by

F(U)=\tau_{\leq 1}\mathbb{Z}[U\times_{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}\mathcal{W}_{C}]

for
U\in\operatorname{fEt}_{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}=\mathcal{C}
, equipped with the obvious augmentation
\alpha
induced by
U\times_{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}\mathcal{W}_{C}\to\ast
.

Thus, via this procedure, refined condensed class formations for
\mathcal{C}
are equivalent to certain abelian gerbes over
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
(namely those satisfying the conditions in Proposition
6.8
).

Proof.

Choose the isomorphism
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{\Gamma}
. Then the cosheaf of abelian gerbes on
\mathcal{C}
corresponding to
(F,\alpha)
via Proposition
6.8
is left Kan extended from its value on
\Gamma
as a
\Gamma
-set, and the datum is just an abelian gerbe
X\in\operatorname{CondAb}
equipped with a
\Gamma
-action. By the same token an
S
-valued point of
\mathcal{W}_{C}
amounts to a
\Gamma
-torsor
\widetilde{S}\to S
together with a
\Gamma
-equivariant map
\widetilde{S}\to X
. This exactly means that
\underline{C}=X/\Gamma
mapping to
\underline{\mathcal{C}}=B\Gamma
, giving the claims.
∎

Remark 6.15
.

In more pedestrian terms, if we choose a basepoint of
\mathcal{W}_{C}
, which induces a basepoint of
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
hence an isomorphism
\underline{\mathcal{C}}\simeq B\Gamma
for a uniquely determined finite group
\Gamma
, then we get
\mathcal{W}_{C}\simeq BW
where
W
is a condensed group which is an extension of
\Gamma
with abelian kernel
A
:

1\to A\to W\to\Gamma\to 1.

The condensed abelian group
A
with its induced conjugation action of
\Gamma
describes the condensed class formation underlying
C
, and the extra datum

\kappa:\frac{1}{\#\Gamma}\mathbb{Z}\to R\Gamma(B\Gamma;A[2])

is described as follows: its value on
\frac{1}{\#\Gamma}
classifies the given abelian extension
A\to W\to\Gamma
.

Remark 6.16
.

In this situation, if
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is a Galois full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
and we take the induced refined condensed class formation
C^{\prime}
on
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
as in Example
5.12
given just by restriction, then we get the induced commutative square of condensed anima

\textstyle{\mathcal{W}_{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{W}_{C^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathcal{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}.}

By Proposition
6.8
, the abelian gerbe
\mathcal{W}_{C^{\prime}}\to\underline{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}
is determined as the abelianization of the composite map
\mathcal{W}_{C}\to\underline{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}
.

Note that the topos-theoretic notion of abelianization commutes with base change. Thus we get the following more concrete description of the situation. If
1\to A\to W\to\Gamma\to 1
is the group extension corresponding to a choice of basepoint of
\mathcal{W}_{C}
, and
\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
is the quotient of
\Gamma
describing
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{C}
, then the right-hand map corresponds to

W^{\prime}\to\Gamma^{\prime},

where
W^{\prime}=W/[N,N]
with
N=\operatorname{ker}(W\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma^{\prime})
, and
[N,N]
denotes the commutator subgroup of
N
. In particular the kernel of
W^{\prime}\to\Gamma^{\prime}
, which describes the band of the gerbe, is
N^{ab}
. On the other hand from the class formation perspective we see that the band is also
A^{K}
, where
K=\operatorname{ker}(\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime})
acts on
A:=\operatorname{ker}(W\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma)
by restricting the action of
\Gamma
by conjugation. The isomorphism
N^{ab}\simeq A^{K}
relating these descriptions is induced by the transfer.

Now we turn to the problem of controlling the inverse limits in

\mathcal{W}_{C}=\varprojlim_{N}\mathcal{W}_{C_{N}}

from Lemma
6.13
.

Proposition 6.17
.

Let
C
be a condensed refined class formation with underlying condensed class formation
(\mathcal{C},A)
. Suppose that
for all connected objects
U\in\mathcal{C}
,

\operatorname{ker}(A(U)\to A(\ast))

is compact Hausdorff, where the map is the transfer map associated to
U\to\ast
(corresponding to the natural covariant functoriality of
H_{1}F
via
A=H_{1}F
).

Then there exists a condensed group
W
such that

\mathcal{W}_{C}\simeq BW.

Moreover
W
is an extension of
A(\ast)
by a compact Hausdorff group, hence (Remark
2.8
) is locally compact Hausdorff provided
A(\ast)
is.

Proof.

Let
N
index the Galois full subcategories
\mathcal{C}_{N}\subset\mathcal{C}
such that
\mathcal{C}_{N}
is isomorphic to finite
\Gamma
-sets for some finite group
\Gamma
. As in Remark
6.15
,
\mathcal{W}_{C_{N}}
gives us, after a choice of basepoint, a condensed group extension

1\to A\to G\to\Gamma\to 1.

By the special case of Remark
6.16
where
\Gamma^{\prime}=1
, we have
G^{ab}\simeq A^{\Gamma}
, and the homomorphism
G^{ab}\to\Gamma^{ab}
is surjective with kernel
\operatorname{tr}(A)\subset A^{\Gamma}
under this isomorphism. From the hypothesis that
\operatorname{ker}(\operatorname{tr})
is compact we deduce that
\operatorname{ker}(G\to G^{ab}\simeq A^{\Gamma})
is an extension of a finite group by a compact group, hence is compact.

Moreover, we see that the abelianization of
\mathcal{W}_{C_{N}}
is independent of
N
, namely it is the trivial gerbe with band
A(\ast)
where here
\ast
means the terminal object of
\mathcal{C}
. We deduce that the inverse system formed by the
\mathcal{W}_{C_{N}}
maps to the constant inverse system with value
B(A(\ast))
, and that the fiber of this map is an inverse system of gerbes with
compact Hausdorff
band. By Lemma
2.14
, we deduce a fiber sequence

BK\to\mathcal{W}_{C}\to B(A(\ast))

for some compact Hausdorff group
K
. Thus
\mathcal{W}_{C}=BW
with
W
an extension of
A(\ast)
by
K
, finishing the proof.
∎

Let us now refine this to an equivalence of categories, showing that refined class formations (of a certain type) are equivalent to “Weil groupoids”; compare
[
65
]
.

Theorem 6.18
.

The functor sending a condensed refined class formation
C
to the map of condensed anima

\mathcal{W}_{C}\to\underline{\mathcal{C}}

from Definition
6.9
induces an equivalence between the following two
(2,1)
-categories:

1.

The full subcategory of condensed refined class formations spanned by those whose underlying condensed class formation
(\mathcal{C},A)
satisfies the following: for all connected
U\in\mathcal{C}
, we have:

(a)

A(U)
is locally compact Hausdorff;

(b)

\operatorname{ker}(A(U)\to A(\ast))
is compact.

2.

The full subcategory of
\operatorname{Fun}(\Delta^{1},\operatorname{CondAn})
spanned by those maps of the form
B\varphi:BW\to B\Gamma
where
\varphi:W\to\Gamma
is a homomorphism of locally compact Hausdorff groups such that:

(a)

\Gamma
is profinite and
\varphi:W\to\Gamma
has dense image;

(b)

W\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\varprojlim_{N}W/W^{c}_{N}
where
N
runs over all open normal subgroups of
\Gamma
; here, for
H\subset\Gamma
an open subgroup, we let
W_{H}=\varphi^{-1}H
and let
W_{H}^{c}=\overline{[W_{H},W_{H}]}
be the closure of the commutator subgroup of
W_{H}
. Moreover we require that the transition maps in this inverse limit have compact kernel.

(c)

For any open subgroup
H\subset\Gamma
and any open normal subgroup
N\subset H
, the transfer map induces an isomorphism

W_{H}/W_{H}^{c}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}(W_{N}/W_{N}^{c})^{H/N}.

of locally compact Hausdorff abelian groups.

The functor backwards sends
X\to Y
in
\operatorname{Fun}(\Delta^{1};\operatorname{CondAn})
satisfying the above conditions to the refined condensed class formation

(\operatorname{fEt}_{Y},\left(\tau_{\geq-1}R\Gamma(-\times_{Y}X;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})\right)^{\vee},\epsilon),

where
(-)^{\vee}
stands for internal Hom to
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
and the augmentation
\epsilon
to
\mathbb{Z}
is induced by
-\times_{Y}X\to\ast
. In particular, the underlying class formation assigns
H\mapsto\varphi^{-1}H/\overline{[\varphi^{-1}H,\varphi^{-1}H]}
equipped with the sheaf structure given by the transfer maps.

Proof.

First suppose given a
C=(\mathcal{C},F,\alpha)
as in 1. By Proposition
6.17
we have that
\mathcal{W}_{C}\to\underline{\mathcal{C}}
is of the form
BW\to B\Gamma
where
W
is a locally compact Hausdorff group which is an extension of
A(\ast)
by a compact Hausdorff group.

Suppose
\Gamma^{\prime}\subset\Gamma
is an open subgroup, and let
C^{\prime}
denote the restriction of
C
to the corresponding slice category
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{C}
as in Example
5.12
. It follows from the definitions (specifically the cosheaf property) that

\mathcal{W}_{C^{\prime}}=\mathcal{W}_{C}\times_{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}\underline{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}.

On the other hand by Proposition
6.17
we have
\mathcal{W}_{C^{\prime}}=BW^{\prime}
, and it follows that
W^{\prime}=\varphi^{-1}\Gamma^{\prime}\subset W
and
W/W^{\prime}\simeq\Gamma/\Gamma^{\prime}
, which means that
\varphi
has dense image.

By Lemma
6.13
we have the inverse limit of locally compact Hausdorff groups

W=\varprojlim_{N}W_{N}

where the transition maps are quotient maps by compact normal subgroups (Remark
6.16
). Here
W_{N}
is such that
\mathcal{W}_{C_{N}}=BW_{N}
; by Remark
6.15
it fits into an extension

1\to A_{N}\to W_{N}\to\Gamma/N\to 1

where
A_{N}=A(\Gamma/N)
. Since
A_{N}
is abelian and
W_{N}
is Hausdorff, the kernel of the surjection

W\to W_{N}

contains
\overline{[\varphi^{-1}N,\varphi^{-1}N]}
. To see the opposite containment, we can pass to the open subgroup
N
as in the previous paragraph to reduce to showing
W/\overline{[W,W]}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}A(\ast)
. But on the finite levels we know that
W_{N}^{ab}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}A(\ast)
by Remark
6.16
(here without needing to take closure of the commutator subgroup in forming the abelianization), and then we get the desired claim by passing to the limit. This proves property (b).

Finally, via the above identifications, property (c) is a translation of the fact that
A=H_{1}F^{tr}
is a sheaf.

Next suppose given
\varphi:W\to\Gamma
is as in 2, and let’s check that

(\operatorname{fEt}_{B\Gamma},\left(\tau_{\geq-1}(R\Gamma((-)\times_{Y}X;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})\right)^{\vee},\epsilon)

is a condensed refined class formation as claimed. We have that
\operatorname{fEt}_{B\Gamma}
is a Galois category by
6.4
. For
\Gamma^{\prime}\subset\Gamma
an open subgroup, corresponding to
B\Gamma^{\prime}\to B\Gamma
in
\operatorname{fEt}_{B\Gamma}
, we have

H^{0}(B\Gamma^{\prime}\times_{B\Gamma}BW;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}

via
\epsilon
: this follows because
B\Gamma^{\prime}\times_{B\Gamma}BW=BW^{\prime}
with
W^{\prime}=\varphi^{-1}\Gamma^{\prime}
due to the hypothesis that
\varphi
has dense image. Moreover, for the same reason

H^{1}(B\Gamma^{\prime}\times_{B\Gamma}BW;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{cont}(W^{\prime};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}).

When we apply Pontryagin duality, we therefore get something with
H_{0}=\mathbb{Z}
and

H_{1}=W^{\prime}/\overline{[W^{\prime},W^{\prime}]}.

Then it follows from Propositon
6.8
that we have a refined class formation. Unraveling the above, we see that these two procedures (going from 1 to 2 and going from 2 to 1) are mutually inverse via natural transformations, finishing the proof.
∎

Under stronger hypotheses, which are satisfied in practice for the class formations from number theory (though
not
for their finite level analogs), we have an even nicer situation:
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
is determined by
\mathcal{W}_{C}
.

Corollary 6.19
.

The functor sending a condensed refined class formation
C=(\mathcal{C},F,\alpha)
to the condensed anima
\mathcal{W}_{C}

from Definition
6.9
induces an equivalence between the following two
(2,1)
-categories:

1.

The full subcategory of condensed refined class formations spanned by those
(\mathcal{C},F,\alpha)
such that for all connected
U\in\mathcal{C}
, we have:

(a)

H_{1}F(U)
is locally compact Hausdorff;

(b)

\operatorname{ker}(H_{1}F(U)\to H_{1}F(\ast))
is compact;

(c)

Every open subgroup of
H_{1}F(U)
of finite index contains
\operatorname{im}(H_{1}F(V)\to H_{1}F(U))
for some connected
V\in\mathcal{C}
mapping to
U
. (This property is the “existence theorem” for the underlying class formation.)

2.

The full subcategory of
\operatorname{CondAn}
spanned by those objects of the form
BW
where
W
is a locally compact Hausdorff group such that:

(a)

W\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\varprojlim_{N}W/N^{c}
where
N
runs over all open normal subgroups of
W
and
N^{c}
is the closure of the commutator subgroup of
N
, and the transition maps in this inverse system have compact kernel;

(b)

For any open subgroup
H\subset W
and any open normal subgroup
N\subset H
, the transfer map induces an isomorphism of locally compact Hausdorff groups

H/H^{c}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}(N/N^{c})^{H/N}.

Proof.

Suppose the condensed refined class formation satisfies conditions 1(a)(b)(c). Then we claim that the associated homomorphism
\varphi:W\to\Gamma
from the previous theorem identifies
\Gamma
as the profinite completion of
W
. Because
W\to\Gamma
has dense image, the map
\widehat{W}\to\Gamma
is surjective, so we need only see that the kernel is trivial, which amounts to the claim that every open subgroup of
W
of finite index contains
\varphi^{-1}\Gamma^{\prime}
for some open subgroup
\Gamma^{\prime}\subset\Gamma
.

However, every open subgroup of
W
contains
(\varphi^{-1}N)^{c}
for some open normal subgroup
N
of
\Gamma
by 2(b) in the previous theorem, and thus corresponds to an open subgroup of the quotient
W/(\varphi^{-1}N)^{c}
. We can intersect with
\varphi^{-1}N/(\varphi^{-1}N)^{c}=H_{1}F(U)
to get an open subgroup there, which by hypothesis 1(b) contains the image of
H_{1}F(U^{\prime})
for some
U^{\prime}
corresponding to some smaller normal subgroup
N^{\prime}\subset N
. It follows that our open subgroup contains
\varphi^{-1}N^{\prime}
, as required.

Conversely, suppose we have
W
satisfying 2(a) and 2(b). Setting
\Gamma=\widehat{W}
we get a homomorphism
\varphi:W\to\Gamma
which satisfies the properties 2(a),(b),(c) of the previous theorem, and therefore corresponds to a condensed refined class formation, for which we need only check the existence theorem. But an open subgroup of
H_{1}F(U)=H/H^{c}
can be viewed as an open subgroup of
W/H^{c}
when
H\subset W
is the open subgroup corresponding to
U
, and for this open subgroup the property 1(c) is tautological.
∎

Thus nice enough refined class formations can equivalently be viewed as certain condensed anima. This applies to all of the number-theoretic examples of class formations discussed above in Example
5.16
, and the corresponding condensed anima is
BW
where
W
is “the” Weil group.

In these number theoretic examples, we have a dichotomy: the underlying condensed class formations
(\mathcal{C},A)
have either one or the other of the following properties.

1.

For all connected
U\in\mathcal{C}
, the condensed abelian group
A(U)
is an extension of
\mathbb{Z}
by a profinite abelian group (called the
\mathbb{Z}
-case
in
[
65
]
);

2.

For all connected
U\in\mathcal{C}
, the condensed abelian group
A(U)
is an extension of
\mathbb{R}
by a compact Hausdorff group (called the
\mathbb{R}
-case
in
[
65
]
).

The
\mathbb{Z}
-case occurs in all non-archimedean settings (finite fields, nonarchimedean local fields, and function fields), while the
\mathbb{R}
-case occurs in all archimedean or mixed settings (archimedean local fields and number fields). It is formally convenient to also allow a third simpler case, the
compact
case, when
A(U)
is compact Hausdorff for all
U
. Thus we end up with the following trichotomy.

Definition 6.20
.

Let
C
be a condensed refined class formation which satisfies 1(a)(b)(c) from Corollary
6.19
. We say that:

1.

C
is of
\mathbb{R}
-type if
H_{1}F(U)
is an extension of
\mathbb{R}
by a compact Hausdorff group for all connected
U\in\mathcal{C}
;

2.

C
is of
\mathbb{Z}
-type if
H_{1}F(U)
is an extension of
\mathbb{Z}
by a profinite group for all connected
U\in\mathcal{C}
;

3.

C
is of compact type if
H_{1}F(U)
is compact Hausdorff for all connected
U\in\mathcal{C}
.

For brevity, if we say that
C
is a
refined class formation of
\mathbb{R}
-type, resp.
\mathbb{Z}
-type, resp. compact type
, we mean that
C
is a condensed refined class formation satisfying 1(a)(b)(c) from Corollary
6.19
as well as 1 resp. 2 resp. 3 just above.

To finish this section we analyze the
\mathbb{Z}
-case; this amounts to a reprise of a special case of a theorem of Brumer relating class formations to the condition of strict cohomological dimension 2 for a profinite group,
[
9
]
Theorem 1.

Proposition 6.21
.

The equivalence in Corollary
6.19
restricts to an equivalence between the following two
(2,1)
-categories:

1.

The category of refined class formations of
\mathbb{Z}
-type;

2.

The full subcategory of
\operatorname{CondAn}
spanned by those condensed anima of the form
BW
, where
W
is of the following type: take a profinite group
\Gamma
of
strict cohomological dimension
2
and a surjective homomorphism
\Gamma\twoheadrightarrow\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
and set

W=\Gamma\times_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}}\mathbb{Z}.

Moreover, in this case we have
H^{i}(U;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})=0
for all
i\geq 2
and all
U\in\operatorname{fEt}_{BW}
, so that the associated condensed refined class formation is given by
F=R\Gamma(-;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})^{\vee}
without any truncation needed.

Proof.

Suppose given a
W
as in 2. We recall the following equivalent form of the strict cohomological dimension
2
hypothesis, see
[
57
]
: for every open subgroup
H\subset\Gamma
, we have

H^{i}(BH;\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})=0

for all
i\geq 2
. (Actually, this characterizes strict cohomological dimension
\leq 2
, but if scd is
\leq 1
then
\Gamma=\{1\}
so there are no surjections to
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
.) Moreover we can replace
\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
by
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
here because uniquely divisible groups have no higher cohomology. This also passes to arbitrary closed subgroups by taking filtered colimits.

Now, consider the extension

1\to\Gamma^{1}\to\Gamma\to\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\to 1.

In particular this induces an action of
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
on
B\Gamma^{1}
and hence on cohomology, and letting
\gamma\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
denote the generator we deduce that:

1.

H^{i}(B\Gamma^{1};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})=0
for
i\geq 2

2.

H^{1}(B\Gamma^{1};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})_{\gamma-1}=H^{1}(B\Gamma^{1};\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})_{\gamma-1}=H^{2}(B\Gamma;\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})=0
;

Thus for
W
, which fits into the extension

1\to\Gamma^{1}\to W\to\mathbb{Z}\to 1,

we have
H^{i}(BW;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})=0
for
i\geq 2
. Every open subgroup of
W
of finite index is of the same form as
W
