so we can repeat this argument there to get the same vanishing. This already proves the last claim. Moreover, the corresponding class formation has values on connected
U\in\operatorname{fEt}_{BW}
equal to the abelianization in locally compact Hausdorff groups of the corresponding open subgroup
H\subset W
of finite index. But since
W\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Z}
we deduce that
H\twoheadrightarrow N\mathbb{Z}\simeq\mathbb{Z}
for some
N>0
and hence the same for its abelianization, which shows that we are in situation 1.

Now let us go from 1 to 2. From the fact that
H_{1}F(U)\to H_{1}F(\ast)
has compact kernel, we deduce that the induced map from the
\mathbb{Z}
-quotient of the former to the
\mathbb{Z}
-quotient of the latter is injective, hence an isomorphism on rationalization. Thus, if we push out
F
via
H_{1}F
mapping to its profinite completion, we get a new copresheaf
F^{\prime}
with
H_{0}\simeq\mathbb{Z}
and profinite
H_{1}
and having a map from
F
where the cofiber has uniquely divisible values, isomorphic to
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}/\mathbb{Z}[1]
on connected objects, and where the copresheaf structure maps associated to maps between connected objects are isomorphisms. This cofiber is thus a cosheaf, and therefore so is
F^{\prime}
. Thus we have produced a new honest class formation
F^{\prime}
for which
H_{1}F^{\prime}(U)
is the profinite completion of
H_{1}F(U)
for all connected
U
.

Thus the underlying profinite group
\Gamma
of our Galois category carries a condensed honest class formation with profinite
H_{1}
, and still satisfying the “existence theorem” axiom. Let us show that this implies that
\Gamma
has strict cohomological dimension 2 (or rather
\leq 2
). This is a theorem of Brumer from
[
9
]
, but let’s give the argument anyway because in our context it is simple. Since the
H_{1}
are profinite, from Theorem
6.17
we see that the corresponding Weil group is also profinite hence identifies with
\Gamma
by Corollary
6.19
. Thus, by going backwards to refined class formations we see that the
\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})
-valued sheaf

R\Gamma(-;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})

on
\operatorname{fEt}_{B\Gamma}
satisfies that its truncation
\tau_{\geq-1}
is also a
\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})
-valued sheaf. We deduce that
\tau_{\geq-1}R\Gamma(-;\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})
is likewise a sheaf. Clearly its sheafified homology groups vanish outside degrees
-1
and
0
and gives
\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
in degree
0
. Thus if we can show it vanishes in degree
-1
as well, we will deduce that

\tau_{\geq-1}R\Gamma(-;\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})=R\Gamma(-;\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})

and hence a fortiori we will have the required vanishing to show that
\Gamma
has strict cohomological dimension 2.

However in degree -1 we get the presheaf sending
H\subset\Gamma
to
\operatorname{Hom}_{cont}(H;\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})
. Since
H
is profinite any such homomorphism is killed by passing to an open subgroup, showing we get vanishing in thet colimit as desired.

Thus we have seen that
\Gamma
has strict cohomological dimension two. Moreover
\Gamma\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma/\overline{[\Gamma,\Gamma]}\simeq\widehat{H_{1}F(\ast)}\twoheadrightarrow\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
gives a surjective homomorphism. Thus we get a
G=\Gamma\times_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}}\mathbb{Z}
as in 2 with an obvious map
W\to G
where
W
is the Weil group corresponding to our class formation. Under the equivalence with condensed refined class formations this corresponds to a map of condensed refined class formations which by inspection is an isomorphism, thus completing the argument that 1 gives 2 and finishing the proof.
∎

Remark 6.22
.

Contained in the above analysis was the following: refined class formations of
profinite
type (i.e. of compact type and such that the class group
A(U)
is profinite for all
U
) are in 1-1 correspondence with profinite groupoids
B\Gamma
such that
\Gamma
is a profinite group of strict cohomological dimension
\leq 2
. As mentioned, modulo language this is a theorem of Brumer,
[
9
]
.

7
Weil-Moore anima

We finished the previous section with a result (Proposition
6.21
) showing that in the
\mathbb{Z}
-case, refined class formations correspond to profinite groups of strict cohomological dimension 2 equipped with a surjection to
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
. Part of the reason this is so nice is that we get the refined class formation directly from the
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
-cohomology (of finite etale covers) without any truncation, the key point being that this cohomology automatically vanishes outside cohomological degrees
0
and
1
.

This fails drastically in the
\mathbb{R}
-case.

Example 7.1
.

Consider the refined class formation for the local field
\mathbb{C}
: the Galois category is trivial, and the corresponding Weil groupoid is
B\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. The
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
-cohomology of
B\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, in degrees
\geq 2
, matches the
\mathbb{Z}
-cohomology up to a shift by Lemma
3.7
, which matches the usual
\mathbb{Z}
-cohomology of
BS^{1}\sim\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{\infty}
in algebraic topology by Lemma
2.4
. Thus we have nonvanishing of
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
-cohomology in all odd degrees.

We view this as a defect: our perspective in this paper is that all reasonable class formations should arise via the
untruncated
cohomology of some object; this should be the ultimate explanation for why the (co)sheaf property holds on the full derived level even though the refined class formation only lives in two degrees. In the
\mathbb{Z}
-cases this object is the Weil groupoid, but in the other cases we need to add higher homotopy groups. We will use the formalism for doing this from Section
4
, leading to the following definition.

Definition 7.2
.

Let
C
be a refined class formation which is either of
\mathbb{Z}
-type, of
\mathbb{R}
-type, or of compact type (see Definition
6.20
), with associated Weil groupoid
\mathcal{W}_{C}\simeq BW
from Corollary
6.19
. A
Weil-Moore
anima for
C
is a condensed anima
X
equipped with an isomorphism

t:\tau_{\leq 1}X\simeq\mathcal{W}_{C}

such that
(X,t)
is a Moore anima in the sense of Definition
4.1
, i.e. (to recall):

1.

\pi_{i}X
is compact Hausdorff for all
i\geq 2
;

2.

H^{i}(X^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})=0
for all
i\geq 2
, where
X^{\circ}=X\times_{BW}BW^{\circ}
and
W^{\circ}
is the connected component of the identity in
W
.

We now show that if
(X,t)
is a Weil-Moore anima, then the condensed anima
X
by itself canonically recovers the class formation via untruncated cohomology, as desired.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
C
be a refined class formation which is either of
\mathbb{R}
-type, of
\mathbb{Z}
-type, or of compact type, and suppose given a Weil-Moore anima
(X,t)
for
C
.

Then:

1.

for every finite etale
Y\to X
, we have
H^{i}(Y;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})=0
for all
i\geq 2
;

2.

the refined class formation
C
is recovered as

C=(\operatorname{fEt}_{X},R\Gamma(-;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})^{\vee},\epsilon)

where
\epsilon
is induced by
(-)\to\ast
.

Proof.

First we show that 1 implies 2. By Corollary
6.19
the class formation
C
is recovered from
\mathcal{W}_{C}
in a similar way but by taking cohomology truncated to degrees
\geq-1
. This truncated cohomology is the same for
\mathcal{W}_{C}
as for
X
because of the datum
t
. On the other hand by 1 the truncation is irrelevant for
X
, so this gives 2.

Now let us prove 1. First suppose we are in the
\mathbb{Z}
-type case. Then the associated Weil group
W
is totally disconnected, so from Example
4.17
we have that every Moore anima
X
for
W
satisfies
X\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}BW
. Thus 1 follows from Proposition
6.21
.

Next suppose we are in the compact type case. Then by the basic theory of compact Hausdorff groups,
W^{\circ}
is the intersection of all open subgroups
H
of
W
. It follows that

X^{\circ}=\varprojlim_{H}X_{H}

where
X^{\circ}=X\times_{BW}BW^{\circ}
and
X_{H}=X\times_{BW}BH
. On the level of
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
cohomology, this means that the cohomology of
X^{\circ}
is the filtered colimit of the cohomologies of the
X_{H}
(Lemma
3.13
). It follows that the stalk of the sheaf

(Y\to X)\mapsto R\Gamma(Y;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})

on the Galois category
\operatorname{fEt}_{X}
identifies with
R\Gamma(X^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
. (Let us work with the underlying
\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})
-valued sheaf here: the cohomology is discrete in positive degrees anyway, by Lemma
3.7
.) Similarly the stalk of the truncation
\tau_{\geq-1}R\Gamma(-;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
is given by
\tau_{\geq-1}\Gamma(X^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
. The Moore anima hypothesis therefore yields that the map from truncated cohomology to untruncated cohomology is an isomorphism on stalks. However, both of these are (coconnective) sheaves: the untruncated cohomology by definition, and the truncated one because it is Pontryagin dual to the cosheaf which gives our refined class formation
C
, see Corollary
6.19
. Thus the map from truncated to untruncated cohomology is an isomorphism, proving 1.

Finally, suppose we are in the
\mathbb{R}
-type case. Let
F:\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{CondAb})
be the cosheaf giving the refined class formation
C
. The axioms imply that for any connected
U\in\mathcal{C}
, the map

H_{1}F(U)\to H_{1}F(\ast)

induces an isomorphism on the
\mathbb{R}
-quotients of these two groups. Thus we can identify all the
\mathbb{R}
-quotients with a single
V\simeq\mathbb{R}
. After a choice of nullhomotopy of an obstruction map

\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{Z}\to R\Gamma(\mathcal{C};V[2]),

we can make a refined class formation of compact type
C^{\prime}
on the same Galois category, with
F^{\prime}\to F
inducing on
H_{1}
the inclusion of the maximal compact subgroup. On the level of Weil groupoids, this gives a fiber sequence

\mathcal{W}_{C^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{W}_{C}\to B\mathbb{R}.

Given our Weil-Moore anima
X\to\mathcal{W}_{C}
, we can therefore pull it back to
X^{\prime}\to\mathcal{W}_{C^{\prime}}
, and this will be a Weil-Moore anima for
\mathcal{W}_{C^{\prime}}
(indeed, we get a similar fiber sequence for
(X^{\prime})^{\circ}
and
X^{\circ}
, then we can use that
B\mathbb{R}
-cohomology doesn’t affect discrete groups). By the compact-type case just established, this shows that for
Y\to X
finite etale, the pullback
Y^{\prime}\to X^{\prime}
has the required vanishing in
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
-cohomology. But the
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
-cohomology of
Y
is the
B\mathbb{R}
-cohomology of the
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
-cohomology of
Y^{\prime}
, which is discrete in positive degrees by Lemma
3.7
, so we can reduce to the comapct-type case treated above.
∎

Now we recall that in Section
4
, we built an obstruction theory for constructing Moore anima. The takeaway was the following, Corollary
4.16
: suppose
G
is an almost compact group satisfying the following two assumptions:

1.

H^{2}(BG^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})=0
;

2.

For all discrete
G/G^{\circ}
-modules
M
and all
i\geq 3
we have

\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{G/G^{\circ}}(M,c_{G})=0

where

c_{G}=\tau_{\geq-1}R\Gamma(BG^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}).

Then
G
admits a Moore anima, unique up to isomorphism.

This motivates the following, which is our main technical observation.

Proposition 7.4
.

Let
C
be a refined class formation which is either of
\mathbb{Z}
-type, of
\mathbb{R}
-type, or of compact type, with associated Weil groupoid
\mathcal{W}_{C}\simeq BW
. If
C
is of
\mathbb{Z}
-type, assume furthermore the following technical hypothesis: the kernel
W^{1}
of the corresponding surjective homomorphism

W\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Z},

which is a profinite group (see Proposition
6.21
), has cohomological dimension
\leq 1
with respect to torsion coefficients.

Then for any discrete
W/W^{\circ}
-module
M
and any
i\geq 3
, we have

\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{W/W^{\circ}}(M,c_{W})=0.

Proof.

First let’s consider the case where
C
is of compact type, so that
W
is a compact Hausdorff group.

Note that
c_{W}
is almost discrete: it is
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
in degree
0
and Pontryagin dual to the (abelianization of the) compact
W^{\circ}
in degree
-1
. It will be technically convenient to instead work with the fully-discrete (Lemma
2.2
)

d_{W}:=\tau_{\geq-2}R\Gamma(BW^{\circ};\mathbb{Z}).

Note by Lemma
3.7
that there is a natural cofiber sequence

R\Gamma(BW^{\circ};\mathbb{Z})\to\mathbb{R}\to R\Gamma(BW^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}),

and we can truncate this to get a cofiber sequence

d_{W}\to\mathbb{R}\to c_{W}.

Translating from
c_{W}
to
d_{W}
, we claim it suffices to prove

\operatorname{Ext}_{W/W^{\circ}}^{i}(M,d_{W})=0

for all
i\geq 4
and all discrete
M
instead. Indeed, we have

\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{W/W^{\circ}}(M;\mathbb{R})=0

for
i>0
by Lemma
2.11
.

Since
R\Gamma(BW^{\circ};\mathbb{Z})\in\operatorname{D}_{W/W^{\circ}}(\operatorname{CondAb})
is discrete, by Lemma
2.12
we can represent it by a complex

[I^{0}\overset{d^{0}}{\rightarrow}I^{1}\overset{d^{1}}{\rightarrow}I^{2}\overset{d^{2}}{\rightarrow}I^{3}\to\dots]

of injective
W/W^{\circ}
-modules. Thus the truncation
d_{W}
is represented by

[I^{0}\overset{d^{0}}{\rightarrow}I^{1}\overset{d^{1}}{\rightarrow}\operatorname{ker}(d^{2})].

Now suppose
H\subset W
is an open subgroup, which we can also view as an open subgroup of
W/W^{\circ}
. Then by the same token, the fixed point complex

[(I^{0})^{H}\to(I^{1})^{H}\to(I^{2})^{H}\to\ldots]

represents
R\Gamma(BH;\mathbb{Z})
, and

[(I^{0})^{H}\to(I^{1})^{H}\to\operatorname{ker}(d^{2})^{H}]

represents
\tau_{\geq-2}R\Gamma(BH;\mathbb{Z})
. On the other hand, exactly as above we have the fiber sequence

\tau_{\geq-2}R\Gamma(BH;\mathbb{Z})\to\mathbb{R}\to\tau_{\geq-1}R\Gamma(BH;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}).

This last term gives a sheaf on
\operatorname{fEt}_{BW}
because it is Pontryagin dual to our refined class formation. The middle term obviously does as well. Thus the first term also gives a sheaf; in other words the complex
[I^{0}\to I^{1}\to\operatorname{ker}(d^{2})]
represents a derived sheaf. Since injective sheaves are derived sheaves sitting in degree
0
(same argument as above), we deduce from this the following conclusion:
\operatorname{ker}(d^{2})[0]
is a derived sheaf. In classical terms, this means that
\operatorname{ker}(d^{2})
is
cohomologically trivial

W/W^{\circ}
-module. It therefore follows by the main result of
[
41
]
that it admits an injective resolution with just two terms:

0\to\operatorname{ker}(d^{2})\to J^{0}\to J^{1}\to 0.

We can splice this on to our complex representing
d_{W}
to get a complex of injectives

[I^{0}\to I^{1}\to J^{0}\to J^{1}]

which represents
d_{W}
. The required Ext vanishing follows.

Now let us handle the
\mathbb{R}
-case. As in the proof of Lemma
7.3
, we can make a compact-type refined class formation
C^{\prime}
such that the associated Weil group
W^{\prime}
satisfies

1\to W^{\prime}\to W\to\mathbb{R}\to 1.

It follows that
W/W^{\circ}\simeq W^{\prime}/(W^{\prime})^{\circ}
and

c_{W}\simeq c_{W^{\prime}}\oplus\mathbb{R}[-1].

As above we already showed Ext vanishing with value
\mathbb{R}
in all positive degrees, this means that the claim for
C
follows from the claim for
C^{\prime}
, proven above.

Finally, consider the
\mathbb{Z}
-case. We first note that the compact-type case treated above admits a refinement when applied to the refined class formation corresponding to the profinite group
\Gamma=W^{1}
. Namely, from the long exact sequence associated to
\cdot p:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
for all prime numbers
p
we deduce from our extra assumption the following:
H^{2}(BH;\mathbb{Z})
is a divisible abelian group for all open subgroups
H\subset\Gamma
. Then
\operatorname{ker}(d^{2})^{H}
is an extension of divisible groups, hence is also divisible. So not only is
\operatorname{ker}(d^{2})
cohomologically trivial, but its
H
-fixed points are divisible for all
H
. Thus again by
[
41
]
we deduce that
\operatorname{ker}(d^{2})
is on the nose injective, with no need of further resolution. Therefore we do one better in our Ext vanishing result for
\Gamma
. Returning to our Weil group
W
, note that since
W
is totally disconnected we have
W^{\circ}=\{1\}
and
c_{W}=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
, and similarly for
W^{1}
. On the other hand

\operatorname{RHom}_{W}(M,\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})\simeq R\Gamma(B\mathbb{Z};\operatorname{RHom}_{W^{1}}(M,\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})),

so as
B\mathbb{Z}
-cohomology makes degrees go down at most by one, using that we saved one degree above we get the conclusion.
∎

Remark 7.5
.

The extra assumption in the
\mathbb{Z}
-type case, that
W^{1}
have cohomological dimension 1 with respect to torsion coefficients, is certainly satisfied in all number theoretic examples. Here is one way of verifying it. Note that it is equivalent to ask that
H^{i}(BH;\mathbb{F}_{p})=0
for all
i>1
all open subgroups
H\subset W^{1}
and all primes
p
. We know this vanishing for
i>2
from Proposition
6.21
, so it’s just about showing
H^{2}(BH;\mathbb{F}_{p})=0
for all
p
(which is exactly what we used in the above proof anyway). Every open subgroup
H\subset W^{1}
arises from some open subgroup of finite index
H^{\prime}\subset W
by passing “up the unramified tower”, formed by the preimages
H^{\prime}_{N}
of
N\mathbb{Z}\subset\mathbb{Z}
under
H^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Z}
. On the other hand the
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-cohomology of
H^{\prime}
is calculated by

\operatorname{RHom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(F(BH^{\prime}),\mathbb{F}_{p})

where
F
is the cosheaf giving our refined class formation, by Proposition
6.21
. We deduce the following:
H^{2}(BH;\mathbb{F}_{p})
is the Pontryagin dual of the inverse limit

\varprojlim_{N}H_{1}F(BH^{\prime}_{N})[p]

of the
p
-torsion in the corresponding “class groups” along the “norm maps”. For example, in the case of nonarchimedean local fields, we find that we need to check that the inverse limit of
\mu_{p}(F_{N})
under the norm maps gives
0
as
F_{N}
runs through the unramified extensions of any nonarchimedean field
F
. This is obvious as extensions of degree
p
induce the
0
map, and there are always more and more of those.

Finally we can prove the main theorem.

Theorem 7.6
.

Let
C
be a condensed refined class formation which is either of
\mathbb{Z}
-type, of
\mathbb{R}
-type, or of compact type. Then there exists a Weil-Moore anima
(X,t)
for
C
, and moreover such a Weil-Moore anima is unique up to isomorphism.

In the
\mathbb{Z}
-type case and the profinite case it is completely unique (contractible space of choices).

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{W}_{C}\simeq BW
denote the associated Weil groupoid (Corollary
6.19
). By definition, a Weil-Moore anima for
C
is the same thing as a Moore anima for
BW
. In the
\mathbb{Z}
-type and profinite cases,
W
is totally disconnected, so the space of such Moore anima is contractible by Example
4.17
. In the
\mathbb{R}
-type and compact-type cases, because of Corollary
4.16
and Proposition
7.4
, we need only show
H^{2}(BW^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})=0
. But
W^{\circ}
is abelian by construction, so this follows from Lemma
4.11
.
∎

Remark 7.7
.

Let
X
be a Weil-Moore anima as above and
A
a compact abelian group with
W=\pi_{1}X
-action. By Lemma
3.10
we have

R\Gamma(X;A)\simeq\operatorname{RHom}_{W/W^{\circ}}(A^{\vee};c_{W}).

Thus Proposition
7.4
translates to the following fundamental fact: suppose
X
is a Weil-Moore anima whose underlying class formation is of
\mathbb{Z}
-type,
\mathbb{R}
-type, or compact-type, and suppose in the
\mathbb{Z}
-type case that it satisfies the extra hypothesis in Proposition
7.4
. Then

H^{i}(X;A)=0

for all compact Hausdorff coefficient systems
A
on
X
and all
i>2
.

We get Weil-Moore anima attached to any of the number theoretic class formations discussed in Example
5.16
. Most prominent for us are the following.

Example 7.8
.

1.

Fix a number field
K
and a nonempty set of places
S
, and consider the condensed refined class formation
C_{K,S}=(\mathcal{C}_{K,S},F_{K,S},\alpha_{K,S})
which was canonically produced in Example
5.16
, whose underlying Galois category consists of the finite extensions
L/K
unramified outside
S
and whose class group takes the value

A(L)=L^{\times}\backslash\mathbb{A}_{L}^{\times}/\prod_{\nu\not\in S_{L}}(L_{\nu})^{\times,1}.

(This can also be interpreted as the “compactly supported Picard group of
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{K,S})
”, at least when
S
is finite and contains all archimedean places.) Then associated to
C_{K,S}
is a Weil-Moore anima
X_{K,S}
, uniquely specified up to isomorphism.

2.

For a nonarchimedean local field
K
, after a small choice (of algebraic closure of its residue field), we got a canonical refined class formation
C_{K}
where
\mathcal{C}_{K}
is the Galois category corresponding to finite separable extensions of
K
, and where the class group is the multiplicative group. Then we get a Weil-Moore anima
X_{K}\simeq BW_{K}
, this time canonically (given the non-canonical choice of refined class formation).

3.

For an archimedean local field
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
, we got a pretty much canonical refined class formation of the same sort as in the nonarchimedean case. Thus we get Weil-Moore anima
X_{\mathbb{R}}
and
X_{\mathbb{C}}
, uniquely specified up to isomorphism. In contrast to the nonarchimdean case, these are different from
\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq BW_{\mathbb{R}}
and
\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{C}}\simeq BW_{\mathbb{C}}
respectively, but they can still be made quite explicit.

For motivation, consider the simpler
X_{\mathbb{C}}
. The Weil groupoid is
\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{C}}=B\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. When we follow the obstruction theory for adding higher homotopy groups to build the Weil-Moore anima, we find it exactly mimics the procedure of building
S^{2}
from
K(\mathbb{Z},2)
from Serre’s thesis. This suggests the following construction of
X_{\mathbb{C}}
, and even
X_{\mathbb{R}}
(which recovers
X_{\mathbb{C}}
by pullback).

Recall that to make our refined class formation for
\mathbb{R}
, or equivalently our Weil grouopid
\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{R}}
, we needed to “choose
\mathbb{H}
”, but only up to Morita equivalence (more precisely, as an object in the Morita 2-groupoid). Namely, our preferred model for
\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{R}}

was as the condensed groupoid of pairs consisting of a choice of algebraic closure
\mathbf{C}
of
\mathbb{R}
and a Morita equivalence
\mathbb{H}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbf{C}\sim\mathbf{C}
.

To proceed towards constructing
X_{\mathbb{R}}
, we start by fixing
\mathbb{H}
more honestly, as a division algebra. A Morita equivalence as above is implemented by an
\mathbb{H}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbf{C}
-module
M
whose underlying
\mathbb{H}
-module will be free of rank one. This describes a map

\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{R}}\to B\mathbb{H}^{\times},

which after choice of basepoint implements the usual identification of
W_{\mathbb{R}}
with a subgroup of
\mathbb{H}^{\times}
(say,
W_{\mathbb{R}}=\mathbb{C}^{\times}\cup j\cdot\mathbb{C}^{\times}
).

The fiber of the above map identifies with
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}
, which more precisely arises as condensed set of closed points in the Brauer-Severi variety
\operatorname{Tw}_{\mathbb{R}}
attached to
\mathbb{H}
. This corresponds to the description

\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}=\mathbb{H}^{\times}/W_{\mathbb{R}}

as a quotient. If we pass to the norm-1 version of everything, then
\mathbb{H}^{\times}
gets replaced by the unit quaternion group
S^{3}
, and
W_{\mathbb{R}}
gets replaced by
S^{1}\cup j\cdot S^{1}
acting on it by isometries, but
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}
stays the same. Then we explained in Example
3.19
how to build a condensed anima
C
with
\pi_{0}C=\ast
and
\pi_{n}C\in\operatorname{CpctLieGp}
for
n\geq 1
together with a map

\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}\to C

which induces an equivalence on categories of discrete coefficient systems (and their cohomology). We get
X_{\mathbb{R}}
by adding back in the
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
-factor:

X_{\mathbb{R}}=C\times B\mathbb{R}_{>0}.

We would also like to have the “obvious” maps between these different Weil-Moore anima. Unfortunately, here the situation is quite a bit less satisfactory than for the Weil groupoids (or refined class formations), which was already slightly unsatisfactory. Namely we will see that the space of possible maps (inducing a given map of Weil groupoids) is nonempty, but possibly non-connected. For example, it’s quite possible that there are two non-homotopic local-to-global maps
X_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\to X_{\mathbb{Q}}
. Of course, in principle this leads to further issues if one ever wants a commutative diagram involving choices of such maps. Nonetheless, with some extra work we will manage to produce a number of relevant commutative diagrams. Furthermore, the non-uniqueness up to homotopy always goes away on profinite completion, see Remark
7.13
.

Proposition 7.9
.

Let
X
and
X^{\prime}
be two Weil-Moore anima, with underlying condensed refined class formations
C
and
C^{\prime}
respectively. If
C
is of
\mathbb{Z}
-type, assume it satisfies the same extra technical hypothesis as in Proposition
7.4
. Suppose given a map
C\to C^{\prime}
, or equivalently (Corollary
6.19
) a map

f_{1}:\tau_{\leq 1}X\to\tau_{\leq 1}X^{\prime}.

Then there exists a map
f:X\to X^{\prime}
inducing
f_{1}
, and more precisely the set of homotopy classes of such
f
inducing
f_{1}
is a torsor for the group

H^{2}(X;\pi_{2}X^{\prime}),

where
\pi_{2}X^{\prime}
is the twisted coefficient system over
X
gotten by pulling the standard
\pi_{1}X^{\prime}
action on
\pi_{2}X^{\prime}
back via
f_{1}
.

Proof.

This is standard obstruction theory. We interpret
f_{1}
equivalently as a map

X\to\tau_{\leq 1}X^{\prime}.

The second stage of the Postnikov tower for
X^{\prime}
is classified by a k-invariant in
H^{3}(\tau_{\leq 1}X^{\prime};\pi_{2}X^{\prime})
, or more honestly by a cocyle-level object, a higher gerbe. Lifting our given map
X\to\tau_{\leq 1}X^{\prime}
to
\tau_{\leq 2}X^{\prime}
is the same as splitting the pullback of this gerbe, which is the same as trivializing the corresponding cocycle on pullback to
X
. This can be done if and only if the corresponding class in

H^{3}(X;\pi_{2}X^{\prime})

vanishes, but it does vanish by Remark
7.7
.
∎

Thus, for example, if
F
is a number field and
\nu
is a place of
F
, we can make a local-global map of Weil-Moore anima

X_{F_{\nu}}\to X_{F}

inducing “the” (unique up to homotopy) map of refined class formations discussed following Lemma
5.17
. Unfortunately it is probably not unique up to homotopy, and because of this, we need some extra work to produce certain relevant commutative diagrams.

Lemma 7.10
.

Suppose given Weil-Moore anima
X,Y,Y^{\prime}
, all of which satisfy the conditions of the previous result. Suppose also given a map
g:Y\to Y^{\prime}
and a map
\tau_{\leq 1}X\to\tau_{\leq 1}Y
, giving in particular the sequence of maps

\tau_{\leq 1}X\to\tau_{\leq 1}Y\overset{\tau_{\leq 1}g}{\longrightarrow}\tau_{\leq 1}Y^{\prime}.

Assume that
\pi_{2}g:\pi_{2}Y\to\pi_{2}Y^{\prime}
is surjective.

Then the map (induced by composing with
g
) from the set of lifts of
\tau_{\leq 1}X\to\tau_{\leq 1}Y
to a map
X\to Y
, towards the set of lifts of
\tau_{\leq 1}X\to\tau_{\leq 1}Y^{\prime}
to a map
X\to Y^{\prime}
, is surjective.

Proof.

By functoriality of the obstruction theory in the previous result, the map in question is induced by a map of torsors compatible with the homomorphism

H^{3}(X;\pi_{2}Y)\to H^{3}(X;\pi_{2}Y^{\prime})

induced by
\pi_{2}g
. Thus it suffices to see that this homomorphism is surjective. But the kernel of the surjective map
\pi_{2}Y\to\pi_{2}Y^{\prime}
is also compact Hausdorff, so this follows form the long exact sequence in cohomology and the same vanishing result (Remark
7.7
) we used above.
∎

Now is perhaps the right time to discuss the
\pi_{2}
of Weil-Moore anima, as an excuse to see how to verify the hypotheses of the previous lemma in practice.

Lemma 7.11
.

Let
X
be a Weil-Moore anima with underlying condensed class formation
(\mathcal{C},A)
. Then we have

\pi_{2}X\simeq\left(\operatorname{Sym}^{2}_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda\right)^{\vee},

where
\Lambda
is the torsionfree discrete
\mathbb{Z}
-module gotten as follows: consider the subgroup presheaf

K^{\circ}\subset A

given by the maximal compact subgroup of the compoment group of the class group, and give it a
co-presheaf
structure using the transfer maps on
A
. We thus get a presheaf
(K^{\circ})^{\vee}
via Pontryagin duality, and
\Lambda
is the stalk of this presheaf (on
\mathcal{C}
). From this description wee see a
\widehat{\pi_{1}X}=\widehat{W}
-action and hence a
W
-action on
\Lambda
, thereby also giving a
W
-action on
\left(\operatorname{Sym}^{2}_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda\right)^{\vee}
, and this agrees with the usual action of
\pi_{1}X
on
\pi_{2}X
via the above isomorphism.

Moreover, if
X\to X^{\prime}
is a map of Weil-Moore anima such that:

1.

for all
U^{\prime}\in\operatorname{fEt}_{X^{\prime}}
connected the pullback
U=U^{\prime}\times_{X^{\prime}}X
is also connected, or equivalently the map
\pi_{1}X\to\pi_{1}X^{\prime}
is surjective on profinite completion;

2.

for all
U^{\prime}\in\operatorname{fEt}_{X^{\prime}}
the induced map on class groups

A(U)\to A^{\prime}(U^{\prime})

is surjective on maximal compact subgroups;

then
\pi_{2}X\to\pi_{2}X^{\prime}
is surjective.

Proof.

Recall from Lemma
4.12
that if
X
is the Moore anima of a general locally compact Hausdorff group
G
, then

\pi_{2}X=\left(H^{3}(BG^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})\right)^{\vee}.

For the fundamental group
G\simeq\pi_{1}X
of a Weil-Moore anima, we have

G=\varprojlim_{N}G_{N}

where
G_{N}
are the Weil groups of the “finite quotients” of
\mathcal{C}
; moreover the maps
G\to G_{N}
are given as the quotient by a compact normal subgroup, hence are open and proper. It follows that the induced map

G^{\circ}\to(G_{N})^{\circ}

is also such a quotient, and that

G^{\circ}\simeq\varprojlim_{N}(G_{N})^{\circ}.

On the other hand
G_{N}
is an extension of a finite group by the class group corresponding to
N
, so
(G_{N})^{\circ}
is the same as the connected component of the class group, with functoriality induced by the transfer map. We get the same relation

K^{\circ}\simeq\varprojlim_{N}K_{N}^{\circ}

on the level of maximal compact subgroups of component groups (indeed, at most we are ignoring an
\mathbb{R}
-factor), and hence we get

H^{3}(BG^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})\simeq H^{3}(BK^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})\simeq H^{4}(BK^{\circ};\mathbb{Z})\simeq\varinjlim_{N}H^{4}(BK_{N}^{\circ};\mathbb{Z}).

But for a real torus
T
we have
H^{4}(BT;\mathbb{Z})=\operatorname{Sym}^{2}_{\mathbb{Z}}(H^{2}(BT;\mathbb{Z}))=\operatorname{Sym}^{2}_{\mathbb{Z}}(T^{\vee}),

and we get the same for an arbitrary connected compact abelian group
T
by passing to filtered colimits. The desired claim

\pi_{2}X\simeq H^{3}(BG^{\circ};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})^{\vee}\simeq\left(\operatorname{Sym}^{2}_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda\right)^{\vee}

follows.

For the second claim, let us first show that 1 and 2 together imply (actually, are equivalent to) the statement that the induced homomorphism

G\to G^{\prime}

on fundamental groups is surjective on maximal compact subgroups. Again by compactness arguments and reduction to the finite level version of the Weil groups, this follows from the fact that a map of group extensions which is surjective on the quotient terms and on the kernel terms is surjective in the middle. We deduce that
G\to G^{\prime}
is also surjective on connected components of maximal compact subgroups.

By Pontryagin duality we get
\Lambda^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\Lambda
. Applying
\operatorname{Sym}^{2}_{\mathbb{Z}}
preserves inclusions of torsionfree abelian groups (one can use filtered colimits to reduce to the case of finite free abelian groups), so applying
\operatorname{Sym}^{2}_{\mathbb{Z}}
then Pontryagin duality again we deduce
\pi_{2}X\twoheadrightarrow\pi_{2}X^{\prime}
as desired.
∎

Example 7.12
.

Let us apply this to the problem of choosing Weil-Moore anima in number-theoretic situations. First, we fix the condensed refined class formations and maps between them as discussed in Section
5
. This gives the
\tau_{\leq 1}
-level of the Weil-Moore data we’re trying to construct.

We start with
K=\mathbb{Q}
and
S
a finite set of places of
\mathbb{Q}
containing
\infty
and such that if it contains a prime then it contains all smaller primes. For every such
S
, we arbitrarily choose a Weil-Moore anima
X_{\mathbb{Q},S}
lifting the relevant condensed refined class formation (Example
5.16
). Moreover, if
S
is of this form and
p
is the smallest prime number
not
in
S
, then we arbitrarily choose a map of Weil-Moore anima

X_{\mathbb{Q},S\cup\{p\}}\to X_{\mathbb{Q},S}

lifting the given map of condensed refined class formations. Furthermore, there is a map of Weil-Moore anima

X_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\to X_{\mathbb{Q},S},

this time unique up to homotopy because
X_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}=B\mathbb{Z}
has cohomological dimension 1, inducing the map of condensed refined class formations given by the unramified at
p
condition. Then, using the above results, we choose a lift of the composite

X_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\to X_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\to X_{\mathbb{Q},S}

along

X_{\mathbb{Q},S\cup\{p\}}\to X_{\mathbb{Q},S},

getting a commutative diagram

\textstyle{X_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{\mathbb{Q},S\cup\{p\}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{\mathbb{Q},S}}

We can also continue to lift
X_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\to X_{\mathbb{Q},S\cup\{p\}}
along further
X_{\mathbb{Q},S\cup\{p,p^{\prime}\}}\to X_{\mathbb{Q},S\cup\{p\}}
of the above form, for the same reason.

With all the above data fixed, we set

X_{\mathbb{Q}}:=\varprojlim_{S}X_{\mathbb{Q},S}

as our Weil-Moore anima for
\mathbb{Q}
. It is indeed easy to see that this is a Weil-Moore anima for
\mathbb{Q}
, using that:

1.

we have an inverse limit of Galois groups and an inverse limit of class groups, giving an inverse limit of Weil groups;

2.

we can use Lemma
3.13
to see that the higher homotopy doesn’t mess with the
\pi_{1}
or
\pi_{0}
of the limit and to control the cohomology of the inverse limit (to verify the Weil-Moore axioms).

The above data in particular gives
X_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\to X_{\mathbb{Q}}
for all
p
, and of course we can also fix a Weil-Moore anima
X_{\mathbb{R}}
for
\mathbb{R}
as in Example
7.8
and a local-global map
X_{\mathbb{R}}\to X_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

For an arbitrary number field
K
, if we choose
S
as above to contain all primes of
\mathbb{Q}
which ramify in
K
, then by pullback along

X_{\mathbb{Q},S}\to BG_{\mathbb{Q},S}\leftarrow BG_{K,S_{K}}

(which is canonical) we obtain analogous data for
K
, in a natural way (given the fixed data for
\mathbb{Q}
).

It’s possible that system of data might be sufficient in practice, but it is nonetheless a bit annoying that we have not allowed arbitrary finite sets of places containing
\infty
, but only those of the form “
\infty
together with all primes
\leq N
” (or in the case of number fields
K
, only those finite sets of places arising as preimages of such constrained finite sets of places of
\mathbb{Q}
, and additionally required to contain all ramified primes). Perhaps a more refined analysis could yield further data. Of course, if one fixes a number field and a finite set of places and doesn’t worry about any compatibility as this data varies, then one can just fix choices of the Weil-Moore anima and local-global maps.

Remark 7.13
.

Although two choices of Weil-Moore maps need not be homotopic, they will always be homotopic on profinite completion (for which see Proposition
3.18
). To show this, note that if
X
is a Weil-Moore anima, then the map

\widehat{X}\to\widehat{\tau_{\leq 1}X}

is an isomorphism on
\tau_{\leq 2}
. Indeed, it is obviously an isomorphism on
\tau_{\leq 1}
, so by the Postnikov tower it suffices to show that if
A
is a finite abelian group with
\pi_{1}X
-action, then

H^{2}(\tau_{\leq 1}X;A)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}H^{2}(X;A).

Choosing a baseopint of
X
we have
\tau_{\leq 1}X=BW
and
\tau_{\leq 2}X=K(\pi_{2}X,2)/W
, so it suffices to show that
\operatorname{Hom}(\pi_{2}X,A)=0
, which follows from the fact that
\pi_{2}X
is connected (Lemma
7.11
).

Now, given another Weil-Moore anima
Y
and a map

Y\to\tau_{\leq 1}X

(or equivalently a map of the underlying condensed refined class formations), by the above this induces

Y\to\tau_{\leq 2}\widehat{X}

Then the obstructions to existence and uniqueness of further lifts along the Postnikov tower of
\widehat{X}
lie in

H^{i}(Y;\pi_{\ast}\widehat{X})

for
i\geq 3
, which vanish by Remark
7.7
.

8
The Weil-Moore analogs of some theorems of Tate

We will now prove the analogs of some fundamental theorems of Tate in Galois cohomology (
[
63
]
) for these new objects, the Weil-Moore anima. Our focus is on the cohomology of Weil-Moore anima with discrete coefficients, often restricted to be
p
-power torsion for some fixed prime
p
. The first theorem expresses that Weil-Moore anima have strict cohomological dimension 2 with discrete coefficients, modulo some technicalities in the
\mathbb{Z}
-cases.

Theorem 8.1
.

Let
X
be a Weil-Moore anima with underlying class formation either of
\mathbb{Z}
-type,
\mathbb{R}
-type, or compact type, and let
W=\pi_{1}X
denote “the” associated Weil group, with profinite completion
\widehat{W}
(the Galois group). If
X
is of
\mathbb{Z}
-type, assume the same additional technical assumption from Proposition
7.4
is satisfied. Then for any discrete group
M
with
W
-action, we have:

1.

H^{i}(X;M)=0
for
i>3
;

2.

If either
M
comes from a
\widehat{W}
-action or
M
is torsion, then
H^{3}(X;M)=0
.

Proof.

We recall from Remark
7.7
that the cohomology with
compact Hausdorff
coefficients vanishes in degrees
\geq 3
. Now we must move to discrete coefficients.

Suppose
M
comes from a
\widehat{W}
-action; thus we need to show cohomology vanishing in degrees
>2
. By Lemma
3.6
cohomology on
X
commutes with filtered colimits, so we reduce to the case where
M
is a finitely generated discrete
\widehat{W}
-module; in particular
M
is finitely generated as an abelian group. Consider the exact sequence

0\to M_{tors}\to M\to M\otimes\mathbb{R}\to M\otimes(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})\to 0.

We have cohomology vanishing in degrees
>2
for
M\otimes(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
and
M_{tors}
because these are compact, and we have cohomology vanishing in degrees
>1
for
M\otimes\mathbb{R}
by Lemma
3.6
. We deduce cohomology vanishing in degrees
>3
for
M
. Now we need to improve this to get cohomology vanishing for degree
3
as well. Since
M
is finitely generated, it is a quotient of a finite direct sum of copies of
\mathbb{Z}[W/H]
for some open subgroup
H\subset W
of finite index. By cohomology vanishing for the kernel in degree 4, we deduce that the vanishing claim for
M
is implied by that for
\mathbb{Z}[W/H]
. Thus we need

H^{3}(Y;\mathbb{Z})=0

for all connected finite etale
Y\to X
(corresponding to
H\subset W
via Galois theory). But by Lemma
3.7
this is equivalent to
H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})=0
, which holds because that cohomology group is Pontryagin dual to
H_{2}
of our refined class formation hence vanishes by definition.

If
X
is of compact type or
\mathbb{R}
-type, then every discrete abelian group
M
with continuous
W
-action comes from a
\widehat{W}
-action, as
\widehat{W}=W/W^{\circ}
. Thus we are done in those cases. In the
\mathbb{Z}
-case, we have a short exact sequence

1\to W^{1}\to W\to\mathbb{Z}\to 1

where
W^{1}
is a profinite group of cohomological dimension
\leq 1
with torsion discrete coefficients (hence
\leq 2
with arbitrary discrete coefficients), so we conclude because in any case
\mathbb{Z}
has cohomological dimension 1.
∎

Remark 8.2
.

The analogous result to 2 for Galois cohomology with restricted ramification (with odd-primary coefficients, or for number fields with no real places, these being “obvious” necessary restrictions) is conjectured to be true, but is currently unknown, and in fact it turns out to be equivalent to Leopoldt’s conjecture. See
[
45
]
for a thorough discussion. In the Weil-Moore setting, we avoid Leopoldt’s conjecture.

Having discussed cohomological dimension properties, we now turn to finiteness properties, in the setting of
p
-primary torsion coefficient systems.

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
p
be a prime,
G
a group object in anima, and
M\in\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[G])
such that
M
is
p
-complete and bounded below. The following properties are equivalent:

1.

M\in\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[G])
.

2.

M\otimes_{\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[G]}\mathbb{F}_{p}[\pi_{0}G]\in\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{F}_{p}[\pi_{0}G])
.

Proof.

Note that
\mathbb{F}_{p}[\pi_{0}G]=\pi_{0}(\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[G])/p
. Thus this is a specialization of a general fact about
p
-complete bounded above modules over
p
-complete connective ring spectra, see
[
14
]
Lemma 13.16.
∎

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
p
be a prime,
G
a finite group, and
N\in\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{F}_{p}[G])
. The following properties are equivalent:

1.

N\in\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{F}_{p}[G])
.

2.

N^{H}\in\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{F}_{p})
for all subgroups
H\subset G
.

3.

N_{H}\in\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{F}_{p})
for all subgroups
H\subset G
.

Proof.

It’s clear that 1
\Rightarrow
2,3. On the other hand, 2
\Leftrightarrow
3 because under either 2 or 3, Lemma
5.9
implies that
N_{H}\simeq N^{H}
for all
H
, so that 2 and 3 are literally the same. Thus it suffices to show that 3 implies 1, i.e. if
N_{H}\in\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{F}_{p})
for all
H\subset G
, then
N\in\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{F}_{p}[G])
. Let
G_{p}
be a
p
-Sylow subgroup. The unit and counit for the adjunctions between restriction and induction, which agrees with co-induction, give maps

N\to\operatorname{Ind}\operatorname{Res}N\to N

with composition
\cdot[G:G_{p}]
, hence an isomorphism. Thus
N
is a summand of
\operatorname{Ind}\operatorname{Res}N
. In this manner we reduce to the case when
G
is a
p
-group. But in that case the augmentation

\mathbb{F}_{p}[G]\to\mathbb{F}_{p}

is a nilpotent thickening and hence base-change along it, whcih corresponds to
(-)_{G}
, detects perfect complexes (see again
[
14
]
Lemma 13.16, though this is well-known).∎

Proposition 8.5
.

Let
X
be a Weil-Moore anima with underlying class formation either of
\mathbb{Z}
-type,
\mathbb{R}
-type, or compact type, and set
W=\pi_{1}X
. For a prime
p
, the following properties are equivalent:

1.

If
A
denotes the sheaf of abelian groups on
\operatorname{fEt}_{X}
describing the class formation associated to
X
, then for all
Y\to X
in
\operatorname{fEt}_{X}
connected, both
A(Y)[p]
and
A(Y)/pA(Y)
are finite abelian groups; or equivalently, the derived
A(Y)/p
is finite in each degree.

2.

For all
Y\to X
finite etale connected,
R\Gamma(Y;\mathbb{F}_{p})\in\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{F}_{p})
.

3.

Let
R
be a
p
-power torsion
E_{1}
-ring and let
M\in\operatorname{Perf}(R)
with
\Omega X
-action. If we are in the
\mathbb{Z}
-case, assume that
M
descends to
B\widehat{W}
. Then
R\Gamma(X;M)\in\operatorname{Perf}(R)
.

Proof.

Suppose 1 holds, i.e.
A(Y)/p
is finite for all
Y
. Then if
F
denotes the cosheaf on
\operatorname{fEt}_{X}
giving the condensed refined class formation, then as
F(Y)
and
A(Y)[1]
only differ by
\mathbb{Z}
in degree
0
, this implies (indeed is equivalent to) the statement that
F(Y)/p
is finite. However by definition

F(Y)=R\Gamma(Y;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})^{\vee}

so that

F(Y)/p=R\Gamma(Y;\mathbb{F}_{p})^{\vee}

By Pontryagin duality we therefore see that 1
\Leftrightarrow
2. Evidently, 2 is the special case of 3 where
R=\mathbb{F}_{p}
and
M=\mathbb{F}_{p}[W/H]
as
H
runs through open subgroups of
W=\pi_{1}X
. Thus to finish proving 1
\Leftrightarrow
2
\Leftrightarrow
3 it suffices to show that 2 implies 3.

The coefficient system
M
is encoded by a condensed group homomorphism

\Omega X\to\underline{\operatorname{Aut}}_{R}(M).

Since
M
is a perfect
R
-module, the target is discrete. Thus by Remark
3.16
there is a discrete anima
A
with a map
X\to A
such that
\pi_{0}A=\ast
and
\pi_{1}A=W/N
is the quotient of
\pi_{1}X=W
by an open normal subgroup of finite index, and such that
M
descends to
A
. Thus we get

\Omega M\to\Omega A\to\operatorname{Aut}_{R}(M).

The second map corresponds to an
E_{1}
-ring homomorphism

\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[\Omega A]\to\operatorname{End}_{R}(M),

making
M
into an
R
-
\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[\Omega A]
-bimodule. By commutation of cohomology with colimits we deduce

R\Gamma(X;M)\simeq M\otimes_{\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[\Omega A]}R\Gamma(X;\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[\Omega A]).

Thus we reduce to showing that
R\Gamma(X;\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[\Omega A])\in\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[\Omega A])
. By Lemma
8.3
we can check this after base-change to
\mathbb{F}_{p}[W/N]
, and again by commutation of
R\Gamma(X;-)
with colimits of discrete coefficient systems this reduces to showing

R\Gamma(X;\mathbb{F}_{p}[W/N])\in\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{F}_{p}[W/N]).

Then by Lemma
8.4
this amounts to 2 for every finite etale
Y\to X
covered by the Galois cover corresponding to
N
, as desired.
∎

We discuss the examples from number theory, to see when this
p
-finiteness holds. Note that in verifying the
p
-finiteness for the class groups as in 1, we are free to work modulo finitely generated abelian groups and uniquely
p
-divisible groups, since those clearly have the desired mod
p
finiteness property.

Example 8.6
.

1.

Suppose
K
is a nonarchimedean local field of characteristic
0
. Modulo finitely generated abelian groups, the class group
K^{\times}
is isomorphic (via exp and log) to
\mathcal{O}_{K}
, and
\mathcal{O}_{K}
is isomorphic to a finite direct sum of copies of
\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}
where
\ell
is the residue characteristic, hence has the required mod
p
finiteness no matter what
\ell
is. More generally we can easily see that the same conclusion holds as long as the characteristic of
K
is different from
p
.

Thus, for a nonarchimedean local field
K
, the corresponding Weil-Moore anima
X_{K}
has the
p
-finiteness property whenever
K
has characteristic
\neq p
. (This is of course just a restatement of the usual finiteness of Galois cohomology in these cases.)

2.

Suppose
K
is an archimedean local field, so
K=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
. In this case the mod
p
finiteness for
K^{\times}
is obvious. Thus for the corresponding Weil-Moore anima
X_{K}
, we have the above
p
-finiteness property.

3.

Suppose
K
is a global field and
S
is a nonempty finite set of places of
F
. To verify the
p
-finiteness conditions for
X=X_{K,S}
, we have to investigate the mod
p
finiteness of the class group

A_{K,S}:=K^{\times}\backslash\mathbb{A}_{K}^{\times}/\prod_{\nu\not\in S}(K_{\nu}^{\times})^{1}.

Using the exact sequence in Example
5.16
, we see that modulo finitely generated abelian groups and uniquely divisible groups,
A_{K,S}
identifies with
\prod_{\nu\in S}K^{\times}_{\nu}
. Thus from the local case we conclude the desired mod
p
finiteness for
A_{K,S}
, and hence the
p
-finiteness property of
X_{K,S}
, as long as
K
has characteristic
\neq p
.

In applying condition 3, it is a bit annoying to have to assume the extra hypothesis that
M
comes from a
\widehat{W}
-action in the
\mathbb{Z}
-type cases. We can explain exactly when this extra hypothesis is necessary, as follows.

Lemma 8.7
.

Let
X
be Weil-Moore anima of
\mathbb{Z}
-type, so
X=BW
with

W\simeq G\times_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}}\mathbb{Z}

with
G
a profinite group of strict cohomologial dimension 2 equipped with a surjective homomorphism
G\to\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
(see Proposition
6.21
). Let

W^{1}=\operatorname{ker}(W\to\mathbb{Z})=\operatorname{ker}(G\to\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}).

Then the following conditions are equivalent.

1.

For every open subgroup
I\subset W^{1}
, we have

R\Gamma(BI;\mathbb{F}_{p})\in\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{F}_{p}).

In other words,
BW^{1}
satisfies the
p
-finiteness properties of Proposition
8.5
.

2.

For every
p
-power torsion
E_{1}
-ring
R
and every
M\in\operatorname{Perf}(R)
with
W
-action, we have

R\Gamma(X;M)\in\operatorname{Perf}(R).

That is, 3 in Proposition
8.5
holds for
X,R,M
without the assumption that
M
descends to
\widehat{X}
.

Proof.

Assume 1. Then by Proposition
8.5
applied to
X=BW^{1}
we have
R\Gamma(BW^{1};M)\in\operatorname{Perf}(R)
for all
M
and
R
as in 2. On the other hand
R\Gamma(X;M)
is the
\mathbb{Z}
-fixed points of
R\Gamma(BW^{1};M)
, hence sits in a fiber sequence with two copies of
R\Gamma(BW^{1};M)
, whence 2.

Conversely, assume 2. To show 1 it suffices to show it for
I\subset W^{1}
open and normal in
W
, as every open subgroup of
W^{1}
contains one of these. The surjection
W/I\to\mathbb{Z}
splits over
d\mathbb{Z}
for some
d>0
; if we choose such a splitting, then we can view
\mathbb{F}_{p}[W/I]\in\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{F}_{p}[d\mathbb{Z}])
. On the other hand it has the
W
-action commuting with this structure, so from 2 we get

R\Gamma(X;\mathbb{F}_{p}[W/I])\in\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{F}_{p}[d\mathbb{Z}]).

On the other hand

R\Gamma(X;\mathbb{F}_{p}[W/I])\simeq R\Gamma(BI;\mathbb{F}_{p})[-1],

giving
R\Gamma(BI;\mathbb{F}_{p})\in\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{F}_{p}[d\mathbb{Z}])
, where the
d\mathbb{Z}
-action comes from restricting the canonical
W/I
-action on
BI
to our chosen splitting.

However, note that
I
can equally well be viewed as a normal subgroup of
G=\widehat{W}
, so the action of
W/I
on
BI
factors through the profinite completion. We deduce that the action of
d\mathbb{Z}
on
BI
likewise factors through the profinite completion. In other words, if we view
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{F}_{p}[d\mathbb{Z}])
as
\mathbb{G}_{m}
, then
R\Gamma(I;\mathbb{F}_{p})
is supported at the torsion. Thus, if perfect, it must be perfect over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, showing that 2 implies 1.
∎

Example 8.8
.

Let
p
be a prime. If
X
is the Weil-Moore anima attached to a nonarchimedean local field of residue characteristic
\neq p
, or attached to a function field of characteristic
\neq p
with restricted ramification at a finite nonempty set of places, then the corresponding
BW^{1}
also has
p
-finiteness. Thus, by the above, Proposition
8.5
part 3 holds for all
M
and
R
without the technical hypothesis that
M
descend to
\widehat{X}
.

On the other hand, if
X
is the Weil-Moore anima attached to a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, then although
X
satisfies the
p
-finiteness properties of Proposition
8.5
, the corresponding
BW^{1}
does not, and therefore the technical hypothesis on
M
is necessary in 3 of Proposition
8.5
.

We recall from Remark
8.31
that if
M
comes from a global Weil-Moore repesentation, then
M
automatically descends to
\widehat{X}
, so from a global perspective this technicality is not so important. From a purely local perspective, however, it indicates that in this context (
p
-adic local fields with
p
-adic coefficients) it is better to replace Weil-Moore representations and Weil-Moore cohomology by their
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-analogs, with cohomology given by the Herr complex, as in
[
17
]
. The same remark will also apply to our discussion of Poitou-Tate duality.

Following Tate, whenever this
p
-finiteness holds we can ask about the Euler characteristic. More precisely, given a
p
-power torsion
E_{1}
-ring
R
and an
M\in\operatorname{Perf}(R)
with
\Omega X
-action (assumed to descend to
\widehat{X}
if
X
is
\mathbb{Z}
-type), we can ask to describe the class

[R\Gamma(X;M)]\in K_{0}(R).

The magic of Tate’s formulas is that in number-theoretic situations, this “Euler characteristic” only depends on the class
[M]\in K_{0}(R)
, forgetting the
\Omega X
-action. (Rather, in the original setting of Galois cohomology, this is only true up to archimedean corrections; but passing to Weil-Moore anima fixes this.) In topological situations, say for a local system on a finite CW-complex, this kind of Euler characteristic formula is simple to explain, because we can break our space up into contractible pieces. But for, e.g. a general profinite group with
p
-finiteness it can easily fail: see the treatment by Serre in his article on Euler-Poincaré measures,
[
58
]
, which analyzes this phenomenon. Here we rephrase Serre’s arguments, but avoiding the scaffolding of Euler-Poincaré measures.

Theorem 8.9
.

Let
X
be a Weil-Moore anima with underlying class formation either of
\mathbb{Z}
-type,
\mathbb{R}
-type, or compact type, and let
W=\pi_{1}X
denote “the” associated Weil group and
A
the induced class formation (a sheaf on
\operatorname{fEt}_{X}
).

Let
p
be a prime number, and assume the
p
-finiteness properties of cohomology of
X
from Proposition
8.5
are satisfied. Then for any integer
\chi\in\mathbb{Z}
, the following properties are equivalent:

1.

For every
G
-Galois cover
Y\to X
, the class of

A(Y)_{\widehat{p}}[\frac{1}{p}]

(derived
p
-completion followed by inverting
p
) in the Grothendieck group of finite dimensional
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-representations of
G
is equal to

[\mathbb{Q}_{p}]-\chi\cdot[\mathbb{Q}_{p}[G]]

(trivial representation minus
\chi
times the regular representation).

2.

For every
G
-Galois cover
Y\to X
, the class of
R\Gamma(Y;\mathbb{Q}_{p})\in\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}[G])
in
K_{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}[G])
is equal to
\chi\cdot[\mathbb{Q}_{p}[G]]
.

3.

For every
G
-Galois cover
Y\to X
, the class of
R\Gamma(Y;\mathbb{F}_{p})\in\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{F}_{p}[G])
in
K_{0}(\mathbb{F}_{p}[G])
is equal to
\chi\cdot[\mathbb{F}_{p}[G]]
.

4.

For every
p
-power torsion
E_{1}
-ring
R
and every
M\in\operatorname{Perf}(R)
with
\Omega X
-action,

[R\Gamma(X;M)]=\chi\cdot[M]\in K_{0}(R),

where if
X
is of
\mathbb{Z}
-type we assume
M
comes from a
\widehat{W}
-action.

Proof.

First we check that 1 and 2 make sense. In 1, note that the
p
-finiteness property (the derived
A(Y)/p
is finite) implies that
A(Y)_{\widehat{p}}\in\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
and hence
A(Y)_{\widehat{p}}[1/p]\in\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
, so that statement makes sense (take the Euler characteristic to get a class in
K_{0}
of finite-dimensional
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-representations of
G
). Similarly, by commutation of cohomology with colimits we have

R\Gamma(Y;\mathbb{Q}_{p})=R\Gamma(Y;\mathbb{Z}_{p})[1/p]

so that
R\Gamma(Y;\mathbb{Q}_{p})\in\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}[G])
and so the statement of 2 makes sense.

To see 1
\Leftrightarrow
2, note that since
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
has characteristic zero,
K_{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}[G])
is the same as the Grothendieck group of finite dimensional
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-representations of
G
. Because the regular representation is self-dual, 2 means that that the class of
F(Y)_{\widehat{p}}[1/p]
is equal to the class of
\chi
times the regular representation. As
F(Y)
and
A(Y)[1]
differ exactly by
\mathbb{Z}
in degree
0
, this gives 1
\Leftrightarrow
2.

That 2
\Leftrightarrow
3 follows from the following results in modular representation theory, see
[
55
]
: first,

K_{0}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}[G])\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}K_{0}(\mathbb{F}_{p}[G]);

and second

K_{0}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}[G])\hookrightarrow K_{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}[G]).

(The first fact is rather formal and holds for any
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-algebra which is finitely generated as a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-module, but the second fact is not. Ofer Gabber communicated the following example showing that the second fact can fail if
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[G]
is replaced by an arbitrary finite free
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-algebra
A
: take
A
to be the subring of 2x2-matrices over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
consisting of those matrices whose bottom-left entry is divisible by
p
. The source is
\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
and the target is
\mathbb{Z}
.)

It remains to show that 3 and 4 are equivalent. Note that 3 is the special case of 4 where
R=\mathbb{F}_{p}[W/N]
and
M=R
for open normal subgroups
N\subset W
of finite index. For the converse, assume 3 holds. As in the proof of Proposition
8.5
we can find
X\to A
with
A
discrete,
\pi_{0}A=\ast
, and
\pi_{1}A=W/N
such that
M
comes from
A
; then
M
becomes an
R
-
\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[\Omega A]
-bimodule and we have

R\Gamma(X;M)\simeq M\otimes_{\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[\Omega A]}R\Gamma(X;\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[\Omega A]).

Thus it suffices to show that

[R\Gamma(X;\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[\Omega A])]=\chi\cdot[\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[\Omega A]]\in K_{0}(\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[\Omega A]).

But isomorphism classes of finitely generated projective modules over
\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[\Omega A]
and
\mathbb{F}_{p}[W/N]
(which is its
\pi_{0}
mod
p
) are the same for elementary reasons of lifting idempotents, so we can check this on base-change to
\mathbb{F}_{p}[W/N]
where it amounts to 3, as desired.
∎

Now we investigate the examples from number theory to see that in each case the Weil-Moore anima has a well-defined Euler characteristic
\chi\in\mathbb{Z}
(in the sense that the above equivalent conditions hold).

Example 8.10
.

1.

Let
K
be a nonarchimedean local field of characteristic
0
. To investigate condition 1 for the Weil-Moore anima
X_{K}
, let
L
be a finite Galois extension of
K
; we need to understand the class in the Grothendieck group of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-linear
\operatorname{Gal}(L/K)
-representations of

V:=(L^{\times})_{\widehat{p}}[1/p]

where we recall that
(-)_{\widehat{p}}
denotes derived
p
-completion. For these purposes we can clearly work modulo finite groups and uniquely
p
-divisible groups, and we can also split short exact sequences. This gives

[L^{\times}]=[\mathbb{Z}]+[\mathcal{O}^{\times}_{L}]=[\mathbb{Z}]+[\mathcal{O}_{L}]

where in the last step we use
\operatorname{exp}
. Applying
p
-completion then inverting
p
, we get

[V]=[\mathbb{Q}_{p}]+[L]

if
F
has residue characteristic
p
and

[V]=[\mathbb{Q}_{p}]

if
F
has residue characteristic
\neq p
. By the normal basis theorem,
L
gives the regular representation as an
K
-vector space, hence gives
[K:\mathbb{Q}_{p}]
times the regular representation as a
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-vector space. Thus 1 holds with
\chi=-[K:\mathbb{Q}_{p}]
when the residue characteristic of
K
is
p
and 1 holds with
\chi=0
when the residue characteristic of
K
is
\neq p
. For simplicity we assumed
K
has characteristic zero in the above analysis, but it’s obvious that we get the same conclusion also when
K
has nonzero characteristic different from
p
.

Thus the Euler characteristic of
X_{K}
for nonarchimedean local
K
is
-[K:\mathbb{Q}_{p}]
if
K
is a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, undefined if
K
has characteristic
p
, and
0
otherwise.

2.

Let
K
be an archimedean local field. If
K\simeq\mathbb{C}
, then the Galois group is trivial, but the Euler characteristic of
(\mathbb{C}^{\times})_{\widehat{p}}[1/p]\simeq\mathbb{Q}_{p}[1]
is
-1
. We get that 1 holds with
\chi=2
and thus the Euler characteristic of
X_{\mathbb{C}}
is
2
. If
K=\mathbb{R}
, we have to do the above calculation equivariantly with respect to the Galois group. Here is one way to express it: for any algebraic closure
\mathbf{C}
of
\mathbb{R}
, we have the short exact sequence

0\to\mathbb{Z}(1)(\mathbf{C})\to\mathbf{C}\overset{\operatorname{exp}}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{C}^{\times}\to 0.

The middle term is uniquely divisible and the kernel term gives the sign representation. Thus
\mathbf{C}^{\times}
gives negative the sign representation. This means that 5 holds with
\chi=1
, so the Euler characteristic of
X_{\mathbb{R}}
is
1
.

Geometrically, of course, these are just the usual Euler characteristics of
S^{2}
and
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}
, and this archimedean version of Tate’s Euler characteristic formula follows from the fact that these spaces admit, say, triangulations (which gives a good explanation for why the Euler characteristic of a local system only depends on the dimension).

Uniformly, we can say that if
K
is an archimedean local field, then the Euler characteristic of
X_{K}
is
[K:\mathbb{R}]
(exactly the opposite sign from the case of finite extensions of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
!).

3.

Now suppose
K
is a number field and
S
is a nonempty finite set of places of
K
. To get a nice result we have to assume that
S
contains all archimedean places and all places above
p
. Again we have to take a Galois extension
L/K
, say with group
G
, and study the class group

A_{L,S}=L^{\times}\backslash\mathbb{A}_{F}^{\times}/\prod_{\mu\not\in S_{L}}\mathcal{O}_{L_{\mu}}^{\times}

after derived
p
-completing then inverting
p
, as a
G
-representation over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. We can work up to finite groups and uniquely
p
-divisible groups. In particular we are free to remove all places from
S_{L}
except those above
p
and
\infty
, and we can ignore Picard groups. Thus
[A_{L,S}]
may as well be

[(L\otimes\mathbb{R})^{\times}]+[(L\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p})^{\times}]-[\mathcal{O}_{L,S_{\infty}\cup S_{p}}^{\times}]=[(L\otimes\mathbb{R})^{\times}]+[(L\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}]-[\mathcal{O}_{L}^{\times}].

We analyze
\mathcal{O}_{L}^{\times}
using Dirichlet’s unit theorem. Actually it’s more convenient to use the following extended form. Let

\widetilde{\mathcal{O}_{L}^{\times}}=\{(u\in\mathcal{O}_{L}^{\times},x\in L\otimes\mathbb{R}):\operatorname{exp}(x)=u\otimes 1\text{ in }(L\otimes\mathbb{R})^{\times}\}.

This related to
\mathcal{O}_{L}^{\times}
as follows: the map
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}_{L}^{\times}}\to\mathcal{O}_{L}^{\times}
has image the group of units which are positive at every real place, hence its cokernel is finite; on the other hand its kernel is

\mathbb{Z}(1)(L\otimes\mathbb{R})

where
\mathbb{Z}(1)
is the sheaf on finite etale
\mathbb{R}
-algebras given by the kernel of the exponential map. Thus

[\mathcal{O}_{L}^{\times}]=[\widetilde{\mathcal{O}_{L}^{\times}}]-[\mathbb{Z}(1)(L\otimes\mathbb{R})].

On the other hand, there is the following form of Dirichlet’s unit theorem. Consider the map

\widetilde{\mathcal{O}_{L}^{\times}}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\to L\otimes\mathbb{R}

sending
((u,x),k)\mapsto(x,1\otimes k)
. This identifies the source with a full lattice inside the target. It follows that the base-change to
\mathbb{R}
of the
\mathbb{Q}
-representation
(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}_{L}^{\times}}\oplus\mathbb{Z})_{\mathbb{Q}}
identifies with the base-change to
\mathbb{R}
of the
\mathbb{Q}
-representation
L
. From general representation theory we deduce that the two
\mathbb{Q}
-representations are isomorphic (the category of finite dimensional representations of a finite group over
\mathbb{Q}
breaks up as a direct sum of categories of finite-dimensional modules over division algebras, and for those the claim is obvious). In particular the associated
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-representations are also isomorphic. Thus

[\widetilde{\mathcal{O}_{L}^{\times}}\oplus\mathbb{Z}]=[\mathcal{O}_{L}],

so that

[\mathcal{O}_{L}^{\times}]=[\mathcal{O}_{L}]-[\mathbb{Z}]-[\mathbb{Z}(1)(L\otimes\mathbb{R})],

or equivalently

[\mathcal{O}_{L}^{\times}]=[\mathcal{O}_{L}]-[\mathbb{Z}]+[(L\otimes\mathbb{R})^{\times}].

On the other hand
[(\mathcal{O}_{L}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}]=[\mathcal{O}_{L}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}]=[\mathcal{O}_{L}]
again by
p
-adic logarithm. Thus we get miraculous cancellation yielding

[A_{L,S}]=[\mathbb{Z}],

so that 5 holds with
\chi=0
. The conclusion is that
X_{K,S}
has Euler characteristic
0
.

Remark 8.11
.

Note the similarity of the above calculations to the calculations of
Herbrand quotients
as in
[
3
]
, which constitute one of the crucial inputs to the verification of the class formation axioms.

This is not exactly a coincidence. At least, we have the following general fact. If
G
is a cyclic group of order
p
and
A
is a
G
-module such that
A[p]
and
A/pA
are finite, then
\widehat{H}^{0}(G;A)
and
\widehat{H}^{1}(G;A)
are also finite, hence the Herbrand quotient

h_{A}=\#\widehat{H}^{0}(G;A)/\#\widehat{H}^{1}(G;A)

is also finite; and it it is determined by the class of
V=A_{\widehat{p}}[1/p]
in the Grothendieck group of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-representations of
G
. We leave it to the reader to make explicit exactly how.

Remark 8.12
.

We have just seen that Tate’s global Euler characteristic formula, in our setting of Weil-Moore anima, becomes the statement that
X_{K,S}
, the Weil-Moore anima with restricted ramification, has Euler characteristic
0
. This is the same answer as one gets in the function field case, but there we have a much nicer explanation, given by Kato (
[
33
]
): if
C
denotes the associated affine curve over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, then given (for example) a lisse
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
-sheaf
V
on
C
, not only is its cohomology on
C
finite dimensional, but it is also finite dimensional on
\overline{C}=C\times_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
. Then the cofiber sequence

R\Gamma(C;V)\to R\Gamma(\overline{C};V)\overset{1-\varphi}{\longrightarrow}R\Gamma(\overline{C};V)

immediately gives vanishing of the Euler characteristic over
C
.

Actually, it gives more, namely it gives a canonical trivialization of
R\Gamma(C;V)
as a point in
K(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})
. On the other hand, if it happens to be the case that
R\Gamma(C,V)=0
, then we clearly get another completely obvious trivialization. Comparing the two trivializations gives a well-defined element in
K_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})=(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})^{\times}
. Up to sign conventions, this identifies with the value of the associated L-function at
s=0
. Other L-values can be obtained by twisting by powers of the cyclotomic character. All but finitely many of such twists will have vanishing
R\Gamma
, and in this way one gets a “meromorphic function on the weight space”, and this is just the L-function of
V
.

Conjecturally, according to Kato, there should similarly be a more refined version of the calculations proving Tate’s global Euler characteristic formula, yielding not just a proof that it vanishes in K-theory, but a specific trivialization in K-theory (or at least, in the
\tau_{\leq 1}
-truncation of K-theory); moreover, after varying in a weight space which includes points where the cohomology vanishes, this should explain the existence of
p
-adic L-functions. In other words, in the language of Beilinson (
[
4
]
), Kato’s Iwasawa Main Conjecture predicts an “animation” of Tate’s Euler characteristic formula, and says that a
p
-adic L-function “is” such an animation.

Remark 8.13
.

In the
\mathbb{Z}
-case we again made the annoying assumption that the coefficient system
M
comes from a
\widehat{W}
-action. In Remark
8.7
we gave a criterion for when such an assumption can be ignored for the purposes of getting the conclusion
R\Gamma(X;M)\in\operatorname{Perf}(R)
: namely the criterion says that the “inertia group”

W^{1}=\operatorname{ker}(W\to\mathbb{Z})

should also have the
p
-finiteness property.

When this is the case, we get to ask the Euler characteristic question also for such
M
. But actually, we see directly from the argument in Remark
8.7
that under such a hypothesis we have

[R\Gamma(X;M)]=0\in K_{0}(R),

because
R\Gamma(X;M)
can be written as the fiber of a map between two copies of the same perfect
R
-module.

In particular, the
p
-finiteness property for
W^{1}
can only be satisfied in situations of zero Euler characteristic. This is in complete accordance with our discussion of number-theoretic examples above.

If
X
is a Weil-Moore anima, then by construction, the composite map

X\to BW\to BG

realizes
G
(the Galois group of the Galois category defining the class formation underlying
X
) as the profinite completion of
\pi_{1}X=W
(the Weil group). Given a coefficient system on
BG
, we can ask to compare its cohomology on
BG
(that is, the usual Galois cohomology) with the cohomology of its pullback to
X
(that is, the Weil-Moore cohomology). We will now prove results describing such a comparison in number-theoretic examples.

We start with a simple but important lemma expressing the difference between the cohomology of
X_{K,S}
and
X_{K,S^{\prime}}
when
S
and
S^{\prime}
differ by a single place.

Lemma 8.14
.

Let
K
be a number field,
S
a nonempty set of places of
K
, and
\nu
a place of
K
not in
S
such that
\nu
is either nonarchimedean or complex. Let
S^{\prime}=S\cup\{\nu\}
. Fix a commutative diagram of Weil-Moore anima

\textstyle{X_{K_{\nu}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{K,S^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{\mathcal{O}_{\nu}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{K,S}}

inducing the commutative square of refined class formations as discussed in Example
7.12
, where
X_{\mathcal{O}_{\nu}}\simeq B\mathbb{Z}
is the Weil-Moore anima for the residue field if
\nu
is nonarchimedean and
X_{\mathcal{O}_{\nu}}=B\mathbb{R}_{>0}
if
\nu
is complex.

Then for any discrete coefficient system
M
on
X_{K,S}
, the above diagram gives a pullback on
M
-cohomology.

Proof.

By devissage with the Postnikov tower and Lemma
8.27
, we reduce to the case
M=\mathbb{Z}[G_{K,S}/N]
for an open normal subgroup
N\subset G_{K,S}
of finite index. Then replacing
K
by the Galois cover corresponding to
N
we can further reduce to the case
M=\mathbb{Z}
. Since the claim with
\mathbb{R}
-coefficients is obvious from Proposition
3.6
, we are reduced to showing that the
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
-cohomology gives a pullback. But by definition this is Pontryagin dual to the claim that the class groups sit in a (derived) pushout square, which follows from the definitions.
∎

Remark 8.15
.

On the level of fundamental groups, we get a pushout of condensed groups, because
W_{K,S}=W_{K,S^{\prime}}/I_{\nu}
where
I_{\nu}
is the inertia group. Combining with the above cohomology result and an induction on the Postnikov tower, we deduce that the above commtuative square behaves like a pushout square with respect to maps to discrete anima. It also behaves like a pushout square with respect to maps to condensed anima with compact Hausdorff homotopy, by an analogous argument.

It seems that, even though the above square is (presumably?) not literally a pushout of condensed anima, it at least behaves like one with respect to maps to most targets. Intuitively,
X_{K,S}
is obtained from
X_{K,S^{\prime}}
by “filling in the puncture at
\nu
”.

Remark 8.16
.

The analog of the above result also holds for Galois cohomology, in a suitable context: suppose
p
is a prime,
K
is a number field,
S
a finite set of places of
K
containing all archimedean places and all places above
p
, and let
M
be a
p
-primary torsion discrete
G_{K,S}
-module. If
\nu
is a place of
K
not in
S
and we set
S^{\prime}=S\cup\{\nu\}
, then we have a pullback diagram

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 38.21219pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-36.85976pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{R\Gamma(BG_{K,S};M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 62.21219pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 62.21219pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{R\Gamma(BG_{K_{\nu}};M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 97.53885pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-38.21219pt\raise-31.94446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{R\Gamma(BG_{K,S^{\prime}};M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 63.4762pt\raise-31.94446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 63.4762pt\raise-31.94446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{R\Gamma(BG_{\kappa_{\nu}};M)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

where
\kappa_{\nu}
denotes the residue field. Indeed, if we take etale cohomology instead of Galois cohomology everywhere, this is a straightforward consequence of standard gluing triangles (plus the fact that the etale site of
K_{\nu}
agrees with that of its henselian analog, which is a consequence of Krasner’s lemma). On the other hand etale cohomology and Galois cohomology agree in the given situation by
[
42
]
Proposition 2.9.

Now we can compare Galois cohomology to Weil-Moore cohomology. We will see that the difference is concentrated at the archimedean places.

The local case is simple.

Lemma 8.17
.

For any
\mathbb{Z}
-type Weil-Moore anima
X
with
G=\widehat{\pi_{1}X}
, the comparison map

R\Gamma(BG;M)\to R\Gamma(X;M)

is an isomorphism for all discrete torsion
G
-modules
M
.

Proof.

Recall that in this case,
X=BW
, and

W=G\times_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}}\mathbb{Z}

for some surjection
G\twoheadrightarrow\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
. Thus we reduce to the familiar claim that the map
B\mathbb{Z}\to B\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
induces an isomorphism on cohomology for all discrete torsion coefficient systems.
∎

Remark 8.18
.

Note that this also shows that
\widehat{BW}=B\widehat{W}
in this setting.

Now we handle the global case.

Theorem 8.19
.

Let
p
be a prime,
K
a number field, and
S
a set of places of
K
containing all archimedean places and all places above
p
. Fix choices for global Weil-Moore anima
X_{K,S}
and local Weil-Moore anima
X_{K_{\nu}}
for
\nu|\infty
together with the local-global maps

X_{K_{\nu}}\to X_{K,S}

as discussed in Example
7.12
. Note that by construction this map lives over the analogous natural map

BG_{K_{\nu}}\to BG_{K,S}

of Galois groupoids.

Then for any
p
-primary torsion discrete
G_{K,S}
-module
M
, the resulting commutative square

\textstyle{R\Gamma(BG_{K,S};M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\prod_{\nu\mid\infty}R\Gamma(BG_{K_{\nu}};M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R\Gamma(X_{K,S};M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\prod_{\nu\mid\infty}R\Gamma(X_{K_{\nu}};M)}

is a pullback.

Proof.

By devissage and use of filtered colimits we can reduce to the case
M=\mathbb{F}_{p}[G_{K,S}/N]
for open normal
N\subset G_{K,S}
. Replacing
K
by the Galois extension corresponding to
N
it suffices to treat
M=\mathbb{F}_{p}
. Moreover, shrinking
N
if necessary we can always assume that all infinite places of
K
are complex. Thus we need to show that if
p
is a prime and
K
is a number field with no real places, then

R\Gamma(BG_{K,S};\mathbb{F}_{p})\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}R\Gamma(X_{K,S};\mathbb{F}_{p})\times_{\prod_{\nu|\infty}R\Gamma(X_{K_{\nu}};\mathbb{F}_{p})}\prod_{\nu|\infty}\mathbb{F}_{p}.

If we compare with the case of Lemma
8.14
where
\nu
is complex, we find that this is also equivalent to showing that

R\Gamma(BG_{K,S};\mathbb{F}_{p})\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}R\Gamma(X_{K,S\cap S_{f}};\mathbb{F}_{p}),

where
S_{f}
denotes the set of all nonarchimedean places.

Next let us reduce to the case where
S
is the set of all places of
K
. For this, note that if we compare Lemma
8.14
with Remark
8.16
and Lemma
8.17
, we see that for
S
finite, the statements for
S
and
S\cup\{\nu\}
are equivalent for any
\nu
not in
S
. It follows readily that the statements for any two finite sets
S
and
S^{\prime}
are equivalent (meaning that the homotopy fibers of the comparison maps are isomorphic via the obvious natural map when
S\subset S^{\prime}
). Passing to the colimit we deduce the same also for infinite sets, whence the desired reduction.

Thus we can assume
S
is the set of all places. The cohomology on the right is given by global sections of the derived sheaf

(F/p)^{\vee}

where
F
is the cosheaf giving the refined class formation,
(-)/p
means derived modding out by
p
, and
(-)^{\vee}
means Pontryagin duality. This receives a map from

\mathbb{F}_{p}=(\mathbb{Z}/p)^{\vee}

via the augmentation
\alpha:F\to\mathbb{Z}
, and on hypercohomology this map implements the comparison map we’re trying to prove is an isomorphism. Thus, it suffices to show that the sheafification of the fiber of the map of presheaves
\mathbb{F}_{p}\to(F/p)^{\vee}
vanishes. But this fiber is

(A/p)^{\vee},

where
A
is the copresheaf giving the class groups. Thus, to prove the result, what we need to show is that when we take the colimit of these values
(A/p)^{\vee}
along all extensions
L
of
K
inside a fixed algebraic closure, we get
0
.

Recall that these class groups are given by

A(L)=(L^{\times}\backslash\mathbb{A}_{L}^{\times})/\prod_{\mu\mid\infty}(L_{\mu}^{\times})^{1}.

Note that the cokernel of multiplication by
p
on
A(L)
is profinite (on the level of
S
-class groups for finite
S
it is finite as we saw above, and then we take the inverse limit). Thus, we deduce from the existence theorem that the inverse limit of
A(L)/pA(L)
gives
0
. We are therefore reduced to checking that the inverse limit of the
p
-torsion in
A(L)
gives
0
. For this, consider the short exact sequence

0\to L^{\times}\to\oplus_{\mu|\infty}\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{A}_{L,f}^{\times}\to A(L)\to 0.

By the snake lemma we deduce that suffices to show the following two claims:

1.

\varprojlim_{L}\operatorname{ker}\left(L^{\times}\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}\to\prod_{\mu\in S_{f}}L_{\mu}^{\times}\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}\right)=0
.

2.

\varprojlim_{L}\left(\prod_{\mu\in S_{f}}\mu_{p}(L_{\mu})\right)=0
.

For 1, in fact the kernel vanishes for fixed
L
, without needing to pass to the inverse limit, by the Grunwald-Wang theorem. For 2, note that if
E^{\prime}/E
is any field extension of degree divisible by
p
, then the norm map
\mu_{p}(E^{\prime})\to\mu_{p}(E)
is
0
. Thus it suffices to show (up to replacing
L
by an extension) that for all
\mu\in S_{f}
there is an extension
L^{\prime}/L
whose local degree at
\mu
is divisible by
p
. This can be arranged by taking a sufficiently large
p
-cyclotomic extension.
∎

Remark 8.20
.

In the above pullback square, we took the product only over the archimedean places in
S
. But actually we can also throw in as many non-archimedean places as we like and the statement will still hold. This is immediate from Lemma
8.17
. In particular, taking all places in
S
, we can interpret Theorem
8.19
as saying that the compactly supported Galois cohomology is the same as the compactly supported Weil-Moore cohomology.

More precisely, if
p
is a prime,
K
is a number field, and
S
is a finite set of places containing all archimedean and
p
-adic places, then

R\Gamma_{c}(BG_{K,S};M)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}R\Gamma_{c}(X_{K,S};M)

for all
p
-primary torsion
G_{K,S}
-modules
M
, where

R\Gamma_{c}(BG_{K,S};M)=\operatorname{Fib}\left(R\Gamma(BG_{K,S};M)\to\prod_{\nu\in S}R\Gamma(BG_{K_{\nu}};M)\right)

and

R\Gamma_{c}(X_{K,S};M)=\operatorname{Fib}\left(R\Gamma(X_{K,S};M)\to\prod_{\nu\in S}R\Gamma(X_{K_{\nu}};M)\right).

Remark 8.21
.

There is an obvious
\pi_{\leq 1}
-level analog of this statement: for any discrete groupoid
Y
, we have

\operatorname{Map}(BG_{K,S},Y)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\operatorname{Map}(X_{K,S},Y)\times_{\prod_{\nu}\operatorname{Map}(X_{K_{\nu}},Y)}\prod_{\nu}\operatorname{Map}(BG_{K_{\nu}},Y).
