Inducting on the Postnikov tower, we deduce the same statement for
Y
any discrete anima whose higher homotopy groups are
p
-primary torsion. In this sense we can say that
BG_{K,S}
(which is the usual etale homotopy type of
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{K,S})
, at least as far as
p
-primary coefficients are concerned) is obtained from
X_{K,S}
by “filling in the archimedean punctures” (by coning off
S^{2}
to a point for complex places, and coning off
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}
to the orbifold point
BC_{2}
at real places). I thank Jacob Lurie for suggesting this interpretation.

The last result we want to discuss is Poitou-Tate duality. But it will help to set up a bit of formalism of coefficient objects first. We will work in the
\mathbb{Z}
-linear context, and we take the following definition.

Definition 8.22
.

Let
X
be a Weil-Moore anima, or more generally a condensed anima with
\pi_{0}X=\ast
.

1.

Define a
condensed coefficient system
on
X
to be an object
M
in the
\infty
-category
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{CondAn}_{/X})
of unbounded derived abelian group objects in the slice topos
\operatorname{CondAn}_{/X}
; that is,
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{CondAn}_{/X})
is
\infty
-category of small
\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})
-valued sheaves on the site
\operatorname{Extr}_{/X}
of extremally disconnected profinite sets with a map to
X
.

2.

Define a
discrete coefficient system
to be a condensed coefficient system such that the object on
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{CondAb})
obtained by pulling back under (any choice of)
\ast\to X
is discrete, i.e. lies in
\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})\subset\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{CondAb})
.

Denote the full subcategory of discrete coefficient systems by

\operatorname{D}_{et}(X;\mathbb{Z})\subset\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{CondAn}_{/X}).

Remark 8.23
.

If we fix
\ast\to X
, giving the condensed group
\Omega X
, then
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{CondAn}_{/X})
is the same as the
\infty
-category of
\Omega X
-equivariant derived condensed abelian groups

\operatorname{D}_{\Omega X}(\operatorname{CondAb})

which we considered in Section
2
. Thus, informally,
\operatorname{D}_{et}(X;\mathbb{Z})
is the
\infty
-category of discrete derived abelian groups with continuous
\Omega X
-action.

Remark 8.24
.

By general topos theory,
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{CondAn}_{/X})
has a natural t-structure, has all colimits, and has a natural tensor product; moreover all of these features are preserved under pullback, in particular under the map
\ast\to X
. Note that, over
\ast
, all these operations preserve the full subcategory
\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})\subset\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{CondAn})
. It follows that
\operatorname{D}_{et}(X;\mathbb{Z})
is closed under colimits, tensor products, and t-truncations inside
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{CondAn}_{/X})
, and that all these operations commute with pullback along
\ast\to X
viewed as a conservative functor

\operatorname{D}_{et}(X;\mathbb{Z})\to\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z}).

The heart of
\operatorname{D}_{et}(X;\mathbb{Z})
identifies with the abelian category of discrete abelian groups with continuous
\pi_{1}X=W
-action.

Remark 8.25
.

If we write
X
as a (small) colimit of extremally disconnected profinite sets, we can view
\operatorname{D}_{et}(X;\mathbb{Z})
as the corresponding limit of the usual (unbounded derived) sheaf categories on profinite sets. We will not use this remark, except perhaps to note that
\operatorname{D}_{et}(X;\mathbb{Z})
is a presentable
\infty
-category.

Remark 8.26
.

Suppose
R
is an
E_{1}
-
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra and
M\in\operatorname{D}(R)
. By definition, to give an
N\in\operatorname{Mod}_{R}(\operatorname{D}_{et}(X;\mathbb{Z}))
whose pullback along
\ast\to X
gives
M
is the same as to give a pointed map of condensed anima

X\to B\operatorname{Aut}_{R}(M)

where
\operatorname{Aut}_{R}(M)
is the
condensed
automorphism object of
M
as an
R
-module. If
M\in\operatorname{Perf}(R)
, however, then
\operatorname{Aut}_{R}(M)
is discrete and we are in the realm of maps from
X
to discrete anima. For example, if
R
is an animated commutative ring, then local systems of
R
-modules on
X
of rank
d
can either be understood as objects in
\operatorname{Mod}_{R}(\operatorname{D}_{et}(X;\mathbb{Z}))
whose underlying object in
\operatorname{D}(R)
lies in
\operatorname{Vect}_{d}(R)
or as being classified by maps of condensed anima

X\to B\operatorname{GL}_{d}(R)

where the target is discrete.

We have the following basic results allowing to work with these coefficient systems; compare with the set-up in
[
1
]
Appendix E.

Lemma 8.27
.

Let
X
be a Weil-Moore anima, with underlying class formation of
\mathbb{Z}
-type,
\mathbb{R}
-type, or compact type. Then:

1.

The global sections functor
\operatorname{D}_{et}(X;\mathbb{Z})\to\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{CondAb})
lands in
\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})
and preserves colimits.

2.

For every connected finite etale
Y\to X
, the object
\mathbb{Z}[h_{Y}]\in\operatorname{D}_{et}(X;\mathbb{Z})
corepresenting sections over
Y
is a compact object. If
Y\to X
corresponds to the open subgroup
H\subset W=\pi_{1}X
of finite index, then
\mathbb{Z}[h_{Y}]
lives in degree
0
and corresponds to
\mathbb{Z}[W/H]
as a
W
-module.

3.

Denote by
\operatorname{Lisse}(X)\subset\operatorname{D}_{et}(X;\mathbb{Z})
the thick subcategory generated by the objects
\mathbb{Z}[h_{Y}]
as above. Then
M\in\operatorname{Lisse}(X)
if and only if
H_{i}M=0
for all but finitely many
i
, and
H_{i}M
corresponds to a finitely generated discrete abelian group with continuous
\widehat{W}
-action for all
i
.

4.

The induced colimit-preserving functor

\operatorname{Ind}(\operatorname{Lisse}(X))\to\operatorname{D}_{et}(X;\mathbb{Z})

is fully faithful.

5.

\operatorname{Ind}(\operatorname{Lisse}(X))
inherits the t-structure from
\operatorname{D}_{et}(X;\mathbb{Z})
, i.e. the essential image of the functor in 4 is closed under t-truncation.

6.

Suppose
X
is either compact-type or
\mathbb{R}
-type. Then the full subcategories of
t_{\leq n}
-truncated objects in
\operatorname{Ind}(\operatorname{Lisse}(X))
and
\operatorname{D}_{et}(X;\mathbb{Z})
are identified via the above fully faithful functor, and (hence)
\operatorname{D}_{et}(X;\mathbb{Z})
is the left-completion of
\operatorname{Ind}(\operatorname{Lisse}(X))
.

7.

Suppose
X
is of profinite type (meaning, the class groups in the underlying class formation are profinite abelian groups, or equivalently
X\simeq BW
where
W
is profinite). Then
\operatorname{Ind}(\operatorname{Lisse}(X))=\operatorname{D}_{et}(X;\mathbb{Z})
.

Proof.

For 1, let us first check the claim that

\Gamma(X;M)\in\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})

for all
M\in\operatorname{D}_{et}(X;\mathbb{Z})
. When
M
is in degree
0
, this is given by Lemma
3.6
. The claim follows by devissage when
M
lives in finitely many degrees. Next suppose
M
is connective. We have

\Gamma(X;M)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\varprojlim_{n}\Gamma(X;\tau_{\leq n}M).

By the cohomological dimension estimate in Theorem
8.1
, this limit stabilizes in any given degree, so we deduce the claim for such
M
. (If
X
is
\mathbb{Z}
-type but does not satisfy the technical hypothesis in Theorem
8.1
, we still get finite cohomological dimension, just with bound worse by one.) Finally, if
M
is arbitrary, then

\Gamma(X;\tau_{\geq d}M)\to\Gamma(X;M)

is an isomorphism in degrees
\geq d
so we reduce to the connective case by varying
d
(and shifting).

Next we similarly show that
\Gamma(X;-)
preserves colimits on discrete coefficient systems. It suffices to consider direct sums. By means of truncation and by the finite cohomological dimension property, we reduce to the case where all the objects are in degree
0
, in which case we get the claim from Lemma
3.6
.

For 2, the claim that
\mathbb{Z}[h_{Y}]
is compact is immediate from 1 (applied to
Y
, which is also a Weil-Moore anima). For the claim that
\mathbb{Z}[h_{Y}]
lives in degree
0
and is described as claimed, we can even show this for the object corepresenting global sections over
Y
for all condensed coefficient systems on
X
: it will a fortiori live in the discrete full subcategory. Namely, this
\mathbb{Z}[h_{Y}]
is given by the value on the constant sheaf
\mathbb{Z}
of the left adjoint to pullback from
X
to
Y
. Such left adjoints satisfy base-change by topos nonsense, so the pullback to
\ast\to X
identifies with the analogous object for the pullback
Y\times_{X}\ast\to\ast
, which is just the finite set
W/H
. The claim follows.

For 3, the collection of finitely generated discrete
\widehat{W}
-modules in degree
0
is an abelian subcategory closed under extensions (inside all continuous discrete
W
-modules, which is the heart of
\operatorname{D}_{et}(X;\mathbb{Z})
), which shows that the claimed collection of
M
is a thick subcategory. As by 2 each
\mathbb{Z}[h_{Y}]
lies in the claimed collection, we deduce one containement. For the other containment, by devissage it suffices to show that every finitely generated discrete
\widehat{W}
-module
M
lies in the thick subcategory generated by the
\mathbb{Z}[W/H]
(inside
\operatorname{D}_{et}(X;\mathbb{Z})
). By finite generation, there is an open normal subgroup
N\subset W
of finite index which acts trivially on
M
. Then we can make a (possibly infinite-length) free resolution of
M
by finitely generated free
\mathbb{Z}[W/N]
-modules. In
\operatorname{D}_{et}(X;\mathbb{Z})
this identifies
M
with the filtered colimit of the stupid truncations of this resolution. However, we claim that
M
is a compact object, which will imply that
M
is a retract of one of these truncations and hence lies in the claimed thick subcategory. To prove the compactness, note that the object of
\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})
obtained by pulling
M
back along
\ast\to X
lies in
\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{Z})
; it follows that
M
is dualizable under the tensor product, and therefore compactness of
M
follows from compactness of the unit, given by 1.

Claim 4 is a formal consequence: compact objects form a thick subcategory, hence every object in
\operatorname{Lisse}(X)
is compact, giving the fully faithful embedding as claimed.

For 5, we need to see that if
M\in\operatorname{Ind}(\operatorname{Lisse}(X))
, then
\tau_{[n,m]}M\in\operatorname{Ind}(\operatorname{Lisse}(X))
for all
n\leq m
in
\mathbb{Z}
. Because the t-structure on
\operatorname{D}_{et}(X;\mathbb{Z})
is compatible with filtered colimits, it suffices to show that
\operatorname{Lisse}(X)
is closed under truncations. But this follows from 3.

For 6, by compatibility with filtered colimits, it suffices to show that every object in the heart of
\operatorname{D}_{et}(\mathbb{Z})
lies in
\operatorname{Ind}(\operatorname{Lisse}(X))
. But this follows because the connected component
W^{\circ}
acts trivially on every discrete module, and
W/W^{\circ}\simeq\widehat{W}
.

For 7, it suffices to show that if
M\in\operatorname{D}_{et}(X;\mathbb{Z})
satisfies that
R\Gamma(Y;M)=0
for all finite etale
Y\to X
, then
M=0
. But because
X=BW
with
W
profinite, the the inverse limit over all such
Y
with a compatible map from
\ast\to X
gives
\ast
, so on taking
M
-cohomology we deduce that the pullback of
M
to
\ast
is the filtered colimit of the
\Gamma(Y;M)
hence is
0
as desired.
∎

Remark 8.28
.

The above result expresses that
\operatorname{Ind}(\operatorname{Lisse}(X))
is quite close to
\operatorname{D}_{et}(X;\mathbb{Z})
. However, outside the profinite case (or the
\mathbb{R}
-type case with profinite maximal compact subgroup, which is equivalent with respect to discrete coefficients) it is unlikely that

\operatorname{Ind}(\operatorname{Lisse}(X))\to\operatorname{D}_{et}(X;\mathbb{Z})

can be an isomorphism. For example, if
X
is the Weil-Moore anima of
\mathbb{C}
, then this is equivalent to the question of whether the full subcategory of the derived category of sheaves on
S^{2}
generated under colimits and shifts by the constant sheaf
\mathbb{Z}
identifies with the full subcategory spanned by those derived sheaves with constant homology sheaves. The answer is negative as is explained by a simple Koszul duality argument in
[
1
]
E.2.6.

Recall from Proposition
3.18
the notion of profinite completion of a condensed anima. We now analyze the difference between
D_{et}(-;\mathbb{Z})
for
X
and for its profinite completion
\widehat{X}
. The relation is particularly simple for torsion coefficients.

Proposition 8.29
.

Let
X
be a Weil-Moore anima of
\mathbb{Z}
-type,
\mathbb{R}
-type, or compact type, and let
\widehat{X}
be its profinite completion. For a presentable stable
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
, let
\mathcal{C}_{tors}
denote the torsion full subcategory, consisting of those
X\in\mathcal{C}
such that
X\otimes\mathbb{Q}=0
. Then the pullback

\operatorname{D}_{et}(\widehat{X};\mathbb{Z})_{tors}\to\operatorname{D}_{et}(X;\mathbb{Z})_{tors}

is fully faithful. Moreover, the essential image consists of those
M\in\operatorname{D}_{et}(X;\mathbb{Z})_{tors}
such that
H_{n}M
, which is a discrete torsion
W
-module, comes from a
\widehat{W}
-module, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. In particular, the above pullback functor is an equivalence if
X
is compact-type or
\mathbb{R}
-type.

Proof.

First we show that if
A
is a torsion abelian group with continuous
\widehat{W}=\pi_{1}X
-action, then

R\Gamma(\widehat{X};A)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}R\Gamma(X;A).

Cohomology commutes with filtered colimits on both
X
and
\widehat{X}
by Lemma
3.6
, so we can reduce to the case where
A
is finite and is trivial on some finite Galois cover of
\widehat{X}
. Finite Galois covers of
X
and
\widehat{X}
correspond via
Y\mapsto\widehat{Y}
, and if
G
is the (finite) Galois group then
Y/G=X
and
\widehat{Y}/G=\widehat{X}
. Thus the claim reduces to the case of constant finite coefficients, which follows from the definition of profinite completion.

From this fact, we deduce in particular that
\widehat{X}
has finite cohomological dimension with torsion coefficients (as we know the same for
X
). The same holds for any finite etale cover, because it is an instance of the same fact. Thus by the same argument as in Lemma
8.27
, we get

\operatorname{Ind}(\operatorname{Lisse}_{tors}(\widehat{X}))\hookrightarrow\operatorname{D}_{et}(\widehat{X};\mathbb{Z})_{tors}

where
\operatorname{Lisse}_{tors}(\widehat{X})
is the thick subcategory generated by the
\mathbb{F}_{p}[h_{Y}]
for
Y\to\widehat{X}
finite etale and varying primes
p
. We also get that this fully faithful inclusion identifies
\operatorname{D}_{et}(\widehat{X};\mathbb{Z})_{tors}
with the left t-completion of
\operatorname{Ind}(\operatorname{Lisse}_{tors}(\widehat{X}))
(which is closed under t-truncation). Comparing with
\operatorname{Ind}(\operatorname{Lisse}(X))\hookrightarrow\operatorname{D}_{et}(X;\mathbb{Z})
, we see that to prove the fully faithfulness it suffices to show that Homs over
\widehat{X}
and
X
match between objects in
\operatorname{Lisse}_{tors}(\widehat{X})
. By dualizability we reduce to Homs from the unit, where the claim amounts to the cohomology comparison already given above.

For the claim about the essential image, note that the fully faithful functor

\operatorname{D}_{et}(\widehat{X};\mathbb{Z})_{tors}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{D}_{et}(X;\mathbb{Z})_{tors},

being induced by a pullback map, is t-exact. It follows that an object lies in the essential image if and only if each of its homology objects does, and the claim then follows readily.
∎

Corollary 8.30
.

Suppose
X
and
Y
are Weil-Moore anima such that
Y
is of
\mathbb{R}
-type or compact type. Then for every map
f:X\to Y
and every
M\in\operatorname{D}_{et}(Y;\mathbb{Z})_{tors}
, the pullback
f^{\ast}M
uniquely descends to an object in
\operatorname{D}_{et}(\widehat{X};\mathbb{Z})_{tors}
.

Proof.

By the above,
M
uniquely descends to
\widehat{Y}
, and then the claim follows from functoriality.
∎

Remark 8.31
.

In particular, this applies when
Y
is the Weil-Moore anima of a number field and
X
is the Weil-Moore anima of one of its nonarchimedean local fields. The conclusion is that a global Weil-Moore representation with discrete torsion (possibly derived) coefficients always naturally restricts to a local
Galois
representation, not just a local Weil group representation. This claim clearly does not hold with
\mathbb{C}
-coefficients.

Corollary 8.32
.

Suppose
X
and
Y
are Weil-Moore anima such that
Y
is of compact type or
\mathbb{R}
-type. Then for any map
f:X\to Y
, the pullback functor

f^{\ast}:\operatorname{D}_{et}(Y;\mathbb{Z})_{tors}\to\operatorname{D}_{et}(X;\mathbb{Z})_{tors},

up to homotopy, only depends on the map of condensed refined class formations induced by
f
.

Proof.

By the above,
f^{\ast}
factors through the profinite completion, so this follows from Remark
7.13
.
∎

This means that for the purposes of discrete torsion coefficients, we can ignore the possible non-uniqueness up to homotopy of Weil-Moore maps. (We recall that in number-theoretic examples, whenever
Y
is of
\mathbb{Z}
-type then so is
X
, and hence we are anyway able to aovid the nonuniqueness of Weil-Moore maps, even before passing to profinite completion.)

Finally, we turn to duality. We aim to prove a version of Poitou-Tate duality for Weil-Moore anima. We start with local fields, where in the nonarchimedean case the result is of course just equivalent to usual local Tate duality by Lemma
8.17
. We will give a proof from the Weil-Moore perspective, as a warm-up for the global case. The key thing to understand turns out to be the following: if
F
is the cosheaf giving the refined class formation, then there are two natural ways to build a sheaf from
F
, namely taking by applying the covariant operation
(-)^{tr}
or by applying the contravariant operation
(-)^{\vee}
, and at the level of linear algebra local Tate duality will amount to the assertion that mod
p
, the two resulting sheaves are isomorphic up to a twist and shift. But we will set things up in a more standard way.

Theorem 8.33
.

Let
p
be a prime and
K
a local field of characteristic
\neq p
. Let
X=BW
be a Weil-Moore anima for
K
. Then:

1.

There is a natural map

[X]:R\Gamma(X;\left(\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}\right)(1)[2])\to\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}

which is an isomorphism in degree
0
, the bottom (homological) degree.

2.

For every
p
-power torsion
E_{1}
-
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra
R
and every
M\in\operatorname{Perf}(R)
with
W
-action, assumed to come from a
\widehat{W}
-action if
K
is nonarchimedean, the
R
-
R
-bimodule map

R\Gamma(X;M(1)[2])\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}R\Gamma(X;M^{\vee})\to R\Gamma(X;R(1)[2])\overset{[X]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}R[-1]}{\longrightarrow}R

induces an isomorphism

R\Gamma(X;M^{\vee})\simeq R\Gamma(X;M(1)[2])^{\vee}

in
\operatorname{Perf}(R^{op})
, where
(-)^{\vee}
is the functor of left
R
-linear maps to
R
, viewed as landing in
\operatorname{Perf}(R^{op})
via the right
R
-module structure on
R
.

Proof.

Consider the sheaf on
\operatorname{fEt}_{X}
given by

\mathcal{F}:=\left(R\Gamma(-;(\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})(1)[2])^{\vee}\right)^{tr},

so that, in terms of the fundamental cosheaf
F
from the refined class formation, we have

\mathcal{F}=(F^{tr})_{\widehat{p}}(-1)[-2].

By construction of the class formation the (unsheafified)
H_{0}(F^{tr})
is
\mathbb{Z}^{tr}
and
H_{1}(F^{tr})
is
\mathbb{G}_{m}
and all the other
H_{i}
are
0
. Thus,
(F^{tr})_{\widehat{p}}
has top nonvanishing homology group given by
H_{2}=\mathbb{Z}_{p}(1)
. Tate untwisting and shifting down by two, we deduce that

H_{0}\mathcal{F}=\mathbb{Z}_{p}

and that this is the top nonvanishing homology group sheaf of
\mathcal{F}
. In particular,
1\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
corresponds to a canonical map

[X]:R\Gamma(X;(\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})(1)[2])\to\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}

which is an isomorphism in bottom degree
0
by Pontryagin duality. This proves 1.

For 2, in the non-archimedean case let us replace
X
by
\widehat{X}
, so that in any case we are either
\mathbb{R}
-type or compact type. Let

\Gamma^{!}:\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})[p^{\infty}]\to\operatorname{D}_{et}(X;\mathbb{Z})[p^{\infty}]

be the right adjoint to the global sections functor in the opposite direction (on
p
-primary torsion discrete derived coefficient systems). We claim that the map

(\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})(1)[2]\to\Gamma^{!}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})

adjoint to the map of 1 is an isomorphism, or equivalently (checking mod
p
and twisting) that the induced map

\mathbb{F}_{p}\to(\Gamma^{!}\mathbb{F}_{p})(-1)[-2]

is an isomorphism.

To prove this, note that
\Gamma^{!}\mathbb{F}_{p}
is bounded above in the t-structure by the cohomological dimension estimate in Lemma
8.1
. Thus, by Lemma
8.27
the above map is an isomorphism if and only if induces an isomorphism of sheaves on
\operatorname{fEt}_{X}
when we map in from
\mathbb{F}_{p}[h_{Y}]
for arbitrary
Y\to X
finite etale. When we implement this, by Lemma
8.37
the object
\Gamma^{!}\mathbb{F}_{p}(-1)[-2]
gives rise to the sheaf on
\operatorname{fEt}_{X}
given by

Y\mapsto(R\Gamma(Y;\mathbb{F}_{p}(1)[2])^{\vee})^{tr},

or in other words, in terms of the fundamental cosheaf
F
coming from the refined class formation,

Y\mapsto F^{tr}(Y)/p(-1)[-2],

which is exactly the mod
p
version the
\mathcal{F}
we analyzed above. Moreover, the map
\mathbb{F}_{p}\to(\Gamma^{!}\mathbb{F}_{p})(-1)[-2]
is we are trying to show is an isomorphism is just selecting a global section of this sheaf on
\operatorname{fEt}_{X}
, and this global section is the one we wrote down above in the proof of 1.

On the other hand, the source
\mathbb{F}_{p}
gives rise to the sheaf on
\operatorname{fEt}_{X}
given by

Y\mapsto R\Gamma(Y;\mathbb{F}_{p}),

which in terms of
F
is

Y\mapsto(F/p)^{\vee}.

By construction, this map of sheaves on
\operatorname{fEt}_{X}

R\Gamma(-;\mathbb{F}_{p})\to(R\Gamma(-;\mathbb{F}_{p}(1)[2])^{\vee})^{tr}

is an isomorphism in degree
0
on global sections, and we want to prove it’s an isomorphism. We will do this by seeing that it’s an isomorphism on stalks.

Consider the natural map

\mathbb{F}_{p}\to(F/p)^{\vee}

induced by
\alpha:F\to\mathbb{Z}
, whose fiber is given by the (derived)
p
-torsion in the Pontryagin dual to the class group. By the existence theorem, when we pass to stalks we can replace the class group by its connected component. Thus in the nonarchimedean case
\mathbb{F}_{p}\to(F/p)^{\vee}
is an isomorphism on stalks, while in the archimedean case we get that the stalks of
(F/p)^{\vee}
are given by
\mathbb{F}_{p}
in degree
0
and
\mathbb{F}_{p}(-1)
in degree
-2
. We can likewise analyze
F^{tr}/p(-1)[-2]
. When
F
is nonarchimedean the sheafified
H_{0}F^{tr}
is
\mathbb{Q}
so doesn’t contribute mod
p
; moreover multiplication by
p
on
H_{1}F=\mathbb{G}_{m}
is surjective on stalks because
\operatorname{char}(F)\neq p
. Thus we get that for the stalks of
F^{tr}/p(-1)[-2]
there is only the
\mathbb{F}_{p}
in degree
0
we already discussed at the beginning of this proof. But when
F
is archimedean we additionally get an
\mathbb{F}_{p}(-1)
in degree
-2
(if we normalize so that
H_{0}F^{tr}=\mathbb{Z}
, and not
\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
, on the complex numbers).

In the nonarchimedean case, we are done: a map from
\mathbb{F}_{p}
to itself is either
0
or an isomorphism, but our map was nonzero on global sections by construction. Thus it must be an isomorphism.

In the archimedean case, because we are checking on stalks we reduce to the case
K=\mathbb{C}
. Then the Tate twist and the
(-)^{tr}
operation can be ignored, and our map is simply a map

R\Gamma(X;\mathbb{F}_{p})\to R\Gamma(X;\mathbb{F}_{p}[2])^{\vee}

induced by cup product followed by a nonzero linear form on
H^{2}
. Because of the simple structure of cohomology (nonzero except in degrees
0
and
2
, where it’s one-dimensional) this forces the map to be an isomorphism, as desired.

To sum up, we have shown that the map in 1 induces an isomorphism

(\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})(1)[2]\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Gamma^{!}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}).

Next we claim that

\Gamma^{!}:\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})[p^{\infty}]\to\operatorname{D}_{et}(X;\mathbb{Z})[p^{\infty}]

preserves all colimits, or equivalently that the natural map

M\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Gamma^{!}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}[-1])\to\Gamma^{!}(M)

is an isomorphism for all
M\in\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})[p^{\infty}]
. We can again test mod
p
and thus reduce to showing

M\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\Gamma^{!}(\mathbb{F}_{p})\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Gamma^{!}(M)

for all
M\in\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{F}_{p})
. Because
\Gamma^{!}(\mathbb{F}_{p})
(as calculated above) is just a shift and a twist, the functor on the left commutes with the inverse limit of the Postnikov tower of
M
; the functor on the right evidently does as well because
\Gamma^{!}
is a right adjoint. Thus we can reduce to the case of
M
bounded above, when the left-hand side is also bounded above. Then again by Lemma
8.27
we can test on maps from objects of the form
\mathbb{F}_{p}[h_{Y}]
for
Y\to X
finite etale, in which case the claim follows from the mod
p
finiteness property of
Y
from Proposition
8.5
.

To sum up once more, we have shown that

\Gamma^{!}(M)\simeq M(1)[2]

for all
M\in\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})[p^{\infty}]
. As both
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{!}
preserve colimits, the adjunction between them passes to
R
-module objects for all
E_{1}
-algebras
R
in
\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})[p^{\infty}]
, i.e. all
p
-primary torsion
E_{1}
-
\mathbb{Z}
-algebras
R
. Now, let
M
be as in 2: an
R
-linear coefficient system on
X
whose fiber at
\ast\to X
lies in
\operatorname{Perf}(R)
. Then we can calculate the
R^{op}
-module of
R
-linear maps

M(1)[2]\to\Gamma^{!}(R)

in two ways:

1.

By the adjunction between
\Gamma^{!}
and
\Gamma
, they are the same as
R
-linear maps

R\Gamma(X;M(1)[2])\to R,

so we get

R\Gamma(X;M(1)[2])^{\vee}.

2.

By the above calculation
\Gamma^{!}(R)\simeq R(1)[2]
, we get

R\Gamma(X;M^{\vee}).

This gives an isomorphism

R\Gamma(X;M^{\vee})\simeq R\Gamma(X;M(1)[2])^{\vee},

which by a simple unwinding is induced by the pairing described in 2.
∎

Remark 8.34
.

Of course, in the archimedean cases we have a better explanation for this duality: it follows from Poincare duality for the manifolds
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
S^{2}
. (One also easily sees, with either proof, that the duality holds with
\mathbb{Z}
-coefficients, not just
p
-torsion coefficients.) For a similar geometric proof in the
p
-adic case, see
[
20
]
, where the geometric model is the Fargues-Fontaine curve over a choice of
\mathbb{C}_{p}
.

Now we go the number field case with restricted ramification, which is different in that the cohomology is not self-dual (up to twist), but rather is dual to a compactly-supported variant.

Theorem 8.35
.

Let
p
be a prime,
K
be a number field, and
S
a finite set of places of
K
containing all
p
-adic and archimedean places. Fix Weil-Moore data as in Example
7.12
. For
M
a
p
-primary torsion discrete coefficient system on
X:=X_{K,S}
, set

R\Gamma_{c}(X;M):=\operatorname{Fib}\left(R\Gamma(X;M)\to\oplus_{\nu\in S}R\Gamma(X_{K_{\nu}};M)\right).

Then:

1.

There is a unique map

[X]:R\Gamma_{c}(X;(\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})(1)[3])\to\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}

such that for all (or just one)
\nu\in S
, the composition

R\Gamma(X_{K_{\nu}};(\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})(1)[2])\overset{\partial_{\nu}}{\longrightarrow}R\Gamma_{c}(X;(\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})(1)[3])\overset{[X]}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}

identifies with the local fundamental class
[X_{K_{\nu}}]
from Theorem
8.33
, where
\partial_{\nu}
is the
\nu
-component of the boundary map arising from the definition of
R\Gamma_{c}
.

2.

For all
p
-power torsion
E_{1}
-
\mathbb{Z}
-algebras
R
and all perfect
R
-modules
M
with
\Omega X
-action, the pairing induced by cup product

R\Gamma_{c}(X;M(1)[3])\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}R\Gamma(X;M^{\vee})\to R\Gamma_{c}(X;R(1)[3])\overset{[X]}{\longrightarrow}R

induces an isomorphism

R\Gamma(X;M^{\vee})\simeq R\Gamma_{c}(X;M(1)[3])^{\vee},

in
\operatorname{Perf}(R^{op})
, where
(-)^{\vee}
denotes the duality functor
\operatorname{Perf}(R)^{op}\to\operatorname{Perf}(R^{op})
.

Proof.

We proceed similarly to the proof of local Poitou-Tate duality. For a
p
-primary torsion discrete coefficient system
M
on
X
, we make the following definition of
R\Gamma_{c}(-;M)
as a sheaf on
\operatorname{fEt}_{X}
: for
Y\to X
finite etale, set

R\Gamma_{c}(Y;M):=\operatorname{Fib}\left(R\Gamma(Y;M)\to\oplus_{\nu\in S}R\Gamma(Y\times_{X}X_{K_{\nu}};M)\right).

From this we define a sheaf on
\operatorname{fEt}_{X}
by

\mathcal{F}_{c}:=\left(R\Gamma_{c}(-;(\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})(1)[3])^{\vee}\right)^{tr}.

If
F
denotes the cosheaf giving the condensed refined class formation for
(K,S)
, and ditto
F_{\nu}
for the local field
K_{\nu}
, then setting

F_{Kott}=\operatorname{fib}(\oplus_{\nu\in S}(f_{\nu})_{\ast}F_{\nu}\to F)

where
f_{\nu}
is the local-to-global pushforward map on finite etale sites, we deduce that

\mathcal{F}_{c}=(F_{Kott})^{tr}_{\widehat{p}}(-1)[-2].

By definition of the class groups,
(F_{Kott})^{tr}
has top nonzero homology group given by
H_{1}=\mathbb{G}_{m}
, as a sheaf on the finite etale site of
\mathcal{O}_{K,S}
(identified with
\operatorname{fEt}_{X}
). Thus its
p
-completion has top nonzero homology group given by
H_{2}=\mathbb{Z}_{p}(1)
. We deduce that
\mathcal{F}_{c}
has top nonzero homology group given by

H_{0}\mathcal{F}_{c}=\mathbb{Z}_{p}.

In particular
1\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
provides a canonical

[X]:R\Gamma_{c}(X;(\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})(1)[3])\to\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}.

To prove 1, it suffices to note that the map
\mathbb{G}_{m}=H_{1}F_{Kott}\to H_{1}((f_{\nu})_{\ast}F_{\nu})=(f_{\nu})_{\ast}\mathbb{G}_{m}
is by definition the natural map of restricting global units to local units.

Now we turn to 2. As in the proof of
8.33
, let us consider the functor

\Gamma_{c}^{!}:\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})[p^{\infty}]\to\operatorname{D}_{et}(X;\mathbb{Z})[p^{\infty}]

which is right adjoint to
R\Gamma_{c}
. Part 1 gives us a natural map

(\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})(1)[3]\to\Gamma_{c}^{!}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}),

and we claim it is an isomorphism.

Again as in
8.33
it suffices to show

\mathbb{F}_{p}\to\Gamma_{c}^{!}(\mathbb{F}_{p})(-1)[-3]

is an isomorphism, and for this it suffices to show the induced map of sheaves on
\operatorname{fEt}_{X}
is an isomorphism. By Lemma
8.37
this map of sheaves on
\operatorname{fEt}_{X}
takes the form

R\Gamma(-;\mathbb{F}_{p})\to\left(R\Gamma_{c}(-;\mathbb{F}_{p}(1)[3])^{\vee}\right)^{tr}.

We also have the following additional information from the local-global compatibility property in 1: for all places
\nu
of
K
, the above map sits in a commutative square

\textstyle{R\Gamma(-;\mathbb{F}_{p})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left(R\Gamma_{c}(-;\mathbb{F}_{p}(1)[3])^{\vee}\right)^{tr}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R\Gamma((-)\times_{X}X_{K_{\nu}};\mathbb{F}_{p})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left(R\Gamma((-)\times_{X}X_{K_{\nu}};\mathbb{F}_{p}(1)[2])^{\vee}\right)^{tr}}

where the left arrow is the restriction map, the right arrow is dual to
\partial_{v}
, and the bottom map is the comparison map from local Poitou-Tate duality. Translating over to the
F_{Kott}
and
F_{\nu}
as above, we can rewrite this as

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 45.58926pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-22.47961pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{R\Gamma(-;\mathbb{F}_{p})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 82.0363pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.43448pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 82.0363pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{F_{Kott}^{tr}/p(-1)[-2]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 121.14427pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-45.58926pt\raise-32.43448pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{R\Gamma((-)\times_{X}X_{K_{\nu}};\mathbb{F}_{p})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 69.58926pt\raise-32.43448pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 69.58926pt\raise-32.43448pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(f_{\nu})_{\ast}(F_{\nu})^{tr}/p(-1)[-2]}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

We will also also bring the comparison map of presheaves

(f_{\nu})_{\ast}\mathbb{F}_{p}\to R\Gamma((-)\times_{X}X_{K_{\nu}};\mathbb{F}_{p}),

induced by
\mathbb{F}_{p}\to R\Gamma(-;\mathbb{F}_{p})
, into play. Let us restrict only to archimedean
\nu
, and take the direct sum over all
\nu|\infty
in the bottom row of this commutative square. By Theorem
8.19
, the natural map from
\mathbb{F}_{p}
to the fiber product

\oplus_{\nu|\infty}(f_{\nu})_{\ast}\mathbb{F}_{p}\times_{\oplus_{\nu|\infty}R\Gamma((-)\times_{X}X_{K_{\nu}};\mathbb{F}_{p})}R\Gamma(-;\mathbb{F}_{p})

is an isomorphism on derived sheafification. On the other hand, the other fiber product

\oplus_{\nu|\infty}(f_{\nu})_{\ast}\mathbb{F}_{p}\oplus_{\oplus_{\nu|\infty}(f_{\nu})_{\ast}(F_{\nu})^{tr}/p(-1)[-2]}F_{Kott}^{tr}/p(-1)[-2]

also admits a natural map from
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, namely corresponding to the canonical class we found above in
F_{Kott}^{tr}/p(-1)[-2]
on the one hand, and to the obvious diagonal map
\mathbb{F}_{p}\to\oplus_{\nu|\infty}(f_{\nu})_{\ast}\mathbb{F}_{p}
on the other hand, where these agree in
(f_{\nu})_{\ast}(F_{\nu})^{tr}/p(-1)[-2]
because the global fundamental class restricts to the local one at all
\nu
. Then we claim that this map from
\mathbb{F}_{p}
to the above fiber product is also an isomorphism on derived sheafification.

Assuming this claim for now, we know from local Poitou-Tate duality that our archimedean comparison map is an isomorphism. Thus our global comparison map is an isomorphism if and only if this induced map on fiber product sheaves

\mathbb{F}_{p}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}

is an isomorphism. However by construction the source and target compatibly map to
\oplus_{\nu|\infty}(f_{\nu})_{\ast}\mathbb{F}_{p}
by diagonal embedding. Thus the map is the identity, finishing the proof.

Thus we are left with showing that

\mathbb{F}_{p}\to\oplus_{\nu|\infty}(f_{\nu})_{\ast}\mathbb{F}_{p}\times_{\oplus_{\nu|\infty}(f_{\nu})_{\ast}(F_{\nu})^{tr}/p(-1)[-2]}F_{Kott}^{tr}/p(-1)[-2]

is an isomorphism on stalks, or equivalently, that the following null-composite sequence is a fiber sequence on sheafification:

\mathbb{F}_{p}(1)[2]\to F^{tr}_{Kott}/p\to\oplus_{\nu|\infty}(f_{\nu})_{\ast}\mathbb{F}_{p}^{tr},

where the first map corresponds to our fundamental class, coming as above from the identification
H_{1}F^{tr}_{Kott}=\mathbb{G}_{m}
, and the second map comes from the tautological

F_{Kott}\to\oplus_{\nu}(f_{\nu})_{\ast}F_{\nu}\to\oplus_{\nu}(f_{\nu})_{\ast}H_{0}F_{\nu}=\oplus_{\nu}(f_{\nu})_{\ast}\mathbb{Z}

by applying
(-)^{tr}/p
(which is exact and commutes with pushforwards).

Multiplication by
p
on
H_{1}F^{tr}_{Kott}=\mathbb{G}_{m}
is surjective on the sheaf level because
p
-Kummer extensions are unramified outside
p
, and obviously the kernel of multiplication by
p
is
\mathbb{F}_{p}(1)
. Thus it suffices to show that

H_{0}F^{tr}_{Kott}\to\oplus_{\nu|\infty}(f_{\nu})_{\ast}\mathbb{Z}^{tr}

is an isomorphism on stalks modulo uniquely
p
-divisible groups.

From the definitions we have an exact sequence of presheaves on the finite etale site of
\mathcal{O}_{K,S}

0\to\operatorname{Pic}(-)\to H_{0}F^{tr}_{Kott}\to\oplus_{\nu\in S}(f_{\nu})_{\ast}\mathbb{Z}^{tr}\to\mathbb{Z}^{tr}\to 0.

Now,
\operatorname{Pic}
gives
0
on stalks by the principal ideal theorem, and
\mathbb{Z}^{tr}
is uniquely
p
-divisible on stalks because there are always more and more extensions unramified outside
p
of degree divisible by
p
, say using the
p
-cyclotomic tower. Thus it suffices to show that for nonarchimedean
\nu\in S
, the stalks of
(f_{\nu})_{\ast}\mathbb{Z}^{tr}
are uniquely
p
-divisible. For that, up to replacing
K
by an aribrary finite extension (unramified outside
S
), we need to show that for any
K,S,\nu
, there is an extension
L
of
K
unramified outside
S
whose local degree at
\nu
is divisible by
p
. This can also be arranged using the
p
-cyclotomic tower.

Thus we have proven that

(\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})(1)[3]\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Gamma_{c}^{!}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}).

Next we claim that

M\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Gamma_{c}^{!}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}[-1])\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Gamma_{c}^{!}(M)

for all
M\in\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})[p^{\infty}]
. Exactly as in the proof of
8.33
, this reduces to bounded above
M
, then to the
p
-finiteness property of
8.5
. Again as in
8.33
we deduce that the adjunction between
R\Gamma_{c}
and
\Gamma_{c}^{!}
passes to
R
-module coefficients, and then 2 follows by calculating
R
-linear maps

M(1)[3]\to\Gamma_{c}^{!}(R)\simeq R(1)[3]

in two different ways.
∎

Remark 8.36
.

We chose the above formulation of Poitou-Tate duality, in the setting of a finite set
S
of places, to highlight the analogy with 3-manifolds. But the duality becomes picturesque in a slightly different manner if we pass to the situation where
S
consists of all places, so we are interested in the Weil-Moore anima
X_{K}
of the number field itself. In this situation, given
R
and
M
as above, the analog of the “boundary cohomology”
\oplus_{\nu\in S}R\Gamma(X_{K_{\nu}};M)
is given by the adelic construction

R\Gamma(X_{\mathbb{A}_{K}};M):=\varinjlim_{S_{0}}\prod_{\nu\in S_{0}}R\Gamma(X_{K_{\nu}};M)\times\prod_{\nu\not\in S_{0}}R\Gamma(X_{\mathcal{O}_{\nu}};M),

where
S_{0}
runs over finite subsets of places (of the form considered in Example
7.12
, say). The object on the right can be canonically viewed as a filtered system of Pro-objects in
\operatorname{Perf}(R)
, such that each transition map in the filtered colimit has cofiber lying in
\operatorname{Perf}(R)\subset\operatorname{Pro}(\operatorname{Perf}(R))
; as such it has the canonical structure of a
Tate
R
-module
, or rather a Tate object in
\operatorname{Perf}(R)
in the sense of
[
28
]
. We recall that:

1.

\operatorname{Tate}(R)
contains both
\operatorname{Ind}(\operatorname{Perf}(R))
and
\operatorname{Pro}(\operatorname{Perf}(R))
as full subcategories, and is generated by these;

2.

Maps
X\to Y
where
X
and
Y
each live in one of these full subcategories (but not necessarily the same one) are the “obvious” formal ones;

3.

The intersection of these two subcategories identifies with
\operatorname{Perf}(R)
(sitting inside each in the obvious natural way);

4.

The
R
-linear duality
\operatorname{Perf}(R)^{op}\simeq\operatorname{Perf}(R^{op})
naturally extends to
\operatorname{Tate}(R)^{op}\simeq\operatorname{Tate}(R^{op})
and interchanges
\operatorname{Ind}(\operatorname{Perf}(-))
and
\operatorname{Pro}(\operatorname{Perf}(-))
.

On the other hand,
R\Gamma(X_{K};M)
can be naturally viewed as lying in
\operatorname{Ind}(\operatorname{Perf}(R))
hence also in
\operatorname{Tate}(R)
, by writing
X_{K}=\varprojlim_{S_{0}}X_{K,S_{0}}
which gives a colimit on
R\Gamma
. By the same token there is a natural restriction map

R\Gamma(X_{K};M)\to R\Gamma(X_{\mathbb{A}_{K}};M)

in
\operatorname{Tate}(R)
.

Then the statement of Poitou-Tate duality, at this level, is that the fiber
R\Gamma_{c}(X_{K};M)
of above map lies in
\operatorname{Pro}(\operatorname{Perf}(R))\subset\operatorname{Tate}_{R}
, and the resulting cofiber sequence

R\Gamma(X_{K};M)\to R\Gamma(X_{\mathbb{A}_{K}};M)\to R\Gamma_{c}(X_{K};M)[1]

has the following self-duality: the
R
-linear dual of this cofiber sequence for
M
identifies with the same cofiber sequence but for
M^{\vee}(1)[2]
. On the middle term this follows from local Tate duality, and for the outer terms it follows from global Tate duality (in the form proved above).

From this statement one can, following Nekovar (
[
44
]
) extract dualities in
\operatorname{Perf}(R)
by a formal procedure. Suppose given a “Selmer structure”
\mathcal{S}
for
M
, meaning a cofiber sequence

C\to R\Gamma(X_{\mathbb{A}_{K}};M)\to D

where
C\in\operatorname{Pro}(\operatorname{Perf}(R))
and
D\in\operatorname{Ind}(\operatorname{Perf}(R))
(so, opposite to the situation of
R\Gamma(X_{K};M)
mapping to
R\Gamma(X_{\mathbb{A}_{K}};M)
, where something discrete maps with compact quotient). Then applying
R
-linear duality and local Tate duality we get a “dual Selmer structure”
\mathcal{S}^{\ast}
on
M^{\vee}(1)[2]
given by

D^{\vee}\to R\Gamma(X_{\mathbb{A}_{K}};M^{\vee}(1)[2])\to C^{\vee}.

If we define the Selmer cohomology to be

R\Gamma_{\mathcal{S}}(X_{K};M):=C\times_{R\Gamma(X_{\mathbb{A}_{K}};M)}R\Gamma(X_{K};M),

then it formally follows that
R\Gamma_{\mathcal{S}}(X_{K};M)\in\operatorname{Perf}(R)
, and we have the following duality:

R\Gamma_{\mathcal{S}}(X_{K};M)^{\vee}=R\Gamma_{\mathcal{S}^{\ast}}(X_{K};M^{\vee}(1)[2]).

In the above proofs of Poitou-Tate duality we used a bit of abstract formalism that we now should justify. The situation is the following: let
\mathcal{C}
be a Galois category and
\mathcal{F}
a
\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})
-valued sheaf on
\mathcal{C}
. Consider the functor

M\mapsto R\Gamma(\mathcal{C};M\otimes\mathcal{F})

from
\operatorname{Sh}(\mathcal{C};\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z}))
to
\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})
, where
\otimes
means sheafified derived tensor product over
\mathbb{Z}
. Composing with the Yoneda embedding, we get a co-presheaf

U\mapsto R\Gamma(\mathcal{C};\mathbb{Z}[h_{U}]\otimes\mathcal{F})

on
\mathcal{C}
with values in
\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})
. On the other hand, as we described in Definition
5.6
, the formalism of transfer maps also naturally produces a copresheaf
\mathcal{F}^{tr}
from
\mathcal{F}
, taking the same values as
\mathcal{F}
. The claim is that these two copresheaves are naturally isomorphic.

Lemma 8.37
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a Galois category,
\mathcal{D}
a
\mathbb{Z}
-linear presentable
\infty
-category, and
\mathcal{F}\in\operatorname{Sh}(\mathcal{C};\mathcal{D})
. Then there is a natural isomorphism between the following two functors
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
:

1.

The functor
U\mapsto\Gamma(\mathcal{C};\mathbb{Z}[h_{U}]\otimes\mathcal{F})
;

2.

The functor
\mathcal{F}^{tr}
from Definition
5.6
.

Proof.

By definition of
(-)^{tr}
, it suffices to show that the first functor extends to a functor

\operatorname{Span}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{D}

whose restriction along
\mathcal{C}^{op}\to\operatorname{Span}(\mathcal{C})
identifies with
\mathcal{F}
.

Such an extension comes as follows: we apply the transfers construction to the cosheaf
U\mapsto\mathbb{Z}[h_{U}]
to get a functor
\operatorname{Span}(\mathcal{C})\to\operatorname{Sh}(\mathcal{C};\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z}))
, which one can then compose with
R\Gamma(\mathcal{C};-\otimes\mathcal{F})
. To identify the restiction along
\mathcal{C}^{op}\to\operatorname{Span}(\mathcal{C})
with
\mathcal{F}
, it suffices to show that that the presheaf given by
(U\mapsto\mathbb{Z}[h_{U}])^{tr}
identifes with the presheaf given by
U\mapsto\mathbb{Z}[h_{U}]^{\vee}
(meaning the
\mathbb{Z}
-linear dual). However both these objects live in degree
0
so this is elementary to check: one shows that the canonical self-duality of the finite free
\mathbb{Z}
-module on a finite set implements the desired identification.
∎

Remark 8.38
.

Although
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{CondAb})
is not presentable for technical reasons (there is no small set of generators), the conclusion still holds for
\mathcal{D}=\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{CondAb})
: for example one can reduce to the case
\mathcal{D}=\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})
by checking on
S
-valued points for all extremally disconnected
S
. (Recall that taking
S
-valued points preserves all limits and colimits.)

Remark 8.39
.

We restricted to the
\mathbb{Z}
-linear context for familiarity, but the same claim holds in the more general semi-additive context. One can still check by hand because the base-case involves free
E_{\infty}
-spaces on finite sets, which are still just 1-truncated.

Remark 8.40
.

Suppose, in the situation of Lemma
8.37
, that
\mathcal{F}^{tr}
is actually a cosheaf. For example, this happens if
\mathcal{D}=\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})
and
\mathcal{F}
takes values in some bounded range of degrees, by Lemma
5.9
. It follows from left Kan extension that
\mathcal{F}^{tr}
extends uniquely along Yoneda to a colimit-preserving
\mathbb{Z}
-linear functor

\operatorname{Sh}(\mathcal{C};\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z}))\to\mathcal{D},

which we will continue to denote

\mathcal{G}\mapsto\mathcal{F}^{tr}(\mathcal{G}).

Left Kan extension from Lemma
8.37
then provides a natural transformation

\mathcal{F}^{tr}(-)\to R\Gamma(\mathcal{C};-\otimes\mathcal{F})

which is an isomorphism on the
\mathbb{Z}[h_{U}]
.

We claim that, at least in the situation where
\mathcal{D}=\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})
and
\mathcal{F}
takes values in a bounded range of degrees, the above natural transformation is actually an isomorphism on
all

\mathcal{G}\in\operatorname{Sh}(\mathcal{C};\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z}))
. As in the previous remark, we can deduce the analogous claim for
\mathcal{D}=\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{CondAb})
by evaluating on extremally disconnecteds.

To prove this, by the above it suffices to show that
R\Gamma(\mathcal{C};-\otimes\mathcal{F})
preserves colimits. By the criterion as in
[
12
]
Prop. 4.12, for that it suffices to show that for all
X\in\mathcal{C}
connected, all subgroups
G\subset\operatorname{Aut}(X)
, and all
M\in\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})
with
G
-action, the transfer map

(M\otimes\mathcal{F}(X))_{G}\to(M\otimes\mathcal{F}(X))^{G}

is an isomorphism. (For then it follows that one iteration of the Cech construction suffices to sheafify the presheaf tensor product, and moreover it also follows that the Cech construction preserves colimits sectionwise, and combing we get the desired claim.) By Lemma
5.5
we have that
\mathcal{F}(X)
has vanishing
H
-Tate cohomology for any subgroup
H\subset G
; let us show more generally that the desired conclusion holds with
\mathcal{F}(X)
replaced by any
N\in\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z}[G])
living in bounded degrees satisfying this vanishing hypothesis in Tate cohomology.

By the usual transfer tricks we reduce to the case of
G
a
p
-group for some prime
p
. Resolving
N
by injective
\mathbb{Z}[G]
-modules, we can reduce to the case where
N
lives in degree
0
. Then by the Nakayama-Rim theorem (
[
60
]
Chapter IX),
N
has a two-term projective resolution, so we reduce to the case when
N
is a free
\mathbb{Z}[G]
-module, which is trivial.

To finish, let us use this to give a somewhat different perspective on Poitou-Tate duality, by explaining how Poitou’s general duality theorem for class formations (
[
46
]
) looks from the perspective of Weil-Moore anima.

Theorem 8.41
.

Let
X
be a Weil-Moore anima with underlying class formation either of
\mathbb{Z}
-type,
\mathbb{R}
-type, or compact type. Let
F
denote the cosheaf on
\mathcal{C}=\operatorname{fEt}_{X}
giving the condensed refined class formation, and let
F^{tr}
denote the sheaf obtained by passing to transfer maps.

Then for discrete abelian group
M
with
\widehat{\pi_{1}X}
-action, corresponding on the one hand to a coefficient system on
X
and on the other hand to a sheaf of abelian groups on
\mathcal{C}
, there is a natural map

R\Gamma(\mathcal{C};M\otimes F^{tr})\to R\Gamma(X;M^{\vee})^{\vee}.

Here
(-)^{\vee}
means Pontryagin duality, and on the left we have derived sheafified tensor product over
\mathbb{Z}
.

Moreover, this map is an isomorphism whenever
M
is finitely generated (as an abelian group).

Proof.

We have a tautological pullback functor
\pi^{-1}
from
\operatorname{fEt_{X}}
to condensed anima over
X
, which therefore induces a pullback on
\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})
-valued sheaves. We follow this with Pontryagin duality, then global sections, then internal Hom to
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
again, getting the
\mathbb{Z}
-linear functor

\mathcal{F}\mapsto R\Gamma(X;(\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{F})^{\vee})^{\vee}

from
\operatorname{Sh}(\mathcal{C};\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z}))
to
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{CondAb})
. When we specialize to
\mathcal{F}=\mathbb{Z}[h_{U}]
for
U\in\operatorname{fEt}_{X}=\mathcal{C}
, we get the value

R\Gamma(U;(\mathbb{Z})^{\vee})^{\vee}=R\Gamma(U;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})^{\vee}=F(U)

Thus, the co-presheaf underlying this functor is
F
, which happens to be a cosheaf. We deduce by left Kan extension a natural transformation

F(-)\to R\Gamma(X;(\pi^{\ast}-)^{\vee})^{\vee}

of
\mathbb{Z}
-linear functors
\operatorname{Sh}(\mathcal{C};\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z}))\to\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{CondAb})
which is an isomorphism on all
\mathbb{Z}[h_{U}]
. Applying Remark
8.40
, we can rewrite this as a natural transformation

R\Gamma(\mathcal{C};(-)\otimes F^{tr})\to R\Gamma(X;(\pi^{\ast}-)^{\vee})^{\vee}

which is an isomorphism on all
\mathbb{Z}[h_{U}]
. An arbitrary finitely generated
M
admits a co-free resolution where each term is finite direct sum of copies of
\mathbb{Z}[h_{U}]
or
\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}[h_{U}]
(for
N\in\mathbb{Z}
), so we deduce the natural transformation is an isomorphism on all finitely generated
M
as well.
∎

For example, when we take
X=X_{K,S}
for a number field
K
and a finite set of places
S
containing all archimedean places and all places above some fixed prime
p
, then from this it is simple to deduce the following version of Poitou-Tate duality

R\Gamma(X_{K,S};M^{\vee})\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}R\Gamma_{c}(BG_{K,S};M(1)[3])^{\vee}

relating Weil-Moore cohomology to Kato-style compactly supported Galois cohomology, for
p
-primary torsion coefficient systems on
BG_{K,S}
. This is of course also a consequence of Theorem
8.35
and Lemma
8.19
.
